Exact Bounds of Spearman’s footrule in the Presence of Missing Data with Applications to Independence Testing
Yijin Zeng, Niall M. Adams, Dean A. Bodenham

Abstract
This work studies exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule between two partially observed n n -dimensional distinct real-valued vectors X X and Y Y . The lower bound is obtained by sequentially constructing imputations of the partially observed vectors, each with a non-increasing value of Spearman’s footrule. The upper bound is found by first considering the set of all possible values of Spearman’s footrule for imputations of X X and Y Y , and then the size of this set is gradually reduced using several constraints. Algorithms with computational complexities 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 2 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{2}) and 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 3 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{3}) are provided for computing the lower and upper bound of Spearman’s footrule for X X and Y Y , respectively. As an application of the bounds, we propose a novel two-sample independence testing method for data with missing values. Improving on all existing approaches, our method controls the Type I error under arbitrary missingness. Simulation results demonstrate our method has good power, typically when the proportion of pairs containing missing data is below 15%.



1
Introduction

We study exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule given two partially observed
vectors
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with distinct values.
Spearman’s footrule is a measure of statistical association based on ranks,
introduced by Spearman in
[
42
]
as a robust alternative to Spearman’s rank correlation.
This statistic is defined between two real-valued vectors
X
and
Y

and calculates the sum of absolute differences between the ranks of
corresponding components in
X
and
Y
.
In this work we assume that the vectors contain distinct values and that
there are no ties.

Among rank-based measures of association, Spearman’s rank correlation
and Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient
[
21
]
are more
well-known.
However, Spearman’s footrule has experienced a resurgence of interest,
perhaps due to
[
13
]
, where the
asymptotic normality of Spearman’s footrule is established under the assumption
of independence between
X
and
Y
. Because of its simplicity
and robustness to outliers, Spearman’s footrule has found
applications in various fields, including information
retrieval
[
16
,
23
]
,
web search
[
3
,
4
]
,
rank aggregation
[
15
,
24
]
,
genomics
[
22
]
,
welfare studies
[
32
]

and management science
[
31
,
7
]
.

In practice, it frequently occurs that a subset of the data are missing,
posing a challenge to the direct computation of Spearman’s footrule.
One often attempts to define the Spearman’s footrule using its lower
or upper bound, as
[
16
]

sought to formalize. A common setting with missing data is called top-
k

lists
[
16
,
24
,
15
,
4
]
,
also known as right-censored rankings
[
33
]
,
censored rankings
[
36
]

and progressive censoring
[
40
]
.
This setting assumes that all missing data are implicitly larger than
any observed data, resulting in only the data with smaller ranks being
observed. Under this setting, and additionally assume
that for any pair of
X
and
Y
, at least one
component in
X
or
Y
is observed,
Spearman’s footrule is demonstrated to
remain constant, regardless of the values of missing data
[
16
]
.

In this paper, we study the exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule without the
previously described assumption. To the best of our knowledge, the
issue of defining Spearman’s footrule under conditions of general missingness
has received limited attention, with the exception of the work
in
[
2
]
, where the authors define Spearman’s footrule with
missing data using the conditional expectation given all observed values.

While one might either ignore all pairs with at least one missing
observation or impute any missing data using the observed values, we
show that these practices will result in a biased estimate of
Spearman’s footrule except in special cases such as when the missing
data are missing completely at random. It appears intractable to give
an unbiased estimate of Spearman’s footrule based on observed values only
under general cases of missingness. Our contribution fills this
gap by establishing exact bounds for Spearman’s footrule without relying
on restrictive assumptions about the nature of the missing data.

A related issue to measuring
the statistical association of
X
and
Y
is testing for independence
between the two quantities.
In the presence of missing data,
there is no default approach to testing for independence. When the
missingness mechanisms are known, for example if the data are missing
completely at random, the practice of ignoring the missing data may be
justified and more sophisticated approaches may be viable,
e.g.
[
30
]
. However, in most real-world data analysis
situations, such information is unavailable,
and such practices may not necessarily be valid.
Perhaps the most common practice is to simply ignore all the pairs with
missing data and conduct the independence testing only for completely
observed pairs, as noted in
[
34
,
1
]
.
The “risk” of ignoring pairs with missing values is considered by some
authors to be inconsequential when the proportion of missing data is
less than
5
%
[
37
,
19
]
or

10\%

[
6
,
6
]
, but others argue against
such heuristics
[
28
]
.
We show that even in certain cases where
5\%
to
10\%
of the data is
missing, ignoring or imputing these missing values is perilous.

As an application, we explore the use of our new bounds for Spearman’s footrule
for independence testing in the presence of missing data.
Spearman’s footrule
is a natural statistic for testing independence

[
32
,
17
,
27
,
10
]
,
and has greater statistical power than Spearman’s rank correlation and
Kendall’s
\tau
under certain alternatives

[
17
,
27
,
10
]
.

The key idea of our testing method is to provide tight bounds of all possible

p
-values given the observed values. For a pre-specified significance
level
\alpha
, the null hypothesis is rejected only when the upper bound of the

p
-values, and hence all possible
p
-values,
is less than or equal to
\alpha
.
When at least one
p
-value is not signficant, then the null hypothesis fails
to be rejected.

One motivation for this approach is that it allows us to have confidence in any
significant result, because the significant result was obtained without making
any assumption about the missing data. In other words, if a significant result
is obtained, then even if the missing data could have been observed or an imputation method
had been employed, the result would still have been significant.
While this is a conservative method of testing, the derived bounds for Spearman’s footrule
are tight, and so the results cannot be improved while taking into account all possible values
for the missing data.
The idea of employing bounds to consider all possible outcomes was previously used
in
[
20
]
to take into account the effect
of missing covariates and outcomes in clinical trials and observational studies.

This approach of taking all possible
p
-values into accounts could also be interpreted
as a sensitivity analysis
[
44
,
18
,
11
,
41
]
that considers all possible assumptions on the missing data.
As we mentioned before, if a significant result is obtained by our method, then this
result is consistent among all possible missing data assumptions, since every possible imputation
would lead to a significant result. If, however, our method
fails to reject the null hypothesis, there are two possible outcomes: either all possible missing
data assumptions lead to insignificant results when the lower bound of the
p
-values
is greater than
\alpha
, or a subset of assumptions leads to
significant results when the lower bound of the
p
-value is less than or equal to
\alpha
,
but the upper bound is larger than
\alpha
.
Hence our method answers whether different approaches to handling missing
data lead to different conclusions
[
44
]
.

1.1
Contributions

We provide exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data
for two univariate vectors of distinct values.
Let
X
and
Y
be
n
-dimensional distinct real-valued vectors,
which may only be partially observed.
If the missing values of
X
and
Y
are replaced with real values,
we obtain
imputations

X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
which will be more formally
defined in Section
2.1
.
Theorem
2.10
provides conditions
under which
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
achieve
the lower bound of
Spearman’s footrule,
while Theorem
2.21
deals with
the upper bound.

While it is possible to find
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
that give the lower and upper
bounds simply by enumerating all possible ranks of missing data, this strategy
is computationally infeasible for even moderate sample sizes.
Suppose
X
and
Y
are of length
n
and have

m_{X}
and
m_{Y}
missing values, respectively.
There are
(n!)^{2}/((n-m_{X})!(n-m_{Y})!)
possible permutations of
the ranks. Consequently, when
n=100,m_{X}=m_{Y}=10
, the
number of possible permutations of ranks is approximately
3.95\times 10^{39}
.
The second contribution is to provide efficient algorithms for
computing our exact lower and upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule.

To find
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
for the lower bound,
we propose Algorithm
2
, which requires computation complexity
of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
However, finding the upper bound is more challenging.
An algorithm with computational complexity
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
is provided in
the Supplementary Material for finding the upper bound.

Our work offers insight to anyone using
Spearman’s footrule with missing data.
Furthermore,
given the close link between Spearman’s footrule and Kendall’s
\tau
, our
results lead to non-trivial bounds for Kendall’s
\tau
with missing data.

We explore the use of the bounds for Spearman’s footrule for independence
testing in the presence of missing data. The core idea is to give the bounds
of all possible
p
-values given the observed values. Unlike all other
approaches for testing independence with missing data, our test is capable
of controlling the Type I error regardless of the values of missing data and
the missingness mechanisms. Extensive numerical simulations are conducted and
confirm our method is particularly useful when data are missing not at random.

1.2
Missing data mechanisms

We briefly describe the three missing data scenarios outlined in
[
25
]
: missing completely at random (MCAR), missing at random (MAR) and missing not at random (MNAR) . Consider a vector of univariate real-value samples
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{N})
. Let
\iota=(\iota_{1},\cdots,\iota_{N})
be an indicator function of
z
such that
\iota_{i}
taking value 1 if
z_{i}
is missing and 0 if
z_{i}
is observed. The core idea of
[
25
]
is to admit
\iota
as a probabilistic phenomenon. Let
z
be a realized value of a random variable
Z
. Let
f_{\theta}(\iota|z)
denotes the probability of
I=\iota
given
Z=z
, where
\theta
denotes any unknown parameters of the distribution. Then, the missingness mechanism is MCAR if
f_{\theta}({\iota}|z)=f_{\theta}(\iota|\widetilde{z}),\forall\iota,z,\widetilde{z}.
In such cases, the missingness mechanism
I
is independent of the value of samples. Denote
z^{\prime}
as a sub-vector of
z
including all observed samples in
z
, i.e. including all
z_{i}
such that
\iota_{i}=0
. Then, the missingness mechanism is MAR if
f_{\theta}({\iota}|z)=f_{\theta}(\iota|\widetilde{z}),\forall\iota,z,\widetilde{z}\text{ such that }z^{\prime}=\widetilde{z}^{\prime}.
In such cases, the missingness mechanism
I
is independent of the values of missing samples. If the missingness mechanisms is neither MCAR nor MAR, it is MNAR.

1.3
Related Work

The contributions of this paper are providing exact
bounds of Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data and proposing
a new independence testing method with missing data based on these bounds. A
special missing data scenario often discussed in relation to Spearman’s
footrule is the so-called ”top-
k
lists” situation, as explored in

[
16
,
24
,
15
,
33
,
36
,
40
]
, where all missing data are assumed
larger than any observed data and only smaller ranks can be observed.
Our study of Spearman’s footrule with missing data makes no assumption about
the missing data except that they are distinct values.

To the best of our knowledge, only
[
2
]
considers the same general missing pattern as we do, where Spearman’s footrule is defined as the conditional mean given all observed data. Another closely-related work
[
29
]
assumes all data in
X
,
Y
are distinct and establishes the exact lower bound of Spearman’s footrule under the missing case where all pairs must either both be observed or missing, denoted
Missing Case III
below. The bounds found in this special case are then extended to the case where observations in
X
and
Y
are potentially tied
[
26
,
43
]
. Additional missing data scenarios are studied in
[
8
]
, where the missing data are categorized into partial, interval, and bucket cases, with potential for ties or ranking within a defined range. Their findings indicate that computing the bounds of Spearman’s footrule in “partial missing” cases is NP-hard, leaving the computational feasibility of other scenarios open. In this work, we assume all data are distinct, and values are not tied.

Another line of research closely related to our work is independence testing
in the presence of missing data. Spearman’s rank correlation and Kendall’s

\tau
coefficient in the presence of missing data are defined using
conditional expectation given all observed values in
[
1
]
.
Under the assumption that the incomplete rank vectors are uniformly
distributed over all possible permutations, the distributions of these two
statistics are then derived and used for hypothesis testing. The test results
are analyzed empirically in
[
9
]
. Using the same approach
as in
[
1
]
, Spearman’s footrule has been applied for
trend testing in the presence of missing data
[
2
]
.
The independence testing problem in the case where data are missing at
random has been considered in
[
34
,
30
]
.

Beyond the above test statistics adapted for independence testing with missing
data, broader methodologies for handling missing data are available. These
include case deletion (i.e. only using the fully observed data)

[
39
]
, single imputation
[
38
]
, multiple
imputation
[
37
]
, and the expectation
maximization algorithm
[
12
]
. All methods rely on assumptions
about the nature of the missing data such as missing completely at random
or missing at random. When data are missing not at random,
knowledge of the specific
missingness mechanisms are required
[
38
]
.
For comprehensive overviews of these strategies,

[
25
,
38
,
14
,
5
]
are recommended. Our unique contribution
makes no assumption about the missing data except assuming that all values
with a vector are distinct.
Our testing method offers a quantifiable measure of impact of
missing data on testing outcomes, through identifying bounds on
p
-values.

2
Exact Bounds of Spearman’s Footrule In the Presence of Missing Data

2.1
Notation and definitions

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[n]
denote the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For any
n
-dimensional vector
X
, let
X(i)
denote the
component
of
X
at
index

i\in[n]
. Thus, an
n
-dimensional vector
X
can be written as
X=(X(1),\ldots,X(n))
.

We will only consider vectors with distinct real values, i.e. there are no ties.
Define
\mathcal{V}^{n}
to be the set of
n
-dimensional vectors
with distinct, real values, i.e. if
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
then
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and all components of
X
are distinct.

If
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, given a set of indices
U\subset[n]
,
we shall consider
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
to be an
imputation
of
X
when
X_{*}(i)=X(i)

for all
i\in[n]\setminus U
, and the
imputed values
are
X_{*}(u)
for all
u\in U
.

For any subset
S\subset[n]
of indices, we use the notation
(X(l))_{l\in S}
to denote a vector including the components of
X
corresponding to the indices in set
S
, with the order of components in
(X(l))_{l\in S}
following the order of components in
X
. For example, if
n=5
and
S=\{1,3,4\}
, we then have
(X(l))_{l\in S}=(X(1),X(3),X(4))
.

We define the rank of a component using the indicator function. For a statement
A
, we denote
I\left(A\right)
as a indicator function such that if the statement
A
is correct,
I\left(A\right)=1
, otherwise
I\left(A\right)=0
. Subsequently, for any
n
-dimensional vector
X
of distinct real numbers, the rank of any component
X(i)
in
X
is defined as

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=\sum_{l=1}^{n}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right).

Using this notation, Spearman’s footrule for any two
n
-dimensional real-valued, distinct vectors
X,Y
is defined as

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)|.

If a vector contains missing values, we will refer to it as
partially observed
,
while a vector with no missing values is
fully observed
.
Our objective is to establish the exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule with partially observed vectors
X
and
Y
, where the components of
X
and
Y
can be missing in any configuration. We start by considering three specific cases which, when considered together, cover any possible case of a pair of partially observed vectors.

•

Missing Case I
. All missing values are either all in
X
or all in
Y
. In other words, either
X
or
Y
is fully observed, while the other vector is partially observed.

•

Missing Case II
. For any pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
i\in[n]
, at most one value is missing. Therefore, both
X
and
Y
may contain missing data, and if
X(i)
is missing, the paired
Y(i)
must be observed. On the other hand, if
Y(i)
is missing, the paired
X(i)
must be observed.

•

Missing Case III
. For any pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
i\in[n]
, the two values are either both observed, or both missing. Therefore, both
X
and
Y
may contain missing values, and if
X(i)
is missing, the paired
Y(i)
is also missing. Similarly, if
Y(i)
is missing, the paired
X(i)
is also missing.

Note that these three missing cases are not mutually exclusive. In particular, the
Missing Case I
can be considered as a special case of
Missing Case II
, given that the latter includes the case where only one vector of
X
and
Y
can have missing values.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, which may be only partially observed. We use the notation
U
to denote the set of all indices where
X(i)
is missing but
Y(i)
is observed. Furthermore, we use the notation
V
to denote the set of all indices where
X(i)
is observed but
Y(i)
is missing. Additionally, we use the notation
W
to denote the set of all indices where both
X(i)
and
Y(i)
are missing.

Finally,
R\left(X,X\right)
is used to denote the vector of ranks of all components in
X
, i.e.

\displaystyle R\left(X,X\right)=(R\left(X(1),X\right),\ldots,R\left(X(n),X\right)).

For example, if
X=(7,2,5)
, then
R\left(X,X\right)=(3,1,2)
.
Note that, in practice, we often first convert the vectors
X
and
Y
into the rank vectors

R\left(X,X\right)
and
R\left(Y,Y\right)
, respectively, and work with these rank vectors directly.

2.2
Lower Bound of Spearman’s Footrule

In what follows, we first provide the exact lower bounds of Spearman’s footrule. In Section
2.2.1
and
2.2.2
, the problem for establishing exact lower bounds under
Missing Case I
and
II
are discussed, respectively. Algorithms
1
and
2
provide computationally efficient methods for computing these bounds. The lower bound under
Missing Case III
is discussed in
[
29
]
, although it not shown there that the bound is tight. In Proposition
2.9
, we show this bound is tight. Finally, by synthesizing the results developed for
Missing Case I
,
II
and
III
, we provide the lower bound in the general case. The discussion of the upper bound in Section
2.3
follows the same structure. The proof of all results can be found in the Supplementary Material.

2.2.1
Missing Case I

We begin by carefully studying
Missing Case I
, where the missing values are assumed to be either all in
X
or all in
Y
. Without loss of generality, we proceed by assuming only
X
contains missing values.

The following proposition lays the groundwork for future results. It investigates the conditions for taking the minimum Spearman’s footrule when one component
X(u)
in
X
is replaced by a new value
X_{*}(u)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
.
If we choose

X_{*}(u)
such that
R\left(X_{*}(u_{1}),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)
,
then

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

Moreover, for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for index
u
,

D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

This proposition demonstrates that if one component of
X
is changed, Spearman’s footrule will be minimized
when the rank of that component equals to the rank of its paired component, i.e.
R\left(X_{*}(u_{1}),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)
.

When considering Proposition
2.1
, a natural concern would be whether
the required imputation
X_{*}
actually exists.
To this end, we verify the existence of such imputations
X_{*}
of
X
,
in the more general case where multiple components are imputed
so that the ranks of all imputed components match the ranks of their paired
components in
Y
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of indices. Then there exists
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X(i)\,\,\text{for all }~i\in[n]\setminus U,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,~R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\,\,\text{for all }~i\in U.

(1)

The proof in the Supplementary Material constructs
X_{*}
sequentially by considering the order of the values in the paired vector
(Y(u))_{u\in U}
. The first step is to identify
u_{1}
, the index in
U
with the minimum rank in
Y
, defined as
u_{1}=\text{argmin}\{R\left(Y(u),Y\right)|u\in U\}
.
Then the component
X(u_{1})
is imputed so that its rank satisfies

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{1}),(X(i))_{i\in\left\{[n]\setminus U\right\}\cup\{u_{1}\}}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right).

Following this imputation, the ranks of all observed values in
X
are updated.
U
is then updated to
U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
, and this process is repeated until
U
is empty.
Algorithm
1
below formally describes the procedure of the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
Note that in Algorithm
1
we work with the rank vectors

R\left(Y,Y\right)
and
R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)
,
rather than the actual vectors
Y
and
X_{*}
,
which suffices for our purpose of computing the value of Spearman’s footrule
D(X_{*},Y)
.

Algorithm 1

An Algorithm for Computing the Exact Lower Bound of Spearman’s Footrule Under
Missing Case I

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where
X
may be partially observed and
Y
is fully observed.

2:

Minimum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

Rank all observed components in
X
and
Y
.

4:

while

U\neq\emptyset

do

5:

Identify
u_{1}=\text{argmin}\left\{R\left(Y(i),Y\right)|i\in U\right\}
.

6:

Let
R\left(X(u_{1}),(X(i))_{i\in\left\{[n]\setminus U\right\}\cup\{u_{1}\}}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)
.

7:

for

i\in[n]\setminus U

do

8:

Denote
r_{i}=R\left(X(i),(X(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\right)

9:

Update

R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in\left\{[n]\setminus U\right\}\cup\{u_{1}\}}\right)\leftarrow r_{i}+I\left(r_{i}\geq R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)\right).

10:

end

for

11:

Update
U\leftarrow U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
.

12:

end

while

13:

Output
D\left(X,Y\right)
.

Remark 1
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
1
is analyzed as follows. Ranking all observed components in
X
and
Y

in line
3
requires
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps. Inside the while loop, identifying
u_{1}
in line
5

requires the computational complexity
\mathcal{O}({n})
, and updating ranks of observed components between line
7
and line
10
requires the computational complexity
\mathcal{O}({n})
. Denote
m_{1}=|U|
. The while loop run
m_{1}
times. Since each iteration is
\mathcal{O}(n)
, the computational complexity of loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1}{n})
.
Overall, the computational complexity for Algorithm
1
is

\mathcal{O}(n\log n+m_{1}{n})
.

To further emphasise that, when computing Spearman’s footrule,
it is the
ranks

R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)
of the imputed vector
X_{*}
that
are important, rather than the imputed values
(X_{*}(u))_{u\in U}
themselves,
we have the following lemma which will be useful in proving later results.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of indices. Consider any
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

satisfying the conditions in (
1
), Then
D\left(X_{1},Y\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.

We are now ready to state the main result for
Missing Case I
, which is an extension
of Propostion
2.1
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of indices. Consider any
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
satisfying
the conditions in (
1
). Then

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

Moreover, for any
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that
X^{\prime}(i)=X(i)

for
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

The strategy for proving Theorem
2.4
is to sequentially construct imputations
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for the set of indices
U
.
As before, define
u_{1}=\text{argmin}\{R\left(Y(u),Y\right)|u\in U\}
.

The first vector
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
such
that the ranks of
X_{1}
and
Y
are equal for the set
U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
,
i.e.
R\left((X_{1}(u))_{u\in U\setminus\{u_{1}\}},X_{1}\right)=R\left((Y(u))_{u\in U\setminus\{u_{1}\}},Y\right)
.
The second vector
X_{2}
imputes the
u_{1}
component, so that its rank is equal to that of its
paired component, i.e.

R\left(X_{2}(u_{1}),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)
.
However, it may now be the case that the ranks of the components of
X_{2}

for the indices in
U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
may not equal the ranks of their paired components in
Y
, i.e.
it may be that

R\left((X_{2}(u))_{u\in U\setminus\{u_{1}\}},X_{1}\right)\neq R\left((Y(u))_{u\in U\setminus\{u_{1}\}},Y\right)
.
Therefore, the third vector
X_{3}
is constructed as an imputation of
X_{2}
for the indices
U\setminus\{u_{1}\}
,
so that the ranks of its imputed components are equal to the ranks of its paired components in
Y
,
and

R\left((X_{3}(u))_{u\in U},X_{3}\right)=R\left((Y(u))_{u\in U},Y\right)
.

It is then demonstrated in the proof of Theorem
2.4
that Spearman’s footrule decreases sequentially as follows:

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)\geq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\geq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\geq D\left(X_{3},Y\right).

Since
X_{3}
satisfies the conditions in (
1
), if we use Algorithm
1

to construct
X_{*}
which also satisfies the conditions in (
1
),
by Lemma
2.3
we must have
D\left(X_{3},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Theorem
2.4
reveals an interesting property of Spearman’s footrule. In order to minimize Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data, or in other words, minimize Spearman’s footrule for the pair

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
by imputing values for
(X(u))_{u\in U}

for a subset of indices
U\subset[n]
, one simply needs to
construct an imputation
X_{*}
that satisfies the conditions in (
1
).

This property, however, does not hold for minimizing Spearman’s rank correlation
\rho

and Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient defined for
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\rho(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)|^{2},

(2)

\displaystyle\tau(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>j}\{I\left(X_{i}>X_{j}\right)I\left(Y_{i}<Y_{j}\right)+I\left(X_{i}<X_{j}\right)I\left(Y_{i}>Y_{j}\right)\},

(3)

respectively. Note that
\rho(X,Y)
and
\tau(X,Y)
in Equations (
2
)
and (
3
) are unscaled versions of the coefficients that measure the amount of
discordance. Equation (
6
) provides the more familiar versions of the coefficients
scaled to
[-1,1]
.

As an example, consider the data shown in left-hand part of Table
1
,
where objects
a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h

have been given two different sets of ranks, denoted by
X
and
Y
, but the rank of object
d
for
X
is missing.

A natural question to ask is what rank of
d
will maximize the
“agreement” between the two referees
X
and
Y
.
For any of the three rank correlation statistics,
maximizing the agreement is equivalent to minimizing the statistic,
since a perfect agreement would mean equal ranks and a zero-valued statistic.
Theorem
2.4
concludes that minimizing
Spearman’s footrule requires the rank of
d
to be
4
, to match the rank given by
Y
.
However, as shown in Table 3.1 in Supplementary Material,
Spearman’s rank correlation
\rho
and Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient are both minimized
when the rank of
d
is 1; the right-hand part of Table
1
provides partial results.

This special property of Spearman’s footrule is further explored and extended
in the context of
Missing Case II
, as discussed in Theorem
2.7
.

D
, Spearman’s rank correlation
\rho

and Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient, where the
*
indicates the rank of object
d
for vector
X
is missing.
Right: values of these statistics for selected imputations
X_{*}
of
X
, when the rank of
d
is
imputed as either
3
or

2.2.2
Missing Case II

This section focuses on the lower bound of Spearman’s footrule under
Missing Case II
, where
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
may contain unobserved samples, but for each pair
(X(i),Y(i))
, at least one value is observed.
We shall use
U\subset[n]
to denote the set of indices where
X(i)
is missing but
Y(i)
is observed, and
V\subset[n]
will denote the set of indices where
X(i)
is observed but
Y(i)
is missing. Given this notation, consider the following two conditions:

•

Condition A.
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for
indices
U,V\subset[n]
respectively, such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
and

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X(i)~\text{for all}~i\in[n]\setminus U,~\text{and}~Y_{*}(j)=Y(j)~\text{for all}~j\in[n]\setminus V.

•

Condition B.
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are such, for indices
U,V\subset[n]
,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),i\in U\cup V.

Condition A ensures that
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
satisfying
Missing Case II
.
Condition B ensures the ranks of the imputed components of
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}

are equal to the ranks of their paired components.

It is proved in Theorem
2.7
that the minimum possible value of
Spearman’s footrule between
X
and
Y
equals
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)

when
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
satisfy both Conditions A and B. While finding
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}

that satisfy Condition A is straightforward, it is less obvious that we can always find
imputations that satisfy both Conditions A and B.
Hence, we first prove the existence of such imputations.

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and disjoint
U,V\subset[n]
are such that
U\cup V\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exist imputations
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
satisfying both Conditions A and B.

The proof of Proposition
2.5
in the Supplementary Material follows a similar idea
to the proof of Lemma
2.2
. The construction of
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
is summarised
in Algorithm
2
below.

Algorithm 2

An algorithm for computing the exact lower bound of Spearman’s footrule under
Missing Case II

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where both vectors may be partially observed under
Missing Case II
.

2:

Minimum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

Rank all observed components in
X
,
Y
.

4:

while

U\neq\emptyset\text{ and }V\neq\emptyset

do

5:

Let
u_{1}=\text{argmin}\left\{R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)|i\in V\right\}
.

6:

Let
u_{2}=\text{argmin}\left\{R\left(Y(i),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus V}\right)|i\in U\right\}
.

7:

if

R\left(X(u_{1}),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(u_{2}),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus V}\right)

then

8:

Let
R\left(Y(u_{1}),(Y(j))_{j\in\left\{[n]\setminus V\right\}\cup\{u_{1}\}}\right)=R\left(X(u_{1}),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)

9:

for

i\in[n]\setminus V

do

10:

Denote
R\left(Y(i),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=s_{i}
.

11:

Update
R\left(Y(i),(Y(j))_{j\in\left\{[n]\setminus V\right\}\cup\{u_{1}\}}\right)\leftarrow s_{i}+I\left(s_{i}\geq R\left(X(u_{1}),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\right)
.

12:

end

for

13:

Update
V\leftarrow V\setminus\{u_{1}\}
.

14:

else

15:

Let
R\left(X(u_{2}),(X(j))_{j\in\left\{[n]\setminus U\right\}\cup\{u_{2}\}}\right)=R\left(Y(u_{2}),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus V}\right)

16:

for

i\in[n]\setminus U

do

17:

Denote
R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r_{i}
.

18:

Update
R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in\left\{[n]\setminus U\right\}\cup\{u_{2}\}}\right)\leftarrow r_{i}+I\left(r_{i}\geq R\left(Y(u_{2}),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus V}\right)\right).

19:

end

for

20:

Update
U\leftarrow U\setminus\{u_{2}\}
.

21:

end

if

22:

end

while

23:

if

U\neq\emptyset
or
V\neq\emptyset

then

24:

Run Algorithm
1
when
U\neq\emptyset
. Reverse
X
and
Y
and run Algorithm
1
when
V\neq\emptyset
.

25:

end

if

Remark 2
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
2
is analyzed as follows. Ranking all observed components in
X
and
Y
in line
5
takes
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps.
Inside the while loop, the computational complexity for finding the minimum ranks in line
5
and
6
is
\mathcal{O}(n)
, and for updating ranks of observed components from line
9
to line
12
, and line
16
to line
19
is
\mathcal{O}(n)
. Let
|U|=m_{1}
, and
|V|=m_{2}
. The loop can run up to
m_{1}+m_{2}-1
times.
Since each iteration is
\mathcal{O}(n)
,
the computational complexity for the while loop is

\mathcal{O}(n(m_{1}+m_{2}))
.
The computational complexity for Algorithm
1
, called in line
24
, after ranking all observed components is
\mathcal{O}(nm_{1})
when
U\neq\emptyset
and

\mathcal{O}(nm_{2})
when
V\neq\emptyset
, according to Remark
1
. Therefore, the total computation complexity for Algorithm
2
is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n+n(m_{1}+m_{2}))
.

The following result shows that
all imputations
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
satisfying both Conditions A and B yield the same value of Spearman’s footrule.

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and disjoint
U,V\subset[n]
are such that
U\cup V\neq\emptyset
. If
X_{1},Y_{1}
and
X_{2},Y_{2}

are pairs satisfing Conditions A and B, then
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.

While the equivalence of
Missing Case I
found in Lemma
2.3
can be proved
in a relatively straightforward approach, the proof of
Proposition
2.6
is much more challenging. For
Missing Case I
, the
condition in (
1
) explicitly provides the ranks of all
components in
U
for both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. On the other hand,
Condition B for
Missing Case II
only requires the ranks of all components of

X_{j},Y_{j}
for indices
U\cup V
to be
matched with their paired components, for
j\in\{1,2\}
.
However, the rank of component
X_{1}(i)
in
X_{1}

is not necessary equal to the rank of
X_{2}(i)
in
X_{2}
, for

i\in U\cup V
.
Similarly, it is not necessarily true
for all
i\in U\cup V
that

R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)
.

Furthermore, the following result shows that
minimum possible Spearman’s footrule is achieved when imputations

X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
satisfy both Conditions A and B.

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and disjoint
U,V\subset[n]
are such that
U\cup V\neq\emptyset
. If
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations satisfying both Conditions A and B, then

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

For any other imputations
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U,V
, respectively,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right).

2.2.3
Missing Case III

Given two vectors
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
,
recall that in
Missing Case III
, for any pair of components
(X(i),Y(i))

at index
i\in[n]
,
the two values are either both observed or both missing.
This case for Spearman’s footrule has been previously considered in the literature, and
the following result is proved in Theorem 1 in
[
29
]
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
29
]
)
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
.
For the subvectors,
X^{\prime}=(X(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W}
and

Y^{\prime}=(Y(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W}
, it can be shown that

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

While this result provides a lower bound of Spearman’s footrule, it does
not directly prove that this bound is the minimum possible value of the
Spearman’s footrule.
The following result fills this gap by identifying the
conditions under which this lower bound is tight.

Proposition 2.9
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
.
Define the subvectors
X^{\prime}=(X(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W}
and

Y^{\prime}=(Y(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W}
.
If the following three conditions all hold,

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle~R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\,\,\textrm{for all}~i\in W,

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle~\min_{j\in W}X(j)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus W}X(j),

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle~\min_{j\in W}Y(j)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus W}Y(j),

then
D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

2.2.4
General missing case

We are now ready to provide the lower bound of
Spearman’s footrule in the general case, where the missing pattern of data is a combination
of
Missing Cases I
,
II
, and
III
.
The previous results can be combined to give the following result.

Theorem 2.10
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U
,
V
,
W\subset[n]
are
pairwise disjoint subsets.
Suppose
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
, and
V\cup W
, respectively. If the following three conditions all hold

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle~R\left(X_{*}(i),X\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y\right),\,\,\textrm{for all}~i\in U\cup V\cup W,

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle~\min_{j\in W}X_{*}(j)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus W}X_{*}(j),

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle~\min_{j\in W}Y_{*}(j)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus W}Y_{*}(j),

then

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right),

and for any other imputations
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
,
V\cup W
, respectively,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right).

For the imputations
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
in Theorem
2.10
,
Proposition
2.9
gives

D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=D\left((X_{*}(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W},(Y_{*}(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\right)
,
which suggests that, for indices in
W
, the components of both

X
and
Y
can be ignored for the purposes of calculating the lower
bound. Hence, the minimum possible Spearman’s footrule in the general
missing case can be determined using Algorithm
2
after ignoring
all components with indices in
W
.

2.3
Upper Bound of Spearman’s Footrule

This section provides the exact upper bound of Spearman’s footrule in the
presence of missing data. We consider
Missing Cases I
,
II
,
and
III
separately
and then combine our results to obtain the upper bound in the general
missing case.

2.3.1
Missing Case I

We start by considering
Missing Case I
for
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
,
where the missing components are assumed
to be either all in
X
or all in
Y
. Without loss of generality,
let us assume only
X
contains missing components
while all values in
Y
are observed.

Suppose only a single component
X(u)
is missing. The
following proposition is complementary to Proposition
2.1

which considers the lower bound for
Missing Case I
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be imputations of
X

for an index
u\in[n]

such that

R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right)=1
and
R\left(X_{2}(u_{1}),X_{2}\right)=n
.
Then

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\},

and for any imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for index
u
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}
.

When a single component of
X
is missing, Proposition
2.11

shows that the maximum possible value of Spearman’s footrule is achieved when the
missing component is imputed to have rank either
1
or
n
.

It is shown in the proof that in the special case where

R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)=1
,

X_{2}
maximizes Spearman’s footrule, and in the
special case where
R\left(Y(u_{1}),Y\right)=n
,

X_{1}
maximizes Spearman’s footrule.

To extend Proposition
2.11
to
cases when multiple components of
X
may be missing, we first make the following definition.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
U\subset T\subset[n]
be a subset of indices. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U,T,n)=\{Z\in\mathcal{V}^{n}|~\text{for any}~i\in U,~Z(i)>\max_{j\in T\setminus U}Z(j)\text{ or }Z(i)<\min_{j\in T\setminus U}Z(j)\}.

It will often happen that
T=[n]
in
\mathcal{Z}(U,T,n)
,
but this will not always be the case. This definition allows us to state the following result.

Proposition 2.13
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U\subset[n]
.
Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
, and for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for indices
U
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

When the components of
X
with indices in
U\subset[n]
are missing, Proposition
2.13
allows us to obtain the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule by considering rank vectors of all possible imputations
X_{*}
of
X
in the set
\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.

We briefly consider the number of possible
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
,
up to equivalent rank vectors. An imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)

is such that all components in
U
are either larger or smaller than all components at indices
[n]\setminus U
. Let
m_{1}=|U|
and let
r
be the number of components smaller than all components in
[n]\setminus U
. Then, there are
m_{1}-r
components of
X_{*}

that are larger than its all components with indices in
[n]\setminus U
.

For the
r
smaller components with indices in
U
, each component can take a distinct rank between 1 and
r
. Hence, there are
r!
possibilities for the ranks of these components. Similarly, for the
m_{1}-r
larger components, each component can take a distinct rank between
n-(m_{1}-r)+1
and
n
. Hence, there are
(m_{1}-r)!
possible ranks for these larger components. Since
0\leq r\leq m_{1}
, the number of possible rank vectors of imputations
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
is given by
\sum_{r=0}^{m_{1}}r!(m_{1}-r)!
.

Even for a moderately small
m_{1}
, such as
m_{1}=20
, the number of permutations can exceed
5.14\times 10^{18}
. Therefore, further refinement of Proposition
2.13
is necessary to determine the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule efficiently.
We therefore consider ordering the components of imputations
X_{*}
with indices in
U
according to the values of their paired components. We start by introducing the following lemma.

Lemma 2.14
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1}\leq n
assume
U=\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and

Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m_{1})
.
Let
X^{\prime}
be any imputation of
X
for indices
U
. Then, suppose

X_{*}
is also an imputation of
X
for indices
U
,
and
(X_{*}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X^{\prime}(i))_{i\in\setminus U}
such that

X_{*}(1)>\cdots>X_{*}(m_{1})
. Then,
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Consider any imputation
X^{\prime}
of
X
for indices
U
, Lemma
2.14
suggests the values of Spearman’s footrule will always be larger or equal if the components of
X^{\prime}
with indices in
U
are further arranged inversely according to their paired components. More formally, let us define

Definition 2.15
.

Suppose
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and let
U\subset T\subset[n]
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(Y;U,T,n)=\left\{Z\in\mathcal{Z}(U,T,n)|~\text{for any}~i,j\in U,~Z(i)>Z(j)~\text{if}~Y(i)<Y(j)\right\}.

Then, combining Proposition
2.13
and Lemma
2.14
, the following result
will allow us to efficiently determine an imputation
X_{*}
that maximises Spearman’s footrule.

Theorem 2.16
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and let
U\subset[n]
.
Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
, and for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for indices
U
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Compared with Proposition
2.13
, Theorem
2.16
further narrows down the scope of possible imputations
X_{*}
to the set
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
for achieving the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule.

Remark 3
.

Suppose an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
. Then
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
, which means all components of
X_{*}
in
U
either larger or smaller than all components in
[n]\setminus U
. Let
m_{1}=|U|
and let
r
be the number of components smaller than all components in
[n]\setminus U
. Then, there are
m_{1}-r
components of
X_{*}
that are larger than its all components with indices in
[n]\setminus U
. The ranks of the
r
smaller components in
U
take all values between 1 and
r
, and the ranks of the
m_{1}-r
larger components in
U
take all values between
n-(m_{1}-r)+1
and
n
. Crucially, the rank of each component in
U
is decided by the rank of its paired component.
Since
0\leq r\leq m_{1}
, the number of possible rank vectors for imputations
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
is
m_{1}+1
.

Computing the value of Spearman’s footrule between
X_{*}
and
Y
, for one possible
X_{*}
,
requires computational complexity
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
. Thus, computing Spearman’s footrule for

X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
with the distinct
(m_{1}+1)
rank vectors has computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(nm_{1}\log n)
. However, it is not necessary to re-rank all components and re-calculate the value of Spearman’s footrule every time the ranks of components of
X_{*}
in
U
are altered. Section
2.5
provides an efficient algorithm for computing the maximum Spearman’s footrule under
Missing Case I
with computational cost
\mathcal{O}(m_{1}+n\log n)
.

2.3.2
Missing Case II

This section considers the upper bound of Spearman’s footrule under
Missing Case II
, where both
X
and
Y
may contain missing components, but each pair
(X(i),Y(i))
has
at most one
value missing.

The following result is obtained by applying Theorem
2.16
under
Missing Case I
twice.

Theorem 2.17
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U,V\subset[n]
.
Then, there exist imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))
of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Furthermore, consider any imputation
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

If we denote
m_{1}=|U|
and
m_{2}=|V|
,
then the number of possible rank vectors
of imputations
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
is
m_{1}+1

and the number of possible rank vectors of imputations

Y_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
is
m_{2}+1
,
following reasoning similar to that in Remark
3
for
Missing Case I
.
Hence, the number of possible combinations of rank vectors of imputations

(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

is
(m_{1}+1)(m_{2}+1)
.

2.3.3
Missing Case III

This section provides the upper bound of Speaman’s footrule under
Missing Case III
,
where for each pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
the two values are either
both
observed or
both
missing.

To start, we consider the case where there is only one pair

(X(u),Y(u))
where both values are missing.
The following result is proved by applying
Proposition
2.11
repeatedly.

Proposition 2.18
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
u\in[n]
. Suppose
X_{1},Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for the index
u
such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right)=1,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{1}(u_{1}),Y_{1}\right)=n,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(u_{1}),X_{2}\right)=n,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{2}(u_{1}),Y_{2}\right)=1.

Then,
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
.

When one pair
(X(u),Y(u))
is missing,
Proposition
2.18
gives similar results as in
Proposition
2.11
, offering two possibilities
for determining the maximum possible value of Spearman’s footrule.

Furthermore, to achieve the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule,
Proposition
2.18
requires the paired ranks of the
imputations for the index
u
to be at opposite ends of the sequence

1,\ldots,n
.
For example, if the imputed rank of
X(u_{1})
is 1, then the
imputed rank of its paired component must be
n
; conversely, if the
imputed rank of
X(u_{1})
is
n
, then the rank of its paired
component must be 1. Let
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
be imputations of

X
and
Y
, respectively.
This condition can be expressed as

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)+R\left(Y_{*}(u),Y_{*}\right)=n+1.

It will be useful to define a set of indices for which this condition is
satisfied.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
W\subset[n]
be a subset of indices.
Define

\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(W,n)=\left\{(Z_{1},Z_{2})\in(\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n),\mathcal{V}^{n})|R\left(Z_{1}(i),Z_{1}\right)+R\left(Z_{2}(i),Z_{2}\right)=n+1,~i\in W\right\}.

Using Definition
C.14
, we can state the following result.

Theorem 2.20
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
.
Then there exist imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Furthermore, consider any other imputations
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
W
.
Then
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Theorem
2.20
establishes the existence of imputations

(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X
and
Y
that will maximize
Spearman’s footrule
D\left(X,Y\right)
.
In order to find this pair
(X_{*},Y_{*})
,
we could enumerate all possible imputations. If
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there would be uncountably many imputations.
But, as already mentioned, we only need to consider
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}

to be
rank
vectors, since
the computation of Spearman’s footrule only uses ranks.
Therefore, the problem is reduced to only considering rank vector imputations,
and these can be enumerated.
However, different rank vector imputations may lead to the same value
of Spearman’s footrule statistic.
Therefore, we will only count the number of
possible
distinct values
of Spearman’s footrule statistic
using (ranked) imputations

(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

For any imputations

(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X
and
Y
for indices

W
, we have
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
using
Definition
C.14
.
Therefore, according to Definition
C.3

of
\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
, either

X_{*}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus W}X_{*}(j)

or

X_{*}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus W}X_{*}(j)
,
for any
i\in W
.

Denote
m_{3}=|W|
and
r=\sum_{i\in W}I\left(X_{*}(i)<(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)
, i.e. the number of components of
X_{*}
with indices in
W
smaller than all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
[n]\setminus W
. Then, there are
m_{3}-r

number of components of
X_{*}
with indices in
W
that are
larger than all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
[n]\setminus W
.

In fact, any imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
with the same value of
r
give the same value of Spearman’s footrule.
For the moment, let
r\in\{1,2,\dots,m_{3}-1\}
.
The
r
number of components of
X_{*}
with indices in
W
smaller than all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
[n]\setminus W
take the ranks between between 1 and

r
, and the
m_{3}-r
components of
X_{*}
with indices in
W
larger than all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
[n]\setminus W
take the
ranks between
n-(m_{3}-r)+1
and
n
. According to the definition of
\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
, the ranks of components of
Y_{*}
in
W
are decided by the equation
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)+R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=n+1
. Hence, without changing the values of Spearman’s footrule, we can reorder the pairs of components of
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
in
W
,
so that
the components of
X_{*}
in
W
are arranged from small to large as

1,\ldots,r,n-(m_{3}-r)+1,\ldots,n,

and then the components of
Y_{*}
in
W
are arranged as

n,\ldots,n+1-r,m_{3}-r,1
.

In the preceding paragraph, we considered
r\in\{1,2,\dots,m_{3}-1\}
, but
it is also possible that
r=0
or
r=m_{3}
,
in which case either all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
W

are smaller or larger than all other components, which can be considered
similarly.

Since
r
ranges between
0
to
m_{3}
,
there are at most
m_{3}+1
possible values
of Spearman’s footrule statistic, given by all possible
imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

The above procedure shows how to construct imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})
for each
of these possible
m_{3}+1
values of Spearman’s footrule.
This is detailed in Algorithm 4 in the Supplementary Material.

2.3.4
General Missing Case

This section considers the upper bound of Spearman’s footrule under the general missing case, where the missing pattern of data is a combination of
Missing Cases I
,
II
, and
III
. The previous results can be combined to give the following result.

Theorem 2.21
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U
,
V
,
W\subset[n]
be pairwise disjoint subsets.
Then, there exist
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of imputations
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Furthermore,
consider any imputation
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively. Then we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

The number of possible rank vectors of imputations
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
for achieving the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule under the general missing case can be analyzed by combining the analysis for
Missing Cases I
,
II
, and
III
.

Let us denote
|U|=m_{1},|V|=m_{2},|W|=m_{3}
. Consider any pair of imputations

(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)\cap(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))
of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, where
U
and
V
are disjoint from
W
.
In other words, consider a pair of imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
, which means the components
in
W
satisfy the condition in Definition
C.14
, and
that
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
satisfy the conditions of Definition
C.8
for the relevant components with indices
in
U
and
V
, respectively.

Now suppose the components of
X_{*}
with indices in
U
and the
components of
Y_{*}
with indices in
V
are fixed.
Then, following the discussion of Theorem
2.20
, there are
m_{3}+1

permutations of ranks of the imputations
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
for indices in
W
that would result in
different values of Spearman’s footrule.

Next, suppose the components
X_{*}
and the components of
Y_{*}

with indices in
W
are fixed.
Then there are
m_{1}+1
and

m_{2}+1
permutations of ranks of the imputations
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}

with indices in
U
and
V
, respectively, that result in different values
of Spearman’s footrule,
following a similar argument to Remark
3
.

Hence, considering all imputations for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
,
the total number of imputations to consider in order to find the maximum
value of Spearman’s footrule is
(m_{1}+1)(m_{2}+1)(m_{3}+1)
.

For calculating the values of Spearman’s footrule of the
(m_{1}+1)(m_{2}+1)(m_{3}+1)

number of imputations, an efficient algorithm is provided in Section 2 of the Supplementary Material, which
has computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n\log n+m_{1}m_{2}m_{3})
.

2.4
Additional Results

Obtaining the bounds of Spearman’s footrule with partially observed data
X,Y
allows us to derive the bounds of Kendall’s
\tau
for the same data.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and define Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient according to (
3
). The following result is proved in Theorem 2 in
[
13
]
, which shows that the Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient and Spearman’s footrule
D
differ by at most a constant factor.

Theorem 2.22

(
[
13
]
)
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
. Then, the values of Spearman’s footrule and Kendall’s
\tau
between
X
and
Y
are such that

\displaystyle\tau({X},{Y})\leq D\left(X,Y\right)\leq 2\tau({X},{Y}).

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are partially observed. Let us denote the exact lower and upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule between
X,Y
as
D_{\min}(X,Y)
and
D_{\max}(X,Y)
, respectively, which can be computed according to Theorem
2.10
and Theorem
2.21
, respectively. Since
D_{\min}(X,Y)\leq D(X,Y)\leq D_{\max}(X,Y)
, Theorem
2.22
gives

\displaystyle D_{\min}(X,Y)/2\leq\tau({X},{Y})\leq D_{\max}(X,Y).

(4)

In Section
2.6
, these bounds of the Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient given in (
4
) are investigated empirically using numerical simulations.

2.5
An efficient algorithm for computing the upper bounds

This section provides an efficient algorithm for calculating exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data. We consider
Missing Case I
in this section, i.e. missing components are either all in
X
or all in
Y
. The algorithms for
Missing Case II
,
III
and the general missing cases can be constructed similarly, and are provided in Section 2 of the Supplementary Material.

Without loss of generality, let us assume
X
is partially observed while

Y
is fully observed. Denote the indices of all missing components in

X
as
U
and denote
m_{1}=|U|
. Further, assume

U=\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and the components of
Y
with indices in
U

are such that
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m_{1})
. If this is not the case,
simply relabel the relevant components in
X
and
Y
.

Under
Missing Case I
, Theorem
2.16
allows us to obtain the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule by considering imputations
X_{*}

of
X
for indices
U
in
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.
Remark
3
further demonstrates
that it is adequate to only consider
m_{1}+1
imputations
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, each with a distinct value of
r=\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}(i)<\min(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
, i.e. the number of components of
X_{*}
in
U
that are smaller than all components of
X_{*}
with indices in
[n]\setminus U
, where
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
. Let us denote the
m_{1}+1
imputations as
X_{*}^{(0)},\ldots,X_{*}^{(m_{1})}
, corresponding to the imputations with
r=0,\ldots,m_{1}
. Then the maximum possible value of Spearman’s footrule between partially observed
X
and
Y
is
\max\left\{D(X_{*}^{(0)},Y),\ldots,D(X_{*}^{(m_{1})},Y)\right\}
.

The computational complexity of naively computing
all
m_{1}+1
values in the above set

\left\{D(X_{*}^{(0)},Y),\ldots,D(X_{*}^{(m_{1})},Y)\right\}

is
\mathcal{O}((m_{1}+1)n\log n)
, since computing the value of Spearman’s footrule once is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
.

However, we notice that it is not necessary to re-rank all components and re-calculate the value of Spearman’s footrule every time the ranks of components of
X_{*}
in
U
are altered.
To construct an algorithm for efficiently calculating exact upper bounds of
Spearman’s footrule under
Missing Case I
in
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
,
we provide the following result

Proposition 2.23
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1}\in[n]
,
let
U=\{{1},\ldots,{m_{1}}\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Suppose

Y({1})<\ldots<Y({m_{1}})
, and
let
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

be an imputation of
X
for indices in
U
such that

\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
.
For any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, denote
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
,
and for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, denote
s_{r}=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(d_{i}\leq r\right)
. Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y)

\displaystyle=D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)+2s_{r}-n+m_{1}+C^{(r)},

where
C^{(r)}=|r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|-|n-m_{1}+r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|
.

Proposition
2.23
shows that the maximum possible values of Spearman’s footrule can be found in linear time after ranking all observed components in
X
and
Y
, as shown in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

An efficient algorithm for computing exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule under Missing Case I.

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where either
X
or
Y
is fully observed,
while the other is partially observed.

2:

Maximum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

If
X
is fully observed while
Y
is partially observed, then change the label of
X
and
Y
.

4:

Denote
m_{1}
as the number of missing components in
X
.
If
m_{1}=n
, return
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|i-(n-i+1)|
.

5:

Rank all observed components in
X
and
Y
.

6:

Relabel
X
and
Y
such that
(X(1),\ldots,X(m_{1}))
are unobserved and
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m_{1})
.

7:

Let
U=\{1,2,\dots,m_{1}\}.
For
i\in[n]\setminus U
, let
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

8:

Computing
s_{r}=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(d_{i}\leq r\right)
for any
r=\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
.

9:

For any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, compute

\displaystyle C^{(r)}=|r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|-|n-m_{1}+r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|.

10:

Initialize
D_{0}
=
\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|(n-i+1)-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]}\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}|d_{i}|
.

11:

For
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, compute
D_{r+1}=D_{r}+2s_{r}-n+m_{1}+C^{(r)}
.

12:

Return
\max\{D_{0},\ldots,D_{m_{1}}\}
.

Remark 4
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
3
is analyzed as follows. Ranking and relabeling
all observed components in
X
and
Y
in line
3
requires
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps.
Using these rankings, in line
7
computing each
d_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and so overall
line
7
is
\mathcal{O}(n-m_{1})
.
In line
8
,
s_{0},\dots,s_{m_{1}-1}
can be computed
collectively in
\mathcal{O}(n-m_{1})
steps. See Algorithm 1 in the Supplementary Material.
is
\mathcal{O}(n+m_{1})=\mathcal{O}(n)
. In line
9
, each iteration of the for loop is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and since the loop runs
m_{1}
times, the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
. Line
10
and
11

takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
steps, respectively.
Therefore, the overall computational complexity for Algorithm
3
is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
.

2.6
Simulation results for the bounds

This section provides numerical simulations that evaluate the performance of the exact bounds of Spearman’s footrule, and the bounds of Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient provided in (
4
), between partially observed
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, with different proportions of the missing pairs.

Additionally, we also investigate the behaviour of the methods when (i) the complete data are used and (ii) the missing values are ignored. When the missing values are ignored, the rank correlation coefficients are computed using only the pairs where both components from
X
and
Y
are observed.
When the data are missing not at random (MNAR),
ignoring the missing data can lead to biased estimates of the correlation coefficients,
as shown in Figure
2
.

We first scale all the rank correlation coefficients between
[-1,1]
. For scaling Spearman’s footrule, we define

\displaystyle D_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)=1-{3D\left(X,Y\right)}/{(n^{2}-1)}.

(5)

When
n
is odd,
D_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)\in[-0.5,1]
, but when
n
is even,

D_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)
is in the range
[-0.5\{1+3/(n^{2}-1)\},1]

[
21
]
. For scaling Spearman’s
\rho
and Kendall’s
\tau
, define

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)=1-\rho(X,Y)/\{n(n^{2}-1)\},\,\,\,\,\tau_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)=1-4\tau(X,Y)/\{n(n-1)\},

(6)

respectively, where
\tau(X,Y)
and
\rho(X,Y)
are given in
(
2
) and (
3
),
respectively. It can be shown that both
\rho_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)

and
\tau_{\text{Scale}}(X,Y)
are in the range
[-1,1]

[
21
]
.

2.6.1
The bounds when data are missing completely at random

In the first simulation, data are assumed to be missing completely at random (MCAR).
First,
X,Y
are generated such that each pair
(X(i),Y(i))

is an independent random sample from a two-dimensional standard normal distribution with covariance coefficient 0;
in other words.
X
and
Y
are uncorrelated. The sample size for
X
and
Y
is
n=100
.
Then, for each given proportion
s\in\{0,0.05,\ldots,0.30\}
a subset of indices
T
in

\{1,2,\dots,n\}
of size

\lfloor s\cdot n\rfloor
is selected to be the set of components that are either partially observed or missing.
For each
i\in T
,
there is probability
1/3
such that
X(i)
is missing but
Y(i)
is observed,
probability
1/3
such that
Y(i)
is missing but
X(i)
is observed,
and probability
1/3
such that both
X(i)
and
Y(i)
are missing.
In any of these three cases, where at least one of
X(i)
and
Y(i)

is missing, we say that
(X(i),Y(i))
is an
incomplete pair
.

Figure 1:
Rank correlation coefficients when data are missing completely at random (MCAR).

X,Y
are generated such that

(X,Y)\sim^{iid}N(0,I_{2})
. The methods are described in Table
2
.
The sample size for
X
and
Y
is
n=100
.
Results represent the average of 1000 Monte Carlo simulations.

Subsequently, the bounds of Spearman’s footrule
for partially observed
X
and
Y
will be computed using
Theorems
2.10
and
2.21
, while
the bounds for Kendall’s
\tau
for partially observed data will be computed using
(
4
).
We do not have bounds for Spearman’s
\rho
for partially observed data, but we
compute values for all three rank correlation coefficients in the cases where the
complete data is used or missing values are ignored.
Figure
1
presents the results, which are an average over
1000
Monte Carlo simulations.

Figure
1
shows that
when the (uncorrelated) data are MCAR, ignoring the missing values will result in the methods
producing unbiased estimates of the rank correlation coefficients, i.e.
Footrule-ignore
,
\tau
-ignore

and
\rho
-ignore
are all approximately zero.
Similarly, when the complete data are used,
the three rank correlation coefficients will be unbiased, i.e.

Footrule-complete
,
\tau
-complete

and
\rho
-complete
are all approximately zero.

The range of bounds of Spearman’s footrule,
labelled
Footrule-lower
and
Footrule-upper
,
increases gradually with the increasing proportion of incomplete pairs
s
. When
s=0.3
, the lower and upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule are around -0.2 and 0.4, respectively.

For Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient, when the proportion of incomplete pairs is 0,
the lower and upper bounds of Kendall’s
\tau
,
labelled by
\tau
-lower
and
\tau
-upper
,
range from approximately
-0.3
to
0.3
.
The bounds become wider as the proportion of incomplete pairs increases.
When
s=0.3
, the lower and upper bounds of Kendall’s
\tau
are approximately -0.6 and 0.5, respectively.
It appears that the bounds of Spearman’s footrule and Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient
increase or decrease linearly, depending on the proportion
s
of missing components.

2.6.2
The bounds when data are missing not at random

The second simulation is designed in the same way as the first, except now the data
are
missing not at random
(MNAR), rather than being MCAR.
There are many ways to defined a MNAR mechanism; we propose one approach here,
and consider a second approach in the Supplementary Material.
Here, the index set
T\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
of the missing components
is chosen now depends on the values of
X
and
Y
.
Let
q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I\left(X(i)Y(i)>0\right)

be the number of pairs of components in
X
and
Y
such that their product
X(i)Y(i)>0
.
Each index
i\in[n]

is selected to be in the set
T
of indices of missing components according to the
following probability conditional on
C=|T|
, the size of the set
T
:

\displaystyle p\left(i\in T\,|\,C=\lfloor s\cdot n\rfloor\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\min\left\{1,sn/q\right\},&\mbox{if}X(i)Y(i)>0,\\
\max\{0,(sn-q)/(n-q)\},&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

for any given
s
.
If
sn<q
, then
\min\left\{1,sn/q\right\}<1
and
\max\{0,(sn-q)/(n-q)\}=0
,
and so some components with
X(i)Y(i)>0

will be fully observed, and no components with
X(i)Y(i)\leq 0
will be partially observed.
However, if
sn\geq q
, then all components with
X(i)Y(i)>0
will be partially observed
and, moreover, if
sn>q
then some components with
X(i)Y(i)\leq 0
will be partially observed.
The probability is conditional in order to ensure we have subsets of indices of the desired cardinality

C=\lfloor s\cdot n\rfloor
.

Figure
2
shows that when the data are MNAR with the missingness mechanism
specified in (
2.6.2
), then

Footrule-ignore
,
\tau
-ignore

and
\rho
-ignore

produce biased estimates of their rank correlation coefficients.
Furthermore, as the proportion of incomplete pairs, denoted by
s
, increases,
the bias of

Footrule-ignore
,
\tau
-ignore

and
\rho
-ignore

also increases.
On the other hand,

Footrule-lower
,
Footrule-upper
,
\tau
-lower
and
\tau
-upper

have similar performance compared to when data are MCAR in Figure
1
.
In Section G.2.4 of the Supplementary Material we repeat this experiment using a
different MNAR missing mechanism, with similar results.

3
Independence Testing with Missing Data

We now explore the use of the bounds of Spearman’s footrule derived above for
independence testing in the presence of missing data.
The core idea is to use the bounds for the statistic to obtain bounds for the

p
-value, and then to reject the null hypothesis when

all possible

p
-values will be significant.

More specifically, our method uses the bounds of the Spearman’s footrule
statistic to compute bounds for the possible
p
-values,
given the missing data.
In other words, if the missing data could have been fully observed, the
resultant
p
-value would be within the computed bounds.
Then, for a given significance level
\alpha
, if the bounds show that all
possible
p
-values
are smaller than
\alpha
, the null hypothesis is rejected. On the other hand,
if at least one
p
-value is not signficant, then the null hypothesis fails
to be rejected. Our motivation for this approach is discussed in the
introduction.

Below we will first show how our method bounds the
p
-values of
Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data using the bounds
of the Spearman’s footrule statistic. Then we will perform numerical
simulations for investigating the Type I error and statistical power of our
method. The unique contribution of our method is that, unlike all other
existing methods, it provides an independence testing approach that
controls the Type I error regardless of the missing data mechanism.
The only assumption we make about the data is that the values are
all distinct in order to avoid dealing with the issue of ties.

3.1
Bounds of
p
-values in the presence of missing data

For deriving the bounds of the
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule with missing
data, we begin by studying the distribution of Spearman’s footrule statistic
under the null hypothesis that
X
and
Y
are generated by independent
continuous random variables
without
missing data.

Table 3:
Description of the independence testing methods, often in the presence of
missing data, shown in Figure
3
–
6
.

Testing method

Description

Proposed

Based on
p
-values computed from bounds Footrule-upper and Footrule-lower.

Footrule-ignore

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule, when partially-observed data is ignored.

Footrule-complete

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule, when data is fully observed.

Footrule-mean

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule, using mean imputation for missing values.

Footrule-median

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule, using median imputation for missing values.

Footrule-hot deck

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule, using hot deck imputation for missing values.

\tau
-ignore

Based on
p
-value of Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient, when partially observed data is ignored.

\tau
-complete

Based on
p
-value of Kendall’s
\tau
coefficient, when the data is fully observed.

\tau
-mean

Based on
p
-value of Kendall’s
\tau
, using mean imputation for missing values.

\tau
-median

Based on
p
-value of Kendall’s
\tau
, using median imputation for missing values.

\tau
-hot deck

Based on
p
-value of Kendall’s
\tau
, using hot deck imputation for missing values.

\rho
-ignore

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s
\rho
, when partially observed data is ignored.

\rho
-complete

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s
\rho
, when the data is fully observed.

\rho
-mean

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s
\rho
, using mean imputation for missing values.

\rho
-median

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s
\rho
, using median imputation for missing values.

\rho
-hot deck

Based on
p
-value of Spearman’s
\rho
, using hot deck imputation for missing values.

Alvo and Cabilio’s
\rho

Based on
p
-value of estimate of Spearman’s rank correlation
\rho
, from
[
1
]
.

Alvo and Cabilio’s
\tau

Based on
p
-value of estimate of Kendall’s
\tau
coefficent, from
[
1
]
.

A well-known result
[
13
, Theorem 1]
shows that under the null hypothesis, the distribution of Spearman’s footrule statistic for
n
pairs of observations approximately follows a normal distribution with mean and variance equal to
n^{2}/3
and
2n^{3}/45
, respectively. The accuracy of this approximation is investigated in
[
35
]
, showing that when the sample size is at least
n=40
, this normal approximation performs reasonably well: the difference in skewness between the exact and normal approximation distribution of Spearman’s footrule is
-0.075
and the difference in kurtosis is
-0.074
.

Note that this result regarding the distribution of the test statistic is not
making any assumption about the distribution of the data itself.
Furthermore, if one uses this result to compute a
p
-value for
independence testing, the Type I error will be controlled.

Suppose the sample size
n
is large enough for employing normal approximation, e.g.
n\geq 40
. Denote
F_{n}
as the cumulative distribution function of a normal distribution with mean equal to
n^{2}/3
and variance equal to
2n^{3}/45
.
The
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule is then calculated as

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))=2\cdot{\min}\{F_{n}(D(X,Y)),1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\}.

(9)

Now suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are partially observed, and denote the minimum and maximum values of Spearman’s footrule between
X
and
Y
as
D_{\min}
and
D_{\max}
, respectively. The bounds of the
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule are then determined using the following result.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are partially observed. Assume
n
is
sufficiently large. Let
D_{\min}
and
D_{\max}
be the minimum and
maximum possible values of Spearman’s footrule between
X
and
Y
,
respectively. Denote
p_{1}=p(D_{\min})
and
p_{2}=p(D_{\max})
, where
p(\cdot)
is defined in (
181
). Furthermore, define
p_{\min}=\min\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
and define

\displaystyle p_{\max}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\max\{p_{1},p_{2}\},&\mbox{if }(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0,\\
1,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then, the
p
-value of
D\left(X,Y\right)
is bounded such that
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]
.

In Proposition
3.1
, “
n
is sufficiently large” is used to allow the use of normal approximation for computing the
p
-value of Spearman’s footrule.

We now describe our method for independence testing
in the presence of missing data using
Proposition
3.1
.
Given a pre-specified significance level
\alpha
,
our method will reject the null hypothesis if
p_{\max}<\alpha
,
since this implies that all possible
p
-values are less than

\alpha
and will be significant. However, our method
will fail to reject the null hypothesis if
p_{\min}>\alpha
,
since this implies that all possible
p
-values are greater than

\alpha
and so none will be significant.

Crucially, since our method rejects the null hypothesis only when
p(D(X,Y))

is significant, the probability of our method making a Type I error is no larger
than the probability of making a Type I error when data are fully observed,
which is approximately equal to
\alpha
.
In other words, our method is guaranteed to control
the Type I error.

3.2
Simulation results for independence testing

This section performs numerical simulations to investigate the Type I error and
the statistical power of the proposed method for independence testing when
data are partially observed.

For comparison with the proposed method, we also consider the three methods:
Spearman’s footrule, Kendall’s
\tau
and Spearman’s
\rho
, in the two cases
where the missing data is ignored and the complete data is observed.
When the missing data are ignored, the correlation coefficients are computed only
using pairs where both components from
X
and
Y
are observed.
We also consider these three methods when imputation methods are used;
we consider mean imputation, median imputation and hot deck imputation

[
38
]
.

Two independence testing methods that take missing data into account are also considered.
These methods
[
1
]
are defined as
the conditional expectations of Spearman’s
\rho
and Kendall’s
\tau
, respectively,
given the observed ranks, under the assumption that the ranks of the missing values
are uniformly distributed.
In the following, we will refer to the two methods
as Alvo and Cabilio’s
\rho
and Alvo and Cabilio’s
\tau
methods, respectively.

3.2.1
As the proportion of missing data increases

In the first experiment, we explore how the methods perform as
the proportion
s
of missing data increases. More precisely,
we consider
s
to be the proportion of incomplete pairs, where
we recall that
(X(i),Y(i))
is an incomplete pair if
at least one of
X(i)
or
Y(i)
is missing.

We start by considering the case when the data are missing completely at
random (MCAR), as the proportion of incomplete pairs increases.
For evaluating
the Type I error of all methods, the data are generated following the same
distribution and missingness mechanism for Figure
1
, as described
in Section
2.6.1
, with sample size
n=500
.
The significance level
\alpha=0.05
is used for all test
methods. For evaluating the power, the data are generated in the same way,
except that the covariance matrix is now

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\\
\gamma&1\end{pmatrix}
,
with covariance coefficient
\gamma=0.5
.
The Supplementary Material contains results for the same experiment, but
with different values of
\alpha
, different values of
the covariance coefficient
\gamma
, and different sample sizes
n
.

Figure 3:
Statistical Type I error and power of the proposed method,
as the proportion of missing data
s
increases,
where
s\in\{0.00,0.02,\ldots,0.30\}
.
Other methods that ignore missing data or use complete data,
and Alvo and Cabilio’s
\rho
and Alvo and Cabilio’s
\tau
methods
are also considered.
These methods are described in Table
3
.
The data is missing completely at random (MCAR).
(Left) Type I error:
(X,Y)\overset{iid}{\sim}N(0,I_{2})
;
(Right) Power:
(X,Y)\overset{iid}{\sim}N(0,\Sigma)
, where

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\\
\gamma&1\end{pmatrix}
,
with covariance coefficient
\gamma=0.5
.
For both figures, a significance level
\alpha=0.05
is used and the sample
size
n=200
. The results in the figures are average of 1000 trials.

increases,

.

and Alvo and Cabilio’s

methods

.

size
