. The results in the figures are average of 1000 trials.

Figure
3
shows that all methods appear to control the Type I error
when the data are MCAR.
All methods appear to have good statistical power, but the power of
the proposed method starts to drop
when the proportion of incomplete pairs is larger than 10%, and when

22\%
of pairs are incomplete, the power is
0
.

We next consider the case when the data are missing not at random (MNAR).
The parameters of this experiment are the same as for the MCAR case, but
now the missingness mechanism is
as described in in Section
2.6.2
.

Figure
4
shows that when data are MNAR, only the proposed
Spearman’s footrule method and the complete data methods control
the Type I error, while the other methods fail to control the Type I
error when at least
4\%
of the data is incomplete.
When the proportion of incomplete pairs is

10\%
, the methods that ignore the missing data and Alvo and Cabilio’s

\rho
and
\tau
methods have Type I error close to
25\%
.
Meanwhile, all methods have good power with small proportion of incomplete pairs.
The power of the proposed method starts to drop when the proportion
s

at least
8\%
, and drops to
0
when the proportion

s
is larger than
18\%
.

We also investigate how imputation methods perform
in the experiment above, when data are missing not at random.
We consider using mean imputation, median imputation and hot deck
imputation
[
38
]
for Spearman’s footrule, Kendall’s
\tau
and
Spearman’s
\rho
. The results are shown in Figure
5
, which
demonstrates that these imputation methods also fail to control
the Type I error rate when at least
4\%
of the data is incomplete.

3.2.2
As the sample size increases

We again consider the above case when the data are missing not at random,
but now as the sample size
n
increases, with fixed proportion of
missing pairs
s=0.1
, and fixed correlation coefficient
\gamma=0.5

for the alternative hypothesis.
Figure
6
shows that the proposed method still controls
the Type I error as the sample size increases. Furthermore,
while the proposed method is not very powerful for smaller sample sizes,
as the sample size
n
increases, its power goes to
1
.
On the other hand, the other approaches have good power, but,
excluding the complete data methods, all other approaches fail
to control the Type I error, and their Type I error increases as

n
increases.

Supplementary Material

Appendix A
Some important lemmas

In this section, we provide several lemmas that will be important for
deriving exact bounds of the Spearman’s footrule in the presence of missing data.

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
O,O^{\prime}\subset[n]
are non-empty subsets of indices such that
O\subset O^{\prime}
. Denote
|O^{\prime}\setminus O|=k
. Then, for any
i\in O
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+k\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O^{\prime}}\right)\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Proof.

Let
i\in O
be a fixed index. Then, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O^{\prime}}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)+\sum_{l\in O^{\prime}\setminus O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+\sum_{l\in O^{\prime}\setminus O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right).

Since
|O^{\prime}\setminus O|=k
, we have
k\geq\sum_{l\in O^{\prime}\setminus O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)\geq 0
. Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+k\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O^{\prime}}\right)\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right),

which completes our proof.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,O\subset[n]
are non-empty disjoint subsets of indices such that
U\cup O=[n]
. Then, for any
i\in O
, if either of the following two conditions hold:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):X(j)>X(i),\text{ for any }j\in U,

or

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):R\left(X(j),X\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right),\text{ for any }j\in U,

we have
R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)
.

Proof.

First, we show that if the condition
(\mathrm{i})

is true, then we have
R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)
.

To start, let
i\in O
be a fixed index such that
the condition
(\mathrm{i})
is true, i.e.,
for any
j\in U
,

X(j)>X(i)
.
According to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=\sum_{l\in[n]}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right).

Since
U
and
O
are non-empty disjoint subsets of
[n]
such that
U\cup O=[n]
, then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=\sum_{l\in O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)+\sum_{l\in U}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right).

Now, according to the condition
(\mathrm{i})
, we have

X(j)>X(i)
, for any
j\in U
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in U}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)=0,

which then follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=\sum_{l\in O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(i)\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Thus, we complete our prove when the condition
(\mathrm{i})
is true.

Next, we show that if the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
is true,
we have
R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)
.
Let
i\in O
be any fixed index such that the
condition
(\mathrm{ii})
is true, i.e., for any
j\in U
,

R\left(X(j),X\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)
.
We prove the result for the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
by showing that the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
implies
the condition
(\mathrm{i})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle R\left(X(j),X\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right),\text{ for any }j\in U\Rightarrow X(j)>X(i),\text{ for any }j\in U.

Without loss of generality, denote
u
as the index of the minimum value in
(X(l))_{l\in U}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle X(u)=\min(X(l))_{l\in U}.

Then, all components with indices in
U\setminus\{u\}
are larger than
X(u)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in U}I\left(X(l)\leq X(u)\right)=I\left(X(u)\leq X(u)\right)=1.

Subsequently, since
U
and
O
are non-empty disjoint subsets of
[n]
such that
U\cup O=[n]
, then according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(X(u),X\right)&=\sum_{l\in O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(u)\right)+\sum_{l\in U}I\left(X(l)\leq X(u)\right)\\
&=\sum_{l\in O}I\left(X(l)\leq X(u)\right)+I\left(X(u)\leq X(u)\right)\\
&=\sum_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}I\left(X(l)\leq X(u)\right)\\
&=R\left(X(u),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right).\end{split}

(12)

Next, according to Lemma
A.1
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+1\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right).

According to the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
, we have
R\left(X(j),X\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right),\text{ for any }j\in U
. Notice that
u\in U
, then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+1,

where the last
``\Rightarrow"
holds because
R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)

and
R\left(X(u),X\right)
are both integers.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)+1\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right).

According to (
12
), we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right).

Since
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values, and
u\notin O
,
then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right)\neq R\left(X(u),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right)>R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O\cup\{u\}}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X(u)>X(i).

Recall that
X(u)=\min(X(l))_{l\in U}
. Therefore, we have
X(j)>X(i)
for any
j\in U
.

Then, by applying
the result when the condition
(\mathrm{i})
holds,
we have
R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in O}\right)
,
which completes our proof.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,O
are non-empty disjoint subsets of
[n]
such that
U\cup O=[n]
. Then, if
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}

for indices
U
and

R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U,

we have
R\left(X_{1},X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2},X_{2}\right)
.

Proof.

To start, let us denote

\displaystyle U=\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{m}\},\text{ and }O=\{o_{1},\cdots,o_{n-m}\}.

For both
j=1,2
, since
X_{j}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of
n
distinct real numbers, the ranks of all components in
X_{j}
form a permutation of
\{1,\cdots,n\}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\{R\left(X_{j}(1),X_{j}\right),\cdots,R\left(X_{j}(n),X_{j}\right)\}=\{1,\cdots,n\},\text{ for both }j=\{1,2\}.

Let us denote

\displaystyle S_{j}=\{1,\cdots,n\}\setminus\{R\left(X_{j}(u_{1}),X_{j}\right),\cdots,R\left(X_{j}(u_{m}),X_{j}\right)\},\text{ for both }j=\{1,2\}.

Then, since
U\cup O=[n]=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle\{R\left(X_{j}(o_{1}),X_{j}\right),\cdots,R\left(X_{j}(o_{n-m}),X_{j}\right)\}=S_{j},\text{ for both }j=1,2.

Since
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)
, for any
i\in U
, we have
S_{2}=S_{1}
.

Notice that for any fixed
i\in\{1,\cdots,n-m\}
, and
j=1,2
, the order of
R\left(X_{j}(o_{i}),X_{j}\right)
in
S_{j}
equals to the order of
X_{j}(o_{i})
in
\{X_{j}(o_{1}),\ldots,X_{j}(o_{n-m})\}
.
Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}

for indices
U
, then according to the definition of imputations,
for any
i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n-m\}
, we have
X_{1}(o_{i})=X_{2}(o_{i})
.
Hence, the order of
X_{1}(o_{i})
in
\{X_{1}(o_{1}),\ldots,X_{1}(o_{n-m})\}

equals to the order of
X_{2}(o_{i})
in
\{X_{2}(o_{1}),\ldots,X_{1}(o_{n-m})\}
.
Thus, the order of
R\left(X_{1}(o_{i}),X_{1}\right)
in
S_{1}
equals to the order of
R\left(X_{2}(o_{i}),X_{2}\right)
in
S_{2}
. Furthermore, since
S_{1}=S_{2}
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(o_{i}),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(o_{i}),X_{2}\right),

where
i\in\{1,\cdots,n-m\}
. Since we also have
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U
, and
O\cup U=[n]
, then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in[n].

In order words, we have
R\left(X_{1},X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2},X_{2}\right)
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of lower bounds

This section provides results for deriving exact lower bounds of Spearman’s footrule
in the presence of missing data.

B.1
Proof of Proposition 2.1

This subsection proves Proposition 2.1.
First, we prove four lemmas that will be useful for proving
Proposition 2.1.

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose
X,X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and let
X_{*}
be an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
. Then, if
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+1
, we have
X_{*}(u)>X(u)
and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)>X(u)\right)I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=1.

However, if
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-1
, we have
X_{*}(u)<X(u)
and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(u)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)=1.

Proof.

To start, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)=\sum_{i\in[n]}I\left(X(i)\leq X(u)\right)=I\left(X(u)\leq X(u)\right)+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)\leq X(u)\right).

Since
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)=1+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right).

(13)

Similarly, we can obtain

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=1+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X_{*}(i)<X_{*}(u)\right).

Since
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
,
then according to the definition of imputations,
we have
X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
. Hence, we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=1+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right).

(14)

Subsequently, if
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+1
, by combining (
13
) and (
14
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)+1.

Hence, it can be seen that
X_{*}(u)>X(u)
.

Now, since
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values, we further have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(u)<X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(u)<X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)>X(u)\right)I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=1,

which proves our results when
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+1
.

The case when
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-1
can be proved similarly. Combining (
13
) and (
14
),
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)-1,

which follows
X_{*}(u)<X(u)
.

Since
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values and
X_{*}(u)\neq X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X_{*}(u)<X(i)<X(u)\right)-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X_{*}(u)<X(i)<X(u)\right)=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(u)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)=1,

which proves our results when
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-1
,
and completes out proof.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
.
For any
i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
, let us denote

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\xi_{i}=\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|,\\
\text{ and }&\psi_{i}=\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.\end{split}

Then, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\left|R\left(X_{*}(u),X\right)-R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\right|-\left|R\left(X(u),X\right)-R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(u)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(u)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(u)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(u)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i}).

Proof.

For convenience, let us assume (after relabeling)
u=1
, and denote

\displaystyle\alpha=\left|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\right|,\text{ and }\beta=\left|R\left(X_{*}(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\right|.

According to the definition of Spearman’s footrule, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\right|+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

According to the definition of rank, for any
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{n}I\left(X(1)\leq X(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2}^{n}I\left(X(1)\leq X(i)\right)+I\left(X(1)\leq X(i)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+I\left(X(1)\leq X(i)\right).

Since
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct values, we have
I\left(X(1)\leq X(i)\right)=I\left(X(1)<X(i)\right)
. Hence, for any
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+I\left(X(1)<X(i)\right).

Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)=\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+I\left(X(1)<X(i)\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

Subsequently,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&D\left(X,Y\right)\\
&=\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(1)<X(i)\right)\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\\
&+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(1)>X(i)\right)\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\\
&=\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\xi_{i}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\psi_{i}.\end{split}

Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\\
&=\beta+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(1)<X_{*}(i)\right)\left|R\left(X_{*}(i),(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\\
&+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(1)>X_{*}(i)\right)\left|R\left(X_{*}(i),(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.\end{split}

Since
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
1
,
then according to the definition of imputations,
we have
X_{*}(i)=X(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{1\}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D\left(X_{*},Y\right)&=\beta+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\\
&+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\\
&=\beta+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)\xi_{i}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)\psi_{i}.\end{split}

Now, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)&=\beta-\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left\{I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\right\}\xi_{i}\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left\{I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\right\}\psi_{i}.\end{split}

(15)

Next, since
X,X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are both vectors of distinct real values,
then for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, we have
X(i)\neq X(1)
, and
X_{*}(i)\neq X_{*}(1)
. Further, since
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
1
, we have

X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
. Hence, we also have
X(i)\neq X_{*}(1)
for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
.
Subsequently, for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)\{I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)\geq X(1)\right)\}

\displaystyle-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\{I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)\geq X_{*}(1)\right)\}

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X(1)\right)

\displaystyle-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X_{*}(1)\right)

By rearranging the above terms, we further have

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)

\displaystyle+I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X_{*}(1)\right)

Since for any
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
, we have
X(i)\neq X(1),X_{*}(1)
. Hence,

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)=I\left(X(i)\geq X_{*}(1)\right),

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)\geq X(1)\right)=I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right),

which means

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\geq X_{*}(1)\right)=0

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\\
&=I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right).\end{split}

(16)

Similarly, for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)\{I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)\leq X(1)\right)\}

\displaystyle-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\{I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right)\}

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)+I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X(1)\right)

\displaystyle-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right).

By rearranging the above terms, we further have

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)

\displaystyle=I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)

\displaystyle+I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right).

Since for any
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
, we have
X(i)\neq X(1),X_{*}(1)
. Hence,

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)=I\left(X(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right),

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)\leq X(1)\right)=I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right),

which means

\displaystyle I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right)=0.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\\
&=I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)-I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right).\end{split}

(17)

Putting (
16
) and (
17
) back into (
15
), we obtain

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\beta-\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\xi_{i}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\xi_{i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\psi_{i}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\psi_{i}.

That is,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\beta-\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i}).

This completes our proof.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
. Then, if either of the two following conditions hold

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):R\left(X(u),X\right)>R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-1,

or

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):R\left(X(u),X\right)<R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+1,

we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.
However, if either of the two following conditions hold

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iii}):R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+1,

or

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iv}):R\left(X(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-1,

we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\geq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

Proof.

For convenience, let us assume (after relabeling)
u=1
, and denote

\displaystyle\alpha=\left|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\right|,\text{ and }\beta=\left|R\left(X_{*}(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\right|.

For any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, let us denote

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\xi_{i}=\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|,\\
\text{ and }&\psi_{i}=\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.\end{split}

Then, for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, if

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\geq 0,

we have

\displaystyle\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right),

\displaystyle\text{ and }\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)+1-R\left(Y(i),Y\right).

Hence we have
\xi_{i}=\psi_{i}+1
. However, if

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)<0,

we have

\displaystyle\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right),

\displaystyle\text{ and }\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)-1.

Hence we have
\xi_{i}=\psi_{i}-1
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}\in\{-1,1\},\text{ for any }i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}.

(18)

Next, according to Lemma
B.2
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\beta-\alpha+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i}).

For notation ease, let us denote

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}),

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i}).

Then we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)=\beta-\alpha+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}.

(19)

Suppose the condition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):R\left(X(1),X\right)>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1

holds. Then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)>0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha=|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\geq 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\beta=|R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1-R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\beta=\alpha-1.

Meanwhile, since
R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1
and
X_{*}
is an imputation
of
X
for the index
1
, then according to Lemma
B.1
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)<X(1)\Rightarrow I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)=0,\text{ for any }i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i})=0.

According to Lemma
B.1
, we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)=1.

According to (
18
), we have
\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, which then follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)=1.

Recall that
\beta=\alpha-1
. Subsequently, following (
19
), we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)=-1+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}\leq-1+1+0=0,

which proves our results when the condition
(\mathrm{i})
holds. The other three cases can be proved similarly.

Suppose the condition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):R\left(X(1),X\right)<R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1

holds. Then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)<0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha=|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(Y(1),Y\right)-R\left(X(1),X\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\leq 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\beta=|R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(Y(1),Y\right)-R\left(X(1),X\right)-1.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\beta=\alpha-1.

Since
R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1
, and
X_{*}
is an imputation
of
X
for the index
1
, then according to Lemma
B.1
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)>X(1)\Rightarrow I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)=0\text{ for any }i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})=0.

According to Lemma
B.1
, we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)=1.

According to (
18
), we have
\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, which follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)=1.

Recall that
\beta=\alpha-1
. Subsequently, following (
19
), we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)=-1+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}\leq-1+0+1=0,

which proves our results when the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
holds.

Suppose the condition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iii}):R\left(X(1),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1

holds. Then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\geq 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha=|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(X(1),Y\right)-R\left(Y(1),X\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)>0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\beta=|R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(X(1),Y\right)-R\left(Y(1),X\right)+1.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\beta=\alpha+1.

Since
R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)+1
and
X_{*}
is an imputation
of
X
for the index
1
, then according to Lemma
B.1
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)>X(1)\Rightarrow I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)=0\text{ for any }i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})=0.

According to Lemma
B.1
, we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)=1.

According to (
18
), we have
\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, which follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)\cdot(-1)=-1.

Recall that
\beta=\alpha+1
. Following (
19
), we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)=1+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}\geq 1+0-1=0,

which proves our results when the condition
(\mathrm{iii})
holds.

Suppose the condition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iv}):R\left(X(1),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1

holds. Then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)\leq 0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha=|R\left(X(1),X\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(Y(1),X\right)-R\left(X(1),Y\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)<0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\beta=|R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(1),Y\right)|=R\left(Y(1),X\right)-R\left(X(1),Y\right)+1.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\beta=\alpha+1.

Since
R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(1),X\right)-1
and
X_{*}
is an imputation
of
X
for index
1
, then according to Lemma
B.1
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)<X(1)\Rightarrow I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)=0,\text{ for any }i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)<X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)>X(1)\right)(\psi_{i}-\xi_{i})=0.

According to Lemma
B.1
, we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)=1.

According to (
18
), we have
\xi_{i}-\psi_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, which follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)(\xi_{i}-\psi_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=2}^{n}I\left(X(i)>X_{*}(1)\right)I\left(X(i)<X(1)\right)\cdot(-1)=-1.

Recall that
\beta=\alpha+1
. Following (
19
), we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)-D\left(X,Y\right)=1+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}\geq 1-1+0=0,

which proves our results when the condition
(\mathrm{iv})
holds, and completes our proof.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
u\in[n]
is an index. Let
r

be an integer between
1
to
n
. Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

of
X
for the index
u
such that
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=~r
.

Proof.

For notation ease, let us assume (after relabeling)
u=1
and
X(2),\cdots,X(n)
are ordered such that
X(2)<\cdots<X(n)
.

Suppose
r=1
. Let
X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
and let
X_{*}(1)
be a real number such that
X_{*}(1)<X(2)
. Then
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
. Meanwhile, since
X_{*}(1)<X(2)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)<X(2)<\cdots<X(n)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)<X_{*}(2)<\cdots<X_{*}(n).

Thus, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right)=1.

Therefore,
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=1=r
, which proves our result when
r=1
.

Suppose
1<r<n
. Let
X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
, and let
X_{*}(1)
be a real number such that
X(r)<X_{*}(1)<X(r+1)
. Then
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
. Meanwhile, since
X(r)<X_{*}(1)<X(r+1)
, we have

\displaystyle X(2)<\cdots<X(r)<X(1)<X(r+1)<\cdots<X(n)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{*}(2)<\cdots<X_{*}(r)<X_{*}(1)<X_{*}(r+1)<\cdots<X_{*}(n).

Thus, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right)=r.

Therefore,
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=r
, which proves our result when
1<r<n
.

Suppose
r=n
. Let
X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
and let
X_{*}(1)
be a real number such that
X_{*}(1)>X(n)
. Then,
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
. Meanwhile, since
X_{*}(1)>X(n)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)>X(n)>\cdots>X(2)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{*}(1)>X_{*}(n)>\cdots>X_{*}(2).

Thus, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(i)\leq X_{*}(1)\right)=n.

Therefore,
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=n
, which proves our result when
r=n
,
and completes our proof.

∎

Proposition B.5
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
.
If we choose

X_{*}(u)
such that
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
,
then we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.
Moreover, for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for the index
u
,
we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)
.

Proof.

Notice that there are two statements in Proposition
B.5
:

(\mathrm{i})

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
.
If
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
,
then we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

(\mathrm{ii})

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for an index
u\in[n]
.
If
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, then
for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for index
u
,
we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

Below, we first show that the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true.
Then we prove the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
is true using the statement
(\mathrm{i})
.

First, we prove the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true.
Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)=R\left(X_{*}(u),X\right).

Since
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is
an imputation of
X
for the index
u\in[n]
,
then according to Lemma
A.3
,
we have
R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)=R\left(X,X\right)
, which implies that

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X,Y\right).

Hence, we have proved the statement
(\mathrm{i})

when
R\left(X(u),X\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
.

Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)>R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, let us denote
R\left(X(u),X\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)+a
, where
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
a>0
. According to Lemma
B.2
, we can find vectors
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{a}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that each
vector is an imputation of
X
for the index
u\in[n]
and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{k}(u),X_{k}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-k,\text{ for any }k\in\{1,\cdots,a\}.

Denote
X_{0}=X
. Then, for any
k\in\{1,\cdots,a\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{k-1}(u),X_{k-1}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-(k-1)>R\left(X(u),X\right)-a=R\left(Y(u),Y\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{k}(u),X_{k}\right)=R\left(X_{k-1}(u),X_{k-1}\right)-1.

Subsequently, by applying Lemma
B.3
between each
X_{k},Y
and
X_{k-1},Y
, where
k\in\{1,\cdots,a\}
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X_{0},Y\right)\geq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\geq\cdots\geq D\left(X_{a},Y\right).

Notice that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{a}(u),X_{a}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-a=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)=R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right).

Since
X_{a}
is an imputation of
X_{*}
for the index
u
,
then according to Lemma
A.3
, we have
R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)=R\left(X_{a},X_{a}\right)
, which gives

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X_{a},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

This proves the statement
(\mathrm{i})
when
R\left(X(u),X\right)>R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
.

The case when
R\left(X(u),X\right)<R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
can be proved similarly.
Hence, we have shown the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true.

Next, we show that the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
is true using the statement
(\mathrm{i})
.
Let
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be an imputation of
X
for the index
u
.
According to Lemma
B.2
, we can find an imputation
X_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X^{\prime}
for the index
u\in[n]
,
and
R\left(X_{*}^{\prime}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
. Then, according to
the statement
(\mathrm{i})
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

Notice that
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u\in[n]
, and
R\left(X_{*}(u),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
. Thus, according to Lemma
A.3
,
we have
R\left(X_{*}^{\prime},X_{*}^{\prime}\right)=R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)
, which implies
D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

This proves the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
, and completes our proof.
∎

B.2
Proof of Lemma 2.2

This subsection proves Lemma 2.2. Specifically, we show the following result
is true.

Lemma B.6
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]

is a subset of indices. Then there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

of
X
for indices
U
such that

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X(i),i\in[n]\setminus U,\text{ and }R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\,\,\text{for all }~i\in U.

Proof.

We first consider the case when
U=[n]
.
Let
X_{*}=Y
, then
according to the definition of imputations, we have that
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an
imputation of
X
for
U=[n]
.
Since
X_{*}=Y
, we have
R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y,Y\right)
.
Hence, Lemma
B.6
is true
when
U=[n]
.

However, if
U=\emptyset
, we can let
X_{*}=X
.
Then according to the definition of imputations,

X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an
imputation of
X
for
U=\emptyset
.
Hence, Lemma
B.6
is true
when
U=\emptyset
.

Now, we only need to prove
Lemma
B.6

when
U\neq[n]
and
U\neq\emptyset
.

For any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement of
lemma
B.6
when
|U|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
holds for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

by induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We show
P{(1)}
is true.
Suppose
|U|=1
. Then
P{(1)}

is true according to Lemma
B.2
.

Induction Step:
We show the implication

P{(1)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n~-~2\}
.

Suppose
|U|=k+1
,
and denote
m=k+1
. Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)
U=\{1,\cdots,m\}
and
Y(1),\cdots,Y(m)
such that
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
.

Notice that
|U\setminus\{1\}|=|U|-1=m-1=k
.
Then since
P{(k)}
is true, there exists
X_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\quad i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\}),

(20)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\quad i\in U\setminus\{1\}.

(21)

According to Lemma
A.1
, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-1,

where the last equation holds according to (
21
).
Then, since
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
, we have

R\left(Y(1),Y\right)<\cdots<R\left(Y(m),Y\right)
.
Hence, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-1>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

(22)

Since
P{(1)}
is true, then there exists
X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\quad i\in[n]\setminus\{1\},

(23)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

(24)

According to Lemma
A.1
, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right).

Notice that according to (
23
), for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right).

Hence, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\geq R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:3}}R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:5}}R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right).

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\neq R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)
. Hence, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\geq R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)\Rightarrow R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)>R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)\Rightarrow X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(1).

According to Lemma
A.2
, we then have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:5}}R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

(25)

Now, since
P{(k)}
is true, there exists
X_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X_{2}(i),\quad i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\}),

(26)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\quad i\in U\setminus\{1\}.

(27)

Since
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
, we have
R\left(Y(1),Y\right)<\cdots<R\left(Y(m),Y\right)
. Then, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:8}}R\left(Y(i),Y\right)>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:6}}R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:7}}R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right).

Subsequently, according to Lemma
A.2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(1),X_{3}\right)=R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

Combining this result with (
27
), it follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Notice that for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:7}}X_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:4}}X_{1}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:lemma:1:eqn:1}}X(i).

Thus, we have found an imputation
X_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

of
X
for the indices
U
such that

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X(i),i\in[n]\setminus U,\text{ and }R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\,\,\text{for all }~i\in U.

This proves
P{(k+1)}
is true, and completes our proof.
∎

B.3
Proof of Lemma 2.3

This subsection proves Lemma 2.3, which
is a direct result obtained by applying Lemma
A.3
.

Lemma B.7
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of indices. Let
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
both be imputations of
X
for indices
U
. Then, if for any
i\in U
,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),

we have
D\left(X_{1},Y\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.

Proof.

Since
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are both imputations of
X
for
U
,
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X_{2}(i)=X(i).

Therefore, according to the definition of imputations,

X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for indices
U
. Since for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right).

Then, according to Lemma
A.3
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1},X_{1}\right)=R\left(X_{2},X_{2}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y\right).

This completes our proof.
∎

B.4
Proof of Theorem 2.4

This subsection proves Theorem 2.4:

Theorem B.8
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of indices. Let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for indices
U
such that
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
for any
i\in U
. Then, we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
. Moreover, for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for
U
, we have

D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)
.

Proof.

Notice that there are two statements in Theorem
B.8
:

(\mathrm{i})

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for indices
U\subset[n]
.
If
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
for any
i\in U
,
then we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

(\mathrm{ii})

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be
an imputation of
X
for indices
U\in[n]
.
If
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
, for any
i\in U
, then
for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for indices
U
,
we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

Below, we first show that the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true. Then,
we prove the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
is true
using the statement
(\mathrm{i})
.

First, we show that the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true.
Denote
m=|U|
. When
m=n
, we have

R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y,Y\right)\Rightarrow D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=0
.
According to the definition of Spearman’s footrule, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\geq 0
.
Hence, we have

D\left(X,Y\right)\geq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=0
.
Therefore, we have shown the statement
(\mathrm{i})
is true when
m=n
.

However, when
m=0
, then according to the definition of imputations,
we have
X=X_{*}
. Then, we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X,Y\right)
,
which proves the statement
(\mathrm{i})
when
m=0
.

Now, in order to prove the statement
(\mathrm{i})
, we only need to
consider cases when
1\leq m\leq n-1
.
For any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement of
statement
(\mathrm{i})
when
|U|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
holds for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

by induction on
k
.

Base Case:
Notice that
P{(1)}
is true according to Proposition
B.5
.

Induction Step:
We show the implication

P{(1)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}

For convenience, let us assume (after relabeling)
U=\{1,\cdots,m\}
, and
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
. According to Lemma
B.6
, we can find a vector
X_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\}),

(28)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\quad\text{for any }i\in U\setminus\{1\}.

(29)

That is,
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U\setminus\{1\}
such that (
29
) holds.
Notice that
|U\setminus\{1\}|=|U|-1=k+1-1=k
.
Then since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

According to Lemma
B.6
, we can find a vector
X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus\{1\},

(30)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

(31)

That is,
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for index
1
such that (
31
) holds. Subsequently, since
P{(1)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right).

According to Lemma
A.1
, for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\geq R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:3}}R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right).\end{split}

Subsequently, applying Lemma
A.1
again, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:2}}R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-1.

Since
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
and
U=\{1,\cdots,m\}
, then for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-1>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

Hence, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\geq R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:4}}R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right).

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\neq R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)\Rightarrow R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)>R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)\Rightarrow X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(1).

Subsequently, according to Lemma
A.2
,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:4}}R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

(32)

According to Lemma
B.6
, we can find a vector
X_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X_{2}(i),\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\}),

(33)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\quad\text{for any }i\in U\setminus\{1\}.

(34)

That is,
X_{3}
is an imputation of
X_{2}
for indices

U\setminus\{1\}
such that (
34
) holds.
Notice that
|U\setminus\{1\}|=|U|-1=k+1-1=k
.
Then since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{3},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right).

Since
Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
, we have
R\left(Y(i),Y\right)>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)
for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
. Hence, for any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:7}}R\left(Y(i),Y\right)>R\left(Y(1),Y\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:5}}R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(i))_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right).

Further, according to (
33
),

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right).

For any
i\in U\setminus\{1\}
, we then have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)>R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right).

By applying Lemma
A.2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(1),X_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{1\})}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:5}}R\left(Y(1),Y\right).

Combining this result with (
34
), we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Notice that for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:6}}X_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:3}}X_{1}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.2:eqn:1}}X(i).

Since
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an
n
-dimensional vector of distinct real values such that

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X(i),\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\quad\text{for any }i\in U.

Then according to Lemma
A.3
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3},X_{3}\right)=R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)\Rightarrow D\left(X_{3},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y\right).

Notice that we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X_{3},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true, this completes our proof for the statement
(\mathrm{i})
.

Next, we show that the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
is true using the statement
(\mathrm{i})
. According to Lemma
B.6
, we can find a vector

X_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that it is an imputation of
X^{\prime}
for indices

U\subset[n]
, and
R\left(X_{*}^{\prime}(i),X_{*}^{\prime}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
for any
i\in U
.
Then, according to the statement
(\mathrm{i})
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

Since
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
U\subset[n]
, and
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
for any
i\in U
.
Then, according to Lemma
A.3
, we have
R\left(X_{*}^{\prime},X_{*}^{\prime}\right)=R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)
, which means
D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

This proves the statement
(\mathrm{ii})
and completes our proof.
∎

B.5
Proof of Proposition 2.5

This subsection proves Proposition 2.5. To start, we give the
following lemma, which is a direct result following Lemma
B.6
.

Lemma B.9
.

Suppose
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U\subset[n]
is a subset of
indices. Denote
m=|U|
, and let
(\sigma(l))_{l\in U}

be a vector of any
m
distinct values in
[n]
.
Then, there exist an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for
U
such that

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=\sigma(i),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Proof.

Let
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be a vector such that

R\left(Y(i),Y\right)=\sigma(i),\text{ for any }i\in U
.
Subsequently, according to Lemma
B.6
,
there exist an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for
indices
U
such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)=\sigma(i),\text{ for any }i\in U.

This completes our proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition 2.5.

Proposition B.10
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,V\subset[n]
are disjoint subsets of
[n]
such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
and
U\cup V\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exist imputations
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
and
Y

for
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)
for any
i\in U\cup V
.

Proof.

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement of Proposition
B.10
when
|U|+|V|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
holds for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
by
induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We show
P{(1)}
holds.
If
|U|+|V|=1
, it is either

U=\emptyset,|V|=1

or
|U|=1,V=\emptyset
.
Suppose
U=\emptyset,|V|=1
. Then, let
X_{*}=X
.
According to the definition of imputations,

X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an imputation of
X
for
U=\emptyset
.
Further, according to Lemma
B.6
,
we can find an imputation

Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
Y
for indices
V
such that

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X(i),X\right)

for any
i\in V
. Notice that
X_{*}=X
. Hence, we have

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)
for any
i\in V
.
Recall that
U=\emptyset
. Therefore, we have

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)
for any
i\in U\cup V
.
Hence, we have shown
P{(1)}
is true
when
U=\emptyset,|V|=1
.
The case when
|U|=1,V=\emptyset
can be proved similarly.

Induction Step:
We show the implication

P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.

Suppose
|U|+|V|=k+1
. Then, we have either

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):|U|=0,|V|=k+1\text{ or }|U|=k+1,|V|=0,

or

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):|U|>0,|V|>0,|U|+|V|=k+1,

is true. Below, we consider the case
(\mathrm{i})
and the case
(\mathrm{ii})
separately.

First, suppose the case

(\mathrm{i}):|U|=0,|V|=k+1\text{ or }|U|=k+1,|V|=0
is true.
We consider
|U|=0,|V|=k+1
. Since
|U|=0
, we have
U=\emptyset
.
Let
X_{*}=X
. According to the definition of imputations,

X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is an imputation of
X
for
U=\emptyset
.
Further, according to Lemma
B.6
,
we can find an imputation

Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
Y
for indices
V
such that

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X(i),X\right)

for any
i\in V
. Notice that
X_{*}=X
. Hence, we have

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)
for any
i\in V
.
Recall that
U=\emptyset
. Therefore, we have

R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)
for any
i\in U\cup V
.
Hence, we prove the result
when
|U|=0,|V|=k+1
. The case when
|U|=k+1,|V|=0
can
be proved similarly. Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true under the case
(\mathrm{i})
.

Now, we consider the case

(\mathrm{ii}):|U|>0,|V|>0,|U|+|V|=k+1
. Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)

V=\{1,\cdots,t\}
,

U=\{t+1,\cdots,t+m\}
,

X(1)<\cdots<X(t)
, and

Y(t+1)<\cdots<Y(t+m)
.
Further, let us assume

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right).

However, if

R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)>R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
,
we can then switch the label for
X
and
Y
and relabel the relevant components.

Denote

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

(35)

Since
X(1)<\cdots<X(t)
and
Y(t+1)\cdots<Y(t+m)
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)<\cdots<R\left(X(t),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right),

(36)

and

\displaystyle R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)<\cdots<R\left(Y(t+m),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),

(37)

respectively.
Further, since

R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
,
we have

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U.

(38)

Since
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real values,
then
\{R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right):i\in V\}
is a set of
t
distinct values in
[n]
.
Subsequently, according to Lemma
B.9
,
there exist
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)=Y(i),\quad i\in[n]\setminus V,

(39)

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right),\quad i\in V.

(40)

That is,
Y_{1}
is an imputation of
Y
for indices
V\in[n]

such that (
40
) holds.

Recall that
V=\{1,\cdots,t\}
.
Let us assume
V\setminus\{1\}\neq\emptyset
, i.e.,
t>1
.
Then, for any
i\in V\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:3}}R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)

\displaystyle>^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:0.0}}R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:3}}R\left(Y_{1}(1),Y_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>Y_{1}(1),\text{ for any }i\in V\setminus\{1\}.

Subsequently, by applying Lemma
A.2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(1),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(1),Y_{1}\right).

Notice the above equation still holds when
V\setminus\{1\}=\emptyset
.
Recall that
\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
. Hence, we further have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(Y_{1}(1),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)&=R\left(Y_{1}(1),Y_{1}\right)\\
&=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:3}}R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=\alpha.\end{split}

(41)

Since
U
and
V
are non-empty disjoint subsets of
[n]
,
we have
U\subset[n]\setminus V
. Hence, (
39
) implies

Y_{1}(i)=Y(i)
for any
i\in U
.
Thus, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right).

Since
[n]\setminus V\subset[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})
, we can apply Lemma
A.1
and get that, for any
i\in U
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)&\geq R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)\\
&=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)\\
&\geq^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:1}}R\left(X_{1}(1),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=\alpha.\end{split}

Recall that
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector of distinct real numbers and
1\notin U=\{t+1,\ldots,t+m\}
, where
1\leq t,m
. Hence, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)\neq R\left(Y_{1}(1),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:4}}\alpha.

Therefore, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)>\alpha.

(42)

Since
|V\setminus\{1\}|=|V|-1=m-1
and
|U|=t
,
we have
|V\setminus\{1\}|+|U|=m-1+t=k
.
Since
P{(k)}
is true, then there
exist
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

(43)

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\}),

(44)

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup(V\setminus\{1\}).

(45)

That is,
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X
and
Y_{1}
for
U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
, respectively, such that (
45
) holds.
According to (
44
),
we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(1),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(1),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:4}}\alpha.

(46)

Since
V=\{1,\ldots,t\}
, we have
[n]\setminus V=\{t+1,\ldots,n\}
, and
[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})=\{1,t+1,\ldots,n\}
. Then, since
U=\{t+1,\ldots,t+m\}
, we have

U\subset[n]\setminus V
, and

U\subset[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})
.
Hence, according to (
44
), we have for any
i\in U
,

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),(Y_{1}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)>^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:5}}\alpha.

According to (
43
), we have

X_{2}(1)=X(1)
, and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=\alpha.

Hence, for any
i\in U

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)>\alpha=R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

According to Lemma
A.1
, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)\geq R\left(Y_{2}(i),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)>\alpha=R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

Subsequently, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:8}}R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)>\alpha=R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

According to Lemma
A.2
, we then have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right)&=R\left(X_{2}(1),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=\alpha.\end{split}

(47)

Since
V\subset[n]\setminus U
, (
43
) gives
X_{2}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in V
, and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right),\quad i\in V.

Recall that
V=\{1,\ldots,t\}
. Let us assume
V\setminus\{1\}\neq\emptyset
. Then, for any
i\in V\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

Since
X(t)>\ldots>X(1)
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)>R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:0}}\alpha.

According to Lemma
A.1
, for any
i\in V\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)\geq R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)>\alpha.

Then, for any
i\in V\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:8}}R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)>\alpha=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:9}}R\left(Y_{2}(1),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right).

According to Lemma
A.2
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(Y_{2}(1),Y_{2}\right)&=R\left(Y_{2}(1),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right).\end{split}

Notice that the above equation still holds when
V\setminus\{1\}=\emptyset
.

Subsequently, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(1),Y_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(1),(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:9}}\alpha=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:10}}R\left(X_{2}(1),X_{2}\right).

Combining this result with (
45
), we obtain

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V.

(48)

Recall that we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:7}}Y_{1}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:3}}Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in V,

and

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:2.5:eqn:6}}X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

Hence, we have found imputations
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

of
X
and
Y
for
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
(
48
) holds. Therefore, we have
shown
P{(k+1)}
is true. This proves our results when the
case
(\mathrm{ii})
holds and
completes our proof.

∎

B.6
Proof of Proposition 2.6

This subsection proves Proposition 2.6. First, we prove several lemmas
which will be useful for showing Proposition 2.6.

Lemma B.11
.

Suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
O\subset[n]
is
a non-empty subset of
indices such that
O\neq[n]
. Suppose there exist an index
k\in[n]\setminus O
such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right),

Then, for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in O
, if

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right).

Proof.

To start, let us denote

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right).

Suppose
k_{1},k_{2}\in O
are such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Then, we have either

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{i}):\alpha>R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

or

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{ii}):\alpha\leq R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

is true. In the following, we prove our result when
the case
(\mathrm{i})
and the case
(\mathrm{ii})
are true separately.

Suppose the case

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):\alpha>R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)

is true. Then it follows

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)>R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

and

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)>R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Applying Lemma
A.2
to
(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}

and
(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}
separately,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),

respectively. Since
R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)
, then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right),

which proves our result when the case
(\mathrm{i})
holds.

If, however, the case

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):\alpha\leq R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)

is true, we can apply Lemma
A.1
and get

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)\geq R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\geq\alpha=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right),

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)\geq R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\geq\alpha=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right).

Since
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are both vectors of distinct real values and
k\neq k_{1},k_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)\neq R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)>R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{1}(k_{1})>X_{1}(k),

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)\neq R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)>R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{2}(k_{2})>X_{2}(k).

Subsequently, according to the definition of rank and notice that

X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are vectors of distinct real values, we
can show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)+I\left(X_{1}(k_{1})>X_{1}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)+1,

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)+I\left(X_{2}(k_{2})>X_{2}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)+1.

Recall that
R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\{k\}\cup O}\right).

This proves our results when the case
(\mathrm{ii})
is true, and completes our proof.

∎

Lemma B.12
.

Suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
O_{1},O_{2}\subset[n]

are non-empty disjoint subset of indices such that
O_{1},O_{2}\neq\emptyset

and
O_{1}\cap O_{2}=\emptyset
. Suppose

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2}

Then, for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in O_{1}
, if

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right),

we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right).

Proof.

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement of Lemma
B.12
when
|O_{2}|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
is true for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
by
induction on
k
.

Base Case:
The case
P{(1)}
is shown in Lemma
B.11
.

Induction Step:
We show the implication

P{(1)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
.

Let us now consider the case when
|O_{2}|=k+1
.
Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)

O_{2}=\{1,\ldots,k+1\}
.
Then according to the definition of ranks and notice that

X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are vectors of distinct real values,
for any
i\in O_{2}
,
and
j=1,2
, we can show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right)=R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)-I\left(X_{j}(i)>X_{j}(k)\right).

Notice that for any
i\in O_{2}
,
and
j=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle I\left(X_{j}(i)>X_{j}(k)\right)=I\left(R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)>R\left(X_{j}(k),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)\right).

Hence, for any
i\in O_{2}
,
and
j=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-I\left(R\left(X_{j}(i),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)>R\left(X_{j}(k),(X_{j}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)\right).

Since

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2},

then for any
i\in O_{2}\setminus\{k\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right).

Notice that
|\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)|=k
, and we have

R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)}\right).

(49)

Next, since

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2},

we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in\left\{O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)\right\}\cup\{k\}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in\left\{O_{1}\cup\big(O_{2}\setminus\{k\}\big)\right\}\cup\{k\}}\right).

Notice that (
49
) is true,
and
P{(1)}
is true. Then, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k_{2}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\cup O_{2}}\right).

Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true. Thus, we complete our proof.

∎

Lemma B.13
.

Suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and

O\subset[n]
is a non-empty subset of indices.
Let
O_{1},O_{2}\subset[n]
be
non-empty subsets of indices such that

O_{1}\cup O_{2}=O
,
O_{1}\cap O_{2}\neq\emptyset
, and
O_{1}\setminus O_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Suppose

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2},

(50)

and
\big(R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\big)_{i\in O_{2}}
is a permutation of
\big(R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\big)_{i\in O_{2}}
.
Then, for any
k\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}
, if

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right),

(51)

we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Proof.

To start, since
R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)

for any
i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}
, and
R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)<R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)<R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)\right).

Notice that for any
i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle I\left(R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)<R\left(X_{1}(k_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)\right)=I\left(X_{1}(i)<X_{1}(k_{1})\right),

and

\displaystyle I\left(R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)<R\left(X_{2}(k_{1}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)\right)=I\left(X_{2}(i)<X_{2}(k_{1})\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(i)<X_{1}(k)\right)=\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{2}(i)<X_{2}(k)\right).

Then, since
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are both vectors of distinct real values and
k\notin O_{1}\cap O_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(i)\leq X_{1}(k)\right)=\sum_{i\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{2}(i)\leq X_{2}(k)\right).

(52)

Subsequently, according to the definition of rank, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(l)\leq X_{1}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O_{1}}I\left(X_{1}(l)\leq X_{1}(k)\right)-\sum_{l\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(l)\leq X_{1}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)-\sum_{l\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(l)\leq X_{1}(k)\right).

According to (
51
) and (
52
), we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:equivalence:lemma:2:eqn:3}}R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)-\sum_{l\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{1}(l)\leq X_{1}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:equivalence:lemma:2:eqn:4}}R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}}\right)-\sum_{l\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{2}(l)\leq X_{2}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O_{1}}I\left(X_{2}(l)\leq X_{2}(k)\right)-\sum_{l\in O_{1}\cap O_{2}}I\left(X_{2}(l)\leq X_{2}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}I\left(X_{2}(l)\leq X_{2}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right).

(53)

Since
\big(R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\big)_{i\in O_{2}}

is a permutation of

\big(R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right)\big)_{i\in O_{2}}
,
then there exist a permutation
(\sigma(i))_{i\in O_{2}}
of
O_{2}

such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(\sigma(i)),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2}.

(54)

Let
X_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
be a permutation of
X_{1}
such that

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus O_{2},

and

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X_{1}(\sigma(i)),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2}.

Notice that
O_{1}\setminus O_{2}\subset[n]\setminus O_{2}
, hence we have

(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}=(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}
.
Next, since
k\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}
, we have
X_{1}(k)=X_{3}(k)
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(k),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right).

(55)

According to (
53
), we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(k),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O_{1}\setminus O_{2}}\right).

(56)

Next, notice that
O=O_{2}\cup(O\setminus O_{2})
,
where
O\setminus O_{2}\subset[n]\setminus O_{2}
.
Then, since
X_{3}(i)=X_{1}(\sigma(i)),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2},\text{ and }X_{3}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus O_{2}
, we have

(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}

is a permutation of
(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O}
.
Hence, for any
i\in O_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(\sigma(i)),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

According to (
51
), we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right),\text{ for any }i\in O_{2}.

(57)

Notice that (
56
)
and (
57
) is true.
Then, according to Lemma
B.12
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(k),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Since
(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}

is a permutation of
(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O}
, and

X_{1}(k)=X_{3}(k)
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(k),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(k),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in O}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:equivalence:lemma:2:eqn:5.0}}R\left(X_{2}(k),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in O}\right).

This completes our proof.

∎

Lemma B.14
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let

V=\{1,\cdots,t\}
,
