, and
U,V\subset[n]
are
disjoint subsets of indices such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
.
Suppose
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively.
Then, if

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
,
we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
. Furthermore,
for any other imputations
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of

X,Y
for indices
U,V
, respectively, we have

D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof.

Notice that there are two statements in Theorem
B.25
:

(1)

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,V\subset[n]

are disjoint subsets of indices such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Then
if
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, and

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
, we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

(2)

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,V\subset[n]

are disjoint subsets of indices such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Suppose

X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, and

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
. Then for any other imputations
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of

X,Y
for indices
U,V
, respectively, we have

D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

Below, we first show that the statement
(1)
is true. Then
we prove the statement
(2)

using the statement
(1)
.

First, we show the statement
(1)
is true.
To begin with, let us consider the case when
|U|+|V|=n
.
Since
U\cap V=\emptyset
, then
|U|+|V|=n
means

U\cup V=[n]
.
Since
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
, we have

R\left(X_{*},X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Hence
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=0.
According to the definition
of Spearman’s footrule, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\geq 0=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
,
which proves the statement
(1)
when
|U|+|V|=n
.

When
|U|+|V|=0
, then according to the definition of imputations,
we have
X=X_{*}
and
Y=Y_{*}
. Hence we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=D\left(X,Y\right)
.
This proves statement
(1)
when
|U|+|V|=0
.

Then, we only need to prove statement
(1)
when
n-1\geq|U|+|V|\geq 1
.
For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement of
the statement
(1)
when
|U|+|V|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
holds for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
by
induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We show
P{(1)}
holds.
Suppose
|U|+|V|=1
. Then we have either
|U|=0,|V|=1

or
|U|=1,|V|=0
. For both cases, the statement

P{(1)}
is true according to Theorem
B.8
.

Induction Step:
We show the implication

P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
.

Suppose
|U|+|V|=k+1
. Then, we have either

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{I}):~|U|=k+1,|V|=0\text{ or }|U|=0,|V|=k+1,

or

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{II}):~|U|>0,|V|>0,|U|+|V|=k+1,

is true.

Suppose the
\text{case }(\mathrm{I}):~|U|=k+1,|V|=0\text{ or }|U|=0,|V|=k+1

is true. Then the statement
P{(k+1)}
is true according to Theorem
B.8
.

Suppose the
\text{case }(\mathrm{II}):~|U|>0,|V|>0,|U|+|V|=k+1
is true.
Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)

V=\{1,\ldots,t\}
,

U=\{t+1,\ldots,t+m\}
,

X(1)<\ldots<X(t)
, and

Y(t+1)<Y(t+2)<\ldots<Y(t+m)
.

Notice that for
R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
and
R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
, we have either

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\geq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),

or

\displaystyle R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),

is true. Let us assume
R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
is true.
However, when
R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\geq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
holds,
we can switch the labels between
X
and
Y
, and relabel the relevant components
of the data.

Let us denote

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

(113)

Then, since
\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)
,
we have either

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{i}):\alpha=R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),\text{ and }Y(1)>Y(t+1),

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{ii}):\alpha=R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),\text{ and }Y(1)<Y(t+1),

or

\displaystyle\text{case }(\mathrm{iii}):\alpha<R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),

is true.
In the following, we are going to consider the three cases separately.

Suppose the case
(\mathrm{i})
is true. In other words,

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),

(114)

and

\displaystyle Y(1)>Y(t+1).

(115)

Then, according to Proposition
B.10
,
there exist
X_{1},Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

such that

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

(116)

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\}),

(117)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)\text{ for any }i\in U\cup(V\setminus\{1\}).

(118)

In other words,
X_{1},Y_{1}
are imputations of
X
, and
Y

for indices
U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
, respectively, such that
the equation (
118
) holds.

Since
U
and
V
are disjoint such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
,
then
U\cap(V\setminus\{1\})=\emptyset
, i.e.

U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
are also disjoint.
Notice that
|U|+|V\setminus\{1\}|=m+t-1=k
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

(119)

Next, applying Proposition
B.10
again, we can find
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(U\setminus\{t+1\}),

(120)

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V,

(121)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)\text{ for any }i\in(U\setminus\{t+1\})\cup V.

(122)

In other words,
X_{2}
and
Y_{2}
are imputations of
X_{1}
,
Y_{1}

for indices
U\setminus\{t+1\}
and
V
, respectively, such that
(
122
) is true.

Notice that
U
and
V
are disjoint, i.e.
U\cap V=\emptyset
.
Hence,
(U\setminus\{t+1\})\cap V=\emptyset
.
Next, since
|U\setminus\{t+1\}|+|V|=m+t-1=k
,
we can apply
P{(k)}
and get

\displaystyle D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right).

(123)

According to Lemma
B.21
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right),\text{for any }i\in U\cup V.

Notice that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:9}}X_{1}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:4}}X(i)\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

and

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:10}}Y_{1}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:5}}Y(i)\quad\text{for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

In other words,
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that

R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right),\text{for any }i\in U\cup V
.
Since
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
, then according to Proposition
B.18
, we have

D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:12.0}}D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:6.0}}D\left(X,Y\right),

which proves
P{(k+1)}
when the case
(\mathrm{i})
holds.

Suppose the case
(\mathrm{ii})
or the case
(\mathrm{iii})
holds. In other words,
we have either

\displaystyle\alpha=R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right),\text{ and }Y(1)<Y(t+1),

(124)

or

\displaystyle\alpha<R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right).

(125)

According to the definition of rank,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)&=\sum_{i\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}I\left(Y(i)\leq Y(t+1)\right)\\
&=I\left(Y(1)\leq Y(t+1)\right)+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus V}I\left(Y(i)\leq Y(t+1)\right)\\
&=I\left(Y(1)\leq Y(t+1)\right)+R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right).\end{split}

Notice that
I\left(Y(1)\leq Y(t+1)\right)\geq 0
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right).

Next, suppose (
125
) is true. We further have

\displaystyle R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)

\displaystyle>\alpha=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:0}}R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

However, suppose (
124
) is true. Then, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)

\displaystyle=I\left(Y(1)\leq Y(t+1)\right)+R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)

\displaystyle=1+\alpha>\alpha=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:0}}R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

Hence, when the case
(\mathrm{ii})
or the case
(\mathrm{iii})
is true, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y(t+1),(Y(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)>\alpha=R\left(X(1),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

(126)

According to Proposition
B.10
,
there exist
X_{3},Y_{3}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{3}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

(127)

\displaystyle Y_{3}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\}),

(128)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{3}(i),X_{3}\right)=R\left(Y_{3}(i),Y_{3}\right)\text{ for any }i\in(V\setminus\{1\})\cup U.

(129)

That is,
X_{3}
and
Y_{3}
are imputations of
X
and
Y

for
U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
such that (
129
)
is true.

Notice that
U
and
V
are disjoint, i.e.
U\cap V=\emptyset
.
Hence,
(U\setminus\{t+1\})\cap V=\emptyset
.
Next, since
|U\setminus\{t+1\}|+|V|=m+t-1=k
,
we can apply
P{(k)}
and get

\displaystyle D\left(X_{3},Y_{3}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right).

(130)

Notice that (
126
) is true.
Then, according to Lemma
B.22
,
when
V\setminus\{1\}\neq\emptyset
, for any
i\in V\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{3}(i),(Y_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus\{1\}}\right)\geq R\left(X_{3}(1),(X_{3}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=R\left(X_{3}(1),X_{3}\right)=\alpha.

(131)

Next, according to Lemma
B.6
, there exists
Y_{4}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle Y_{4}(i)=Y_{3}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus\{1\},

(132)

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{4}(1),Y_{4}\right)=R\left(X_{3}(1),X_{3}\right).

(133)

In other words,
Y_{4}
is an imputation of
Y_{3}

of the index 1 such that
(
133
)

is true. Then,
according to Theorem
B.8
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{3},Y_{4}\right)\leq D\left(X_{3},Y_{3}\right).

(134)

According to Lemma
B.23
,
for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{4}(i),(Y_{4}(l))_{l\in\{1\}\cup([n]\setminus V)}\right)>R\left(Y_{4}(1),(Y_{4}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\})}\right)=\alpha.

(135)

Next, according to Proposition
B.10
, there exist
X_{5},Y_{5}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{5}(i)=X_{3}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

(136)

\displaystyle Y_{5}(i)=Y_{4}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\setminus\{1\}),

(137)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{5}(i),X_{5}\right)=R\left(Y_{5}(i),Y_{5}\right)\text{ for any }i\in(V\setminus\{1\})\cup U.

(138)

In other words,
X_{5}
and
Y_{5}
are imputations of
X_{3},Y_{4}

for
U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
, respectively, such that (
138
) is true.

Since
U
and
V
are disjoint,

U
and
V\setminus\{1\}
are also disjoint.
Notice that
|U|+|V\setminus\{1\}|=m+t-1=k
.
Then, according to
P{(k)}
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{5},Y_{5}\right)\leq D\left(X_{3},Y_{4}\right).

(139)

According to Lemma
B.24
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{5}(i),X_{5}\right)=R\left(Y_{5}(i),Y_{5}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V.

Combining (
136
) and (
127
), we have

\displaystyle X_{5}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:26}}X_{3}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:17}}X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

Combining (
137
), (
132
), and (
128
), we have

\displaystyle Y_{5}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:27}}Y_{4}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:22}}Y_{3}(i)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:18}}Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

In other words,
X_{5},Y_{5}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that

R\left(X_{5}(i),X_{5}\right)=R\left(Y_{5}(i),Y_{5}\right),\text{for any }i\in U\cup V
.
Since
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that

R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
, then according to Proposition
B.18
, we have

D\left(X_{5},Y_{5}\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=D\left(X_{5},Y_{5}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:28.0}}D\left(X_{3},Y_{4}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:23.0}}D\left(X_{3},Y_{3}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.7:eqn:19.0}}D\left(X,Y\right).

which proves
P{(k+1)}
when the case
(\mathrm{ii})
or the case
(\mathrm{iii})
holds.
This completes our proof for the statement
(1)
.

Next, we prove the statement
(2)
using the statement
(1)
.
According to Proposition
B.10
, we can find

X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}

are imputations of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
for indices

U
, and
V
, respectively, and

R\left(X_{*}^{\prime}(i),X_{*}^{\prime}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}^{\prime}(i),Y_{*}^{\prime}\right)
for any
i\in U\cup V
.
Then, according to the statement
(1)
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right).

Next, since
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputation of
X,Y

for
U
and
V
, respectively, then we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

\displaystyle Y_{*}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

Further, since
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}

are imputations of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
for indices

U
, and
V
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle X_{*}^{\prime}(i)=X_{*}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U,

\displaystyle Y_{*}^{\prime}(i)=Y_{*}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

In other words,
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

are also imputations of
X,Y
for indices

U
, and
V
, respectively.
Notice that for
X_{*}
and
Y_{*}
, we have
R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V
.
Also, for
X_{*}^{\prime}
and
Y_{*}^{\prime}
, we have
R\left(X_{*}^{\prime}(i),X_{*}^{\prime}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}^{\prime}(i),Y_{*}^{\prime}\right)
for any
i\in U\cup V
. Then, according to Proposition
B.19
, we have
D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=D\left(X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right).

This proves the statement
(2)
and completes our proof.
∎

B.8
Proof of Proposition 2.9

This subsection proves Proposition 2.9. That is, we show the following result is true.

Proposition B.26
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
is a non-empty subset of indices. Define the subvectors
X^{\prime}=(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}
and
Y^{\prime}=(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}
. If the following three conditions all hold,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right),\text{ for any }i\in W,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):\min(X(l))_{l\in W}>\max(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iii}):\min(Y(l))_{l\in W}>\max(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},

then we have
D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof.

To start, according to the definition of Spearman’s footrule, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in W}\big|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\big|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\big|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\big|.

According to the condition
(\mathrm{i})
, for any
i\in W
, we have
R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\big|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\big|=0,

which follows that

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\big|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\big|.

Next, according to the condition
(\mathrm{ii})
, we have
\min(X(l))_{l\in W}>\max(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}
.
Then, according to Lemma
A.2
, for any
i\in[n]\setminus W
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)=R\left(X(i),X^{\prime}\right).

Similarly, according to the condition
(\mathrm{iii})
,
we have
\min(Y(l))_{l\in W}>\max(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}
.
Then, according to Lemma
A.2
, for any
i\in[n]\setminus W
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y(i),Y\right)=R\left(Y(i),Y^{\prime}\right).

Subsequently, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\big|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\big|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\big|R\left(X(i),X^{\prime}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y^{\prime}\right)\big|=D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right).

This completes our proof.
∎

B.9
Proof of Theorem 2.10

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem 2.10.

Theorem B.27
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U,V,W\subset[n]

are pairwise disjoint subsets of indices.
Suppose
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X,Y

for indices
U\cup W
, and
V\cup W
, respectively.
If the following three conditions all hold

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U\cup V\cup W,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):\min(X_{*}(l))_{l\in W}>\max(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iii}):\min(Y_{*}(l))_{l\in W}>\max(Y_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}.

Then, we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
. Furthermore,
for any other imputations
X_{1},Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
,
V\cup W
, respectively,
we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.

Proof.

Notice that there are two statements in Theorem
B.27
:

(1)

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,V,W\subset[n]

are pairwise disjoint subsets of indices.
If
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively,
and condition
(\mathrm{i})
,
(\mathrm{ii})
and
(\mathrm{iii})
all hold,
then we have
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
.

(2)

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U,V,W\subset[n]

are pairwise disjoint subsets of indices.
If
X_{*},Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
,

Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively,
and condition
(\mathrm{i})
,
(\mathrm{ii})
and
(\mathrm{iii})
all hold,
then for any other imputations
X_{*}^{\prime},Y_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of

X,Y
for indices
U,V
, respectively, we have

D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

Below, we first show the statement

is true. Then
we prove the statement

using the statement

.

is true.

and
U\cup V=\emptyset
.

and

. Hence, we have

, which proves the statement
(1)

when
W=\emptyset
and
U\cup V=\emptyset
.

Suppose
W=\emptyset
and

. Then

are imputations of

for indices

, and

, respectively, such that

.
Then, according to Theorem
B.25
, we have

D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\leq D\left(X,Y\right)
, which proves the statement
(1)

when

.

(143)

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W,

(144)

\displaystyle\min(X_{2}(l))_{l\in W}>\max(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},

(145)

and

\displaystyle\min(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in W}>\max(Y_{2}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}.

(146)

Combining (
142
) and (
143
),
we have
X_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{2}(i)

and
Y_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{2}(i)
for any
i\in[n^{\prime}]\setminus W=[n^{\prime}]
.
Further, since
X^{\prime}_{2},Y^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n^{\prime}}
are
imputations of

X^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}
for
U
and
V
, respectively,
we have
X^{\prime}_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{1}(i)
for any
i\in[n^{\prime}]\setminus U

and
Y^{\prime}_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{1}(i)
for any
i\in[n^{\prime}]\setminus V
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{1}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n^{\prime}]\setminus U,

and

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{1}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n^{\prime}]\setminus V.

Notice that
[n^{\prime}]=[n]\setminus W
. Hence, we
have
[n^{\prime}]\setminus U=[n]\setminus(W\cup U)
,
and
[n^{\prime}]\setminus V=[n]\setminus(W\cup V)
.
Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{2}(i)=X^{\prime}_{1}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(W\cup U),

and

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{2}(i)=Y^{\prime}_{1}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(W\cup V).

In other words,
X_{2}
and
Y_{2}
are imputations of
X_{1},Y_{1}

for indices
W\cup U
and
W\cup V
, respectively.
Notice that (
144
), (
145
) and (
146
) are true. Then according to
Theorem
B.27
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\geq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right).

Since (
144
), (
145
) and (
146
) are true, then according to
Proposition
B.26
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)=D\left(X^{\prime}_{2},Y^{\prime}_{2}\right).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\geq D\left(X^{\prime}_{2},Y^{\prime}_{2}\right)=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.10:eqn:0}}D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right).

This completes proof for the statement
(2)
, and finishes our proof.

∎

Appendix C
Proof of upper bounds

This section provides results for deriving exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule
in the presence of missing data.

C.1
Proof of Proposition 2.11

This subsection proves Proposition 2.11. We start by showing
the following lemma:

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}

be imputations of
X
for an index
u\in[n]

such that
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,\text{ and }R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
. Then, if

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):~R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)

holds, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.
However, if

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):~R\left(X(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)

holds, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
.

Proof.

We prove the results for the case
(\mathrm{i})
and
(\mathrm{ii})
separately.

Suppose the case
(\mathrm{i}):~R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)

is true.
Then, we have either

R\left(X(u),X\right)=n
or

R\left(X(u),X\right)<n
.
Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)=n
. Then, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)=n=R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right).

Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
,
then according to Lemma
A.3
, we have

R\left(X_{2},X_{2}\right)=R\left(X,X\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.

Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)<n
.
Denote
R\left(X(u),X\right)=n-b
,
where
b\in\mathbb{N}
and
b>0
.
Then, according to Lemma
B.2
,
there exist
V_{1},\ldots,V_{b}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that
for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,b\}
,
V_{k}

is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
and

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k}(u),V_{k}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+k.

Define
V_{0}=X
. Then, for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,b\}
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k-1}(u),V_{k-1}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+(k-1)\geq R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k}(u_{1}),V_{k}\right)=R\left(V_{k-1}(u_{1}),V_{k-1}\right)+1.

By applying Lemma
B.3
for each pair
V_{k},Y
and
V_{k-1},Y
, where
k\in\{1,\ldots,b\}
,
we have

\displaystyle D\left(V_{0},Y\right)\leq D\left(V_{1},Y\right)\leq\ldots\leq D\left(V_{b},Y\right).

(147)

Notice that

\displaystyle R\left(V_{b}(u),V_{b}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)+b=n=R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right).

Since
V_{b}
and
X_{2}
are
both imputation of
X
for the index
u
,
then according to Lemma
A.3
,
we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{2},Y\right)=D\left(V_{b},Y\right).

According to (
147
), we further have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\geq D\left(V_{0},Y\right).

Since
V_{0}=X
, then we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.
This completes our proof when the case
(\mathrm{i})
is true.

When the case
(\mathrm{ii})
is true, the results can be proved similarly. Suppose the case
(\mathrm{ii})
is true,
then we have either
R\left(X(u),X\right)=1
,
or
R\left(X(u),X\right)>1
is true.
Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)=1
. Then,

\displaystyle R\left(X(u),X\right)=1=R\left(X_{1}(u),X\right).

Since
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
, then
according to Lemma
A.3
we have

R\left(X_{1},X_{1}\right)=R\left(X,X\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
.

Suppose
R\left(X(u),X\right)>1
. Denote
R\left(X(u_{1}),X\right)=1+a
, where
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
a>0
. Then, according to Lemma
B.2
,
there exist
V_{1},\ldots,V_{a}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
such that
for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
,
V_{k}

is an imputation of
X
for the index
u
and

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k}(u),V_{k}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-k.

Define
V_{0}=X
. Then, for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k-1}(u),V_{k-1}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-(k-1)\leq R\left(X(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(u),Y\right),

and

\displaystyle R\left(V_{k}(u),V_{k}\right)=R\left(V_{k-1}(u),V_{k-1}\right)-1.

By applying Lemma
B.3

for each pair
V_{k},Y
and
V_{k-1},Y
, where
k\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(V_{0},Y\right)\leq D\left(V_{1},Y\right)\leq\cdots\leq D\left(V_{a},Y\right).

(148)

Notice that

\displaystyle R\left(V_{a}(u),V_{a}\right)=R\left(X(u),X\right)-a=1=R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right).

Since
V_{a}
and
X_{1}
are
both imputation of
X
for the index
u
,
then according to Lemma
A.3
,
we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y\right)=D\left(V_{a},Y\right).

According to (
148
), we further
have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\geq D\left(V_{0},Y\right).

Since
V_{0}=X
, then we have
D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\geq D\left(X,Y\right)
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Proposition 2.11.

Proposition C.2
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}

be imputations of
X
for an index
u\in[n]

such that
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,\text{ and }R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
. Then,

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\},

and for any imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for index
u
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
C.1
,
if
R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, we have

D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}
.
However, if
R\left(X(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, we have

D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}
. According to Lemma
B.2
, there exist imputations
X_{3},X_{4}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X^{\prime}
for
the index
u
such that
R\left(X_{3}(u),X_{3}\right)=1
, and
R\left(X_{4}(u),X_{4}\right)=n
.

Then, according to Lemma
C.1
,
if
R\left(X^{\prime}(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{3},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{3},Y\right),D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\}
.
However, if
R\left(X^{\prime}(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y(u),Y\right)
, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{3},Y\right),D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\}.
Hence, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{3},Y\right),D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\}
.

Notice that
X_{3},X_{4}
are both imputations of
X^{\prime}
for the index

u
, and
X^{\prime}
is the imputation of
X
for index
u
. Hence, according to
the definition of imputations, we can show that

X_{3},X_{4}
are both imputation of
X
for the index
u
. Notice that

X_{1},X_{2}
are imputation of
X
for the index
u
such that

R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,\text{ and }R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
. Then, according to Lemma
A.3
, we have

R\left(X_{3},X_{3}\right)=R\left(X_{1},X_{1}\right)
,
and

R\left(X_{4},X_{4}\right)=R\left(X_{2},X_{2}\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{3},Y\right)=D\left(X_{1},Y\right)

and
D\left(X_{4},Y\right)=D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
. Notice that

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{3},Y\right),D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}.

This completes our proof.
∎

C.2
Proof of Proposition 2.13

This subsection proves Proposition 2.13. First, we make the following definition:

Definition C.3
.

Let
U\subset T\subset[n]
be a subset of indices. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U,T,n)=\{Z\in\mathcal{V}^{n}|~\text{for any}~i\in U,~Z(i)>\max_{j\in T\setminus U}Z(j)\text{ or }Z(i)<\min_{j\in T\setminus U}Z(j)\}.

Then, we provide the following lemma, which will be useful for proving
Proposition 2.13.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
U\subset[n]
be a non-empty subset of indices, and suppose
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
. Let
V\subset[n]
be
a non-empty subset of indices such that
V\cap U=\emptyset
.
Then, if
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n],n)
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for
V
, we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U\cup V,[n],n)
.

Proof.

Since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

(149)

Next, since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for indices
V
, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

Notice that
[n]\setminus(U\cup V)\subset[n]\setminus V
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\cup U)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j)=\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{1}(j),\text{ and }\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j)=\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{1}(j).

Meanwhile, since
U\cap V=\emptyset
, we have
U\subset[n]\setminus V
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Then, according to (
149
), we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

(150)

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n],n)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus V}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus V}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V.

Notice that
U\cap V=\emptyset
.
Then, we have
U\subset[n]\setminus V
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in U}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in U}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V.

According to (
150
),
we have

\displaystyle\max_{j\in U}X_{2}(j)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\text{ or }\min_{j\in U}X_{2}(j)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j).

Hence, we further have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V.

Combining this result with (
150
),
we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V\cup U.

This completes our proof.

∎

Now, we are ready to prove Proposition 2.13.

Proposition C.5
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U\subset[n]
.
Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
, and for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for indices
U
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Proof.

In order to show Proposition
C.5
, we first show the following statement is true:

\mathbb{S}
:

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U\subset[n]
.
Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Then we prove Proposition
C.5
using statement
\mathbb{S}
.

First, we show the statement
\mathbb{S}
is true.
Suppose
U=\emptyset
, then according to
the definition of
\mathcal{Z}([n],[n],n)

and imputations, we have
X_{*}=X
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X,Y\right)
.
This proves the statement
\mathbb{S}
when
U=\emptyset
.

Suppose
U=[n]
. Then, according to
the definition of
\mathcal{Z}([n],[n],n)

and imputations,
any vector
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is such that

X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}([n],[n],n)

and
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U=[n]
.
Let
X_{*}=X
, then we have
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=D\left(X,Y\right)
.
This proves the statement
\mathbb{S}
when
U=[n]
.

In the following, we only prove the statement
\mathbb{S}
when
U\neq[n]

and
U\neq\emptyset
.

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement
\mathbb{S}

when
|U|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
is true for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

by induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We show
P{(1)}
is true.
Suppose
|U|=1
. Let us denote
U=\{u\}
.

Then, let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}

be imputations of
X
for the index
u\in[n]

such that
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,\text{ and }R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
.
Since
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(u)<\min_{j\in T\setminus U}X_{1}(j).

Hence,
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.
Similarly, since
R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(u)>\max_{j\in T\setminus U}X_{2}(j).

Hence,
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.

Next, according to Proposition
C.2
, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y\right),D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\}
.
Hence, we proved that
P{(1)}
is true.

Induction Steps:
We show the implication

P{(1)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}
for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
.

Denote
m=k+1
. Without loss of generality,
let us assume (after relabeling)
U=\{1,2,\cdots,m\}
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, there exists an imputation
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U\setminus\{1\},[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U\setminus\{1\}
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
.

Since
P{(1)}
is true, there exists an imputation
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(\{1\},[n],n)
of
X_{1}
for the index

\{1\}
such that
D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.

Then, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
. Further, according to Lemma
C.4
,
we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.

Next, since
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U\setminus\{1\}
,
we have
X_{1}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
. Further, since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for the index
\{1\}
, we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
. Hence, we have
X_{2}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
. In other words,
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
,

Thus,
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.
Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true.
This completes our proof for the statement
\mathbb{S}
.

Next, we prove Proposition
C.5
using the Statement
\mathbb{S}
.
Let us denote

\displaystyle S=\{D\left(V,Y\right):V\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)\text{ is an imputation of }X\text{ for indices }U\}.

Then, the carnality of
S
is finite and there exist

X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=\max S
.

We now show that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
According to statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputation

X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
. Then, according to the definition of

S
and
X_{*}
, we have
D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
According to statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputation

X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X^{\prime}
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.
Since
X^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
,
then
X_{2}
is also an imputation of
X
for indices
U
.
Then, according to the definition of
X_{*}
, we have

D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
. Overall, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
This completes our proof.

∎

C.3
Proof of Lemma 2.14

This section proves Lemma 2.14. First, we prove the following result:

Lemma C.6
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
assume
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(n)
.
Let
X_{1}
be a permutation of
X

such that
X_{1}(1)>\ldots>X_{1}(n)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{1}(i)-Y(i)|.

(151)

Proof.

Let
P{(k)}
be the statement of Lemma
C.6
when
n=k
.
We prove that
P{(k)}
is true for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\geq 2

by induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We show
P{(2)}
is true.
Suppose
n=2
. Then, we have
X=(X(1),X(2))
.
If
X(1)>X(2)
, then
X_{1}=(X(1),X(2))
.
Hence, we have
X=X_{1}
and then (
151
) holds.

Thus, it is sufficient to prove (
151
)
when
X(1)<X(2)
. Suppose
X(1)<X(2)
.
Then, we have
X_{1}=(X(2),X(1))
. Therefore, the right-hand side of (
151
) is such that

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|=|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|.

(152)

Since
Y(1)<Y(2)
, and
X(1)<X(2)
, then there are
following 6 cases of the order between
Y(1),Y(2),X(1)
, and
X(2)
:

\displaystyle(1):

\displaystyle Y(1)<Y(2)\leq X(1)<X(2),

\displaystyle(2):

\displaystyle Y(1)\leq X(1)\leq Y(2)\leq X(2),

\displaystyle(3):

\displaystyle X(1)\leq Y(1)<Y(2)\leq X(2),

\displaystyle(4):

\displaystyle Y(1)\leq X(1)<X(2)\leq Y(2),

\displaystyle(5):

\displaystyle X(1)\leq Y(1)\leq X(2)\leq Y(2),

\displaystyle(6):

\displaystyle X(1)<X(2)\leq Y(1)<Y(2).

In the following, we are going to consider the 6 cases separately.

Suppose the case
(1):Y(1)<Y(2)\leq X(1)<X(2)
is true. Then, the left-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|=X(1)-Y(1)+X(2)-Y(2).

The right-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(2)-Y(1)+X(1)-Y(2)

\displaystyle=X(1)-Y(1)+X(2)-Y(2).

Then, the right-hand side of (
151
) minus the left-hand side of (
151
) equals to

\displaystyle(X(1)-Y(1)+X(2)-Y(2))-(X(1)-Y(1)+X(2)-Y(2))=0.

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when the case (1) is true.

Next, suppose the case
(2):Y(1)\leq X(1)\leq Y(2)\leq X(2)
is true. Then, the left-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(1)-Y(1)+X(2)-Y(2)

\displaystyle=(X(2)-Y(1))+(X(1)-Y(2)).

The right-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(2)-Y(1)+Y(2)-X(1)

\displaystyle=(X(2)-Y(1))+(Y(2)-X(1)).

Subsequently, the right-hand side of (
151
) minus the left-hand side of (
151
) equals to

\displaystyle(X(2)-Y(1))+(Y(2)-X(1))-(X(2)-Y(1))-(X(1)-Y(2))

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-X(1))-(X(1)-Y(2))=2Y(2)-2X(1)\geq 0.

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when the case (2) is true.

Next, suppose the case
(3):X(1)\leq Y(1)<Y(2)\leq X(2)
is true. Then, the left-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=Y(1)-X(1)+X(2)-Y(2)

\displaystyle=(X(2)-X(1))+(Y(1)-Y(2)).

The right-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(2)-Y(1)+Y(2)-X(1)

\displaystyle=(X(2)-X(1))+(Y(2)-Y(1)).

Subsequently, the right-hand side of (
151
) minus the left-hand side of (
151
) equals to

\displaystyle(X(2)-X(1))+(Y(2)-Y(1))-(X(2)-X(1))-(Y(1)-Y(2))

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-Y(1))-(Y(1)-Y(2))=2Y(2)-2Y(1)>0.

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when the case (3) is true.

Next, suppose the case
(4):Y(1)\leq X(1)<X(2)\leq Y(2)
is true. Then, the left-hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(1)-Y(1)+Y(2)-X(2)

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-Y(1))+(X(1)-X(2)).

The right hand side of (
151
) is,

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(2)-Y(1)+Y(2)-X(1)

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-Y(1))+(X(2)-X(1)).

Subsequently, the right hand side of (
151
) minus the left hand side of (
151
) equals

\displaystyle(Y(2)-Y(1))+(X(2)-X(1))-(Y(2)-Y(1))-(X(1)-X(2))

\displaystyle=(X(2)-X(1))-(X(1)-X(2))=2X(2)-2X(1)>0.

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when the case (4) is true.

Next, suppose the case
(5):X(1)\leq Y(1)\leq X(2)\leq Y(2)
is true. Then, the left hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=Y(1)-X(1)+Y(2)-X(2)

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-X(1))+(Y(1)-X(2)).

The right hand side of (
151
) is,

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=X(2)-Y(1)+Y(2)-X(1)

\displaystyle=(Y(2)-X(1))+(X(2)-Y(1)).

Subsequently, the right hand side of (
151
) minus the left hand side of (
151
) equals to

\displaystyle(Y(2)-X(1))+(X(2)-Y(1))-(Y(2)-X(1))-(Y(1)-X(2))

\displaystyle=(X(2)-Y(1))-(Y(1)-X(2))=2X(2)-2Y(1)\geq 0.

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when the case (5) is true.

Next, suppose the case
(6):X(1)<X(2)\leq Y(1)<Y(2)
is true. Then, the left hand side of (
151
) is

\displaystyle|X(1)-Y(1)|+|X(2)-Y(2)|=Y(1)-X(1)+Y(2)-X(2).

The right hand side of (
151
) is,

\displaystyle|X_{1}(1)-Y(1)|+|X_{1}(2)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:2}}|X(2)-Y(1)|+|X(1)-Y(2)|

\displaystyle=Y(1)-X(2)+Y(2)-X(1)

\displaystyle=Y(1)-X(1)+Y(2)-X(2).

Subsequently, the right hand side of (
151
) minus the left hand side of (
151
) equals to

\displaystyle(Y(1)-X(1)+Y(2)-X(2))-(Y(1)-X(1)+Y(2)-X(2))=0

Hence, we have shown (
151
) is true when case (6) is true.
This completes our proof for
P{(2)}
.

Induction Step:
We show the implication
P{(2)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}

for any integer
k\geq 2
.

Let
X_{2}
be a permutation of
X

such that
X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(n-1)

and
X_{2}(n)=X(n)
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|X(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|X_{2}(i)-Y(i)|.

Since
X_{2}(n)=X(n)
, we have

\displaystyle|X(n)-Y(n)|=|X_{2}(n)-Y(n)|.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{2}(i)-Y(i)|.

(153)

In the following, we are going to show
P{(k+1)}
is true
when
X(n)<\min\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}

and
X(n)>\min\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}
,
separately.

Suppose
X(n)<\min\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}
.
Then, since
X_{2}(1),\ldots,X_{2}(n-1)
is
a permutation of
\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}
,
we have

\displaystyle\min\{X(1),\cdots,X(n-1)\}=\min\{X_{2}(1),\cdots,X_{2}(n-1)\}.

Next, since
X_{2}(n)=X(n)
, then we further have

X_{2}(n)=X(n)<\min\{X(1),\cdots,X(n-1)\}=\min\{X_{2}(1),\cdots,X_{2}(n-1)\}
.
Since
X_{2}
is a permutation of
X

such that
X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(n-1)
,
and
X_{2}(n)<\min\{X_{2}(1),\cdots,X_{2}(n-1)\}
,
we have
X_{2}
is a permutation of
X
such that

X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(n)
.

Since
X_{1}
is a permutation of
X

such that
X_{1}(1)>\ldots>X_{1}(n)
,
then we have
X_{2}=X_{1}
.
According to (
153
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{1}(i)-Y(i)|.

Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
when
X(n)<\text{min}\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}
.

However, suppose
X(n)>\min\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}
.
Then, since
X_{2}(1),\cdots,X_{2}(n-1)
is a
permutation of
\{X(1),\cdots,X(n-1)\}
,
we have

\displaystyle\min\{X(1),\cdots,X(n-1)\}=\min\{X_{2}(1),\cdots,X_{2}(n-1)\}.

Since
X_{2}(n)=X(n)
, then we further have

X_{2}(n)=X(n)>\min\{X(1),\ldots,X(n-1)\}=\min\{X_{2}(1),\ldots,X_{2}(n-1)\}
.
Further, since
X_{2}(1)>\ldots X_{2}(n-1)
,
we have
X_{2}(n)>X_{2}(n-1)=\min\{X_{2}(1),\ldots,X_{2}(n-1)\}
,
and

\displaystyle X_{2}(n-1)=\min\{X_{2}(1),\ldots,X_{2}(n)\}.

(154)

Next, let
X_{3}
be a permutation of
X_{2}

such that
X_{3}(i)=X_{2}(i)

for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
,
X_{3}(n-1)=X_{2}(n)
,
and
X_{3}(n)=X_{2}(n-1)
.
Then, since
X_{2}(n)>X_{2}(n-1)
,
we have
X_{3}(n-1)>X_{3}(n)
.

Since
P{(2)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle|X_{2}(n-1)-Y(n-1)|+|X_{2}(n)-Y(n)|\leq|X_{3}(n-1)-Y(n-1)|+|X_{3}(n)-Y(n)|.

Since
X_{3}(i)=X_{2}(i)
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}|X_{2}(i)-Y(i)|=\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}|X_{3}(i)-Y(i)|.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{2}(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{3}(i)-Y(i)|.

(155)

Next, let
X_{4}
be a permutation of
X_{3}

such that
X_{4}(1)>\ldots>X_{4}(n-1)

and
X_{4}(n)=X_{3}(n)
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|X_{3}(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}|X_{4}(i)-Y(i)|.

Since
X_{4}(n)=X_{3}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle|X_{3}(n)-Y(n)|=|X_{4}(n)-Y(n)|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{3}(i)-Y(i)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{4}(i)-Y(i)|.

(156)

According to (
154
), we have

X_{2}(n-1)=\min\{X_{2}(1),\ldots,X_{2}(n)\}.

Since
X_{3}
is a permutation of
X_{2}
and

X_{3}(n)=X_{2}(n-1)
, then we have

X_{3}(n)=\min\{X_{3}(1),\ldots,X_{3}(n)\}.

Next, since
X_{4}
is a permutation of
X_{3}
,
and
X_{4}(n)=X_{3}(n)
,
we have
X_{4}(n)=\min\{X_{4}(1),\ldots,X_{4}(n)\}.

Notice that
X_{4}(1)>\ldots>X_{4}(n-1)
.
Hence, we have
X_{4}(1)>\ldots>X_{4}(n)
.

Since
X_{4}
is a permutation of
X_{3}
,

X_{3}
is a permutation of
X_{2}
,

X_{2}
is a permutation of
X
,
we have
X_{4}
is a permutation of
X
.
Then, since
X_{1}
is a permutation of
X
,
and
X_{1}(1)>\ldots>X_{1}(n)
,
we have
X_{4}=X_{1}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{1}(i)-Y(i)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{4}(i)-Y(i)|

\displaystyle\geq^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:5}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{3}(i)-Y(i)|

\displaystyle\geq^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{2}(i)-Y(i)|

\displaystyle\geq^{\eqref{supp:lemma:2.14:1:eqn:3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X(i)-Y(i)|.

Thus, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true.
This completes our proof.

∎

Now, we show Lemma 2.14 is true:

Lemma C.7
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m\leq n
assume
U=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and

Y(1)<\cdots<Y(m)
.
Let
X^{\prime}
be an imputation of
X
for indices
U
. Suppose

X_{*}
is also an imputation of
X
for indices
U
,
and
(X_{*}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X^{\prime}(i))_{i\in U}
such that

X_{*}(1)>\cdots>X_{*}(m)
.
Then, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Proof.

Since
X^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
, we have

X^{\prime}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
.
Similarly, since
X_{*}^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
, we have
X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
. Hence, we have
X_{*}(i)=X^{\prime}(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
.

Further, since
(X_{*}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X^{\prime}(i))_{i\in U}
, then we have

X_{*}
is a permutation of
X^{\prime}
.
Hence, according to the definition of rank, for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X^{\prime}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right).

Since
X_{*}(i)=X^{\prime}(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we further have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X^{\prime}(j)\leq X^{\prime}(i)\right)=R\left(X^{\prime}(i),X^{\prime}\right),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\left|R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\left|R\left(X^{\prime}(i),X^{\prime}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

(157)

Next, since
X_{*}
is a permutation of
X^{\prime}
,
then according to the definition of rank, for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X^{\prime}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right).

Since
(X_{*}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of
(X^{\prime}(i))_{i\in U}
, then there exist
a permutation
(\sigma(l))_{l\in U}

of
U
such that

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)=X^{\prime}(\sigma(i)),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X^{\prime}(j)\leq X^{\prime}(\sigma(i))\right)=R\left(X^{\prime}(\sigma(i)),X^{\prime}\right),\text{ for any }i\in U.

In other words,
\left(R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)\right)_{i\in U}

is a permutation of
\left(R\left(X^{\prime}(i),X^{\prime}\right)\right)_{i\in U}
. Since
X_{*}(1)>\ldots>X_{*}(m)
, we have

R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)>\ldots>R\left(X_{*}(m),X_{*}\right)
.

Then, according to Lemma
C.6
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|R\left(X^{\prime}(i),X^{\prime}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

Combining this result with (
157
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|R\left(X^{\prime}(i),X^{\prime}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y\right)\geq D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right).

This completes our proof.

∎

C.4
Proof of Theorem 2.16

This subsection proves Theorem 2.6. First, we make the following definition:

Definition C.8
.

Suppose
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and let
U\subset T\subset[n]
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(Y;U,T,n)=\left\{Z\in\mathcal{Z}(U,T,n)|~\text{for any}~i,j\in U,~Z(i)>Z(j)~\text{if}~Y(i)<Y(j)\right\}.

Then we prove the following lemma, which will be useful for proving Theorem 2.6.

Lemma C.9
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and for
m\leq n
assume
U=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m)
. Suppose
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
is an imputation of
X
for
U
.
If
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U

such that
(X_{2}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X_{1}(i))_{i\in U}
, and

X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(m)
,
then we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.

Proof.

Since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
,
we have
X_{2}(i)=X(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
.
Then, since
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
,
we have
X_{1}(i)=X(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
.
Hence, we have
X_{1}(i)=X_{2}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
.
Thus, we have

\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j)=\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j)

and

\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j)=\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j)
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Since
(X_{2}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X_{1}(i))_{i\in U}
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Thus, we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.
Notice that
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m)
and
X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(m)
. Hence, we have
\text{for any}~i,j\in U,~X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(j)~\text{if}~Y(i)<Y(j)
. Therefore, we have shown
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem 2.6.

Theorem C.10
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and let
U\subset[n]
.
Then, there exists an imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
, and for any other imputation
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X
for indices
U
,

D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)

U=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m)
.
Let us denote

\displaystyle S=\{D\left(V,Y\right):V\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)\text{ is an imputation of }X\text{ for indices }U\}.

The carnality of
S
is finite and there exist
imputation
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

of
X
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)=\max S
.

We first show that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
According to Proposition
C.5
,
there exist imputation
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)

of
X
for indices
U
such that

D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
.

Next, let
X_{2}
be an imputation of
X
for indices
U

such that
(X_{2}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X_{1}(i))_{i\in U}
, and

X_{2}(1)>\ldots>X_{2}(m)
.
Then, according to Lemma
C.7
, we have

D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y\right)
.

According to Lemma
C.9
, we have

X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.
Then, according to the definition of
X_{*}
, we have

D\left(X_{2},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
. Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
According to Proposition
C.5
,
there exist imputation
X_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
of
X^{\prime}
for indices
U
such that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{3},Y\right)
.

Since
X^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U
,
then
X_{3}
is also an imputation of
X
for indices
U
.
Let
X_{4}
be a imputation of
X
for indices
U
such that

(X_{4}(i))_{i\in U}
is a permutation of

(X_{3}(i))_{i\in U}
, and

X_{4}(1)>\ldots>X_{4}(m)
.
Then, according to Lemma
C.7
, we have

D\left(X_{3},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{4},Y\right)
.

According to Lemma
C.9
, we have

X_{4}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.
Then, according to the definition of
X_{*}
, we have

D\left(X_{4},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
. Hence, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
.
This completes our proof.

∎

C.5
Proof of Theorem 2.17

This section proves Theorem 2.17. We start by proving the following lemma:

Lemma C.11
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U,V\subset[n]

are subsets of indices such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Suppose
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
. If
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X_{1};V,[n],n)
, we have
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
.

Proof.

Since
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X_{1};V,[n],n)
, then
we have
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n],n)
,
and for any
i,j\in V
, we have
Y_{i}>Y_{j}

if
X_{1}(i)<X_{1}(j)
.

Next, since
X_{1}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
, then
we have
X_{1}(i)=X(i)
for any

i\in[n]\setminus U
.
Then, since
U\cap V=\emptyset
, we have

V\subset[n]\setminus U
. Hence, we have

X_{1}(i)=X(i)
for any

i\in V
.

Thus, for any
i,j\in V
, we have

Y_{i}>Y_{j}
if
X(i)<X(j)
.
Therefore, we have shown
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Now, we show Theorem 2.17 is true.

Theorem C.12
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
U,V\subset[n]

are disjoint subsets of indices such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Then, there exist imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))
of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Furthermore, consider any imputation
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Proof.

To start, let us denote

\displaystyle S=\{D\left(V,W\right)

\displaystyle:(V,W)\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

\displaystyle\text{ are imputations of }X,Y\text{ for indices }U\text{ and }V,\text{respectively}\}.

Then, the carnality of
S
is finite and there exist
imputations

\displaystyle(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=\max S
.

We first show that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. According to Theorem
C.10
,
there exist an This completes our proof.imputation
X_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

of
X
for indices
U
such that

D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y\right)
.
Applying Theorem
C.10
again,
there exist an imputation
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X_{1};V,[n],n)

of
Y
for indices
V
such that

D\left(X_{1},Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
. Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.

According to Lemma
C.11
,
we have
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
.
Notice that
X_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
. Then, according to the definition of
(X_{*},Y_{*})
,
we have
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
According to Theorem
C.10
,
there exist an imputation

X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n],n)

of
X^{\prime}
for indices
U
such that

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y^{\prime}\right)
.

Since
X^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
,
we have
X_{2}
is also an imputation of
X
for
U
.
Meanwhile, notice that
Y^{\prime}
is an imputation of
Y

for indices
V
, then according to Lemma
C.11
,
we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
.

Applying Theorem
C.10
again,
there exist an imputation
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X_{2};V,[n],n)

of
Y^{\prime}
for indices
V
such that

D\left(X_{2},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{2}\right)
.

Since
Y^{\prime}
is an imputation of
Y
for
V
, then according to
the definition of imputations, we can show that

Y_{2}
is also an imputation of
Y
for
V
.
Meanwhile, notice that
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X

for indices
U
, then according to Lemma
C.11
,
we have
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
.

Then, according to the definition of
(X_{*},Y_{*})
,
we have
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
This completes our proof.

∎

C.6
Proof of Proposition 2.18

Now, we prove Proposition 2.18 is true.

Proposition C.13
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
u\in[n]
be an index. Suppose
X_{1},Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for the index
u
such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{1}(u),Y_{1}\right)=n,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{2}(u),Y_{2}\right)=1.

Then,
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
.

Proof.

To start, since
R\left(Y_{2}(u),Y_{2}\right)=1
, then
we have
R\left(X(u),X\right)\geq R\left(Y_{2}(u),Y_{2}\right)
.
Hence, according to Lemma
C.1
,
we have
D\left(X,Y_{2}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.

Similarly, since
R\left(Y_{1}(u),Y_{1}\right)=n
, we have

R\left(X(u),X\right)\leq R\left(Y_{1}(u),Y_{1}\right)
.
Hence, according to Lemma
C.1
,
we have
D\left(X,Y_{1}\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.

Hence, we have
\max\{D\left(X,Y_{1}\right),D\left(X,Y_{2}\right)\}\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
.
According to Proposition
C.2
,
we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X,Y_{1}\right),D\left(X,Y_{2}\right)\}
.
Thus, we further have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
.
This completes our proof.

∎

C.7
Proof of Theorem 2.20

This subsection proves Theorem 2.20. First, we make the following definition:

Definition C.14
.

Let
W\subset[n]
be a subset of indices.
Define

\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(W,n)=\left\{(Z_{1},Z_{2})\in(\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n),\mathcal{V}^{n})|R\left(Z_{1}(i),Z_{1}\right)+R\left(Z_{2}(i),Z_{2}\right)=n+1,~i\in W\right\}.

Then, we prove the following two lemmas, which will be useful for proving Theorem 2.20.

Lemma C.15
.

Suppose
X_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and for
2\leq m<n
,
denote
W^{\prime}=\{n-m+1,\ldots,n-1\}
as a subset of indices. Suppose
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
,
and define

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{1}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\},\text{ and }W^{\prime}_{2}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\}.

Suppose
X_{2}
is an
imputation of
X_{1}
for the index
n
. Then if
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
,
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
. However, if
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
,
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+1
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

First, we show that if
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
,
then we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
.

According to the definition of rank, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X_{2}(j)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}I\left(X_{2}(j)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)+I\left(X_{2}(n)\leq X_{2}(i)\right).

Since
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
, we have

I\left(X_{2}(n)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)=0
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Then, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}I\left(X_{2}(j)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)=R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right).

(158)

Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for the index
n
, then we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{n\}=[n-1]
.
Hence,
(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}
is
a permutation of
(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}
.
Meanwhile, since
W^{\prime}=\{n-m+1,\ldots,n-1\}\subset[n-1]
,
we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Then, it follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

(159)

Using the definition of ranks and notice that
X_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

is a vector of distinct real values, we can show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-I\left(X_{1}(n)<X_{1}(i)\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

If
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, then according to the definition of
W^{\prime}_{1}
, we have
X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
.
Since
n\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
, we have

X_{1}(i)>X_{1}(n)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right)-1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

(160)

However, if
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
, according to the definition of
W^{\prime}_{2}
, we have
X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
.
Since
n\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
, we have

X_{1}(i)<X_{1}(n)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(161)

Thus, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:1}}R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:2}}R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:3}}R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1.

Similarly, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:1}}R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:2}}R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:3.0}}R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right).

Hence, we have shown that when
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
,
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
.

Similarly, we can show that if
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
,
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+1
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
.

According to the definition of rank, for any
i\in W
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X_{2}(j)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}I\left(X_{2}(j)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)+I\left(X_{2}(n)\leq X_{2}(i)\right).

Since
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
, we have

I\left(X_{2}(n)\leq X_{2}(i)\right)=1
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\left(X_{2}(u_{1}),X_{2}\right)&=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}I\left(X_{2}(i)\leq X_{2}(u_{1})\right)+1\\
&=R\left(X_{2}(u_{1}),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)+1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.\end{split}

(162)

Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for the index
n
, then we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{n\}=[n-1]
.
Hence,
(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}
is
a permutation of
(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}
.
Meanwhile, since
W^{\prime}=\{n-m+1,\ldots,n-1\}\subset[n-1]
,
we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Then, it follows that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

(163)

Using the definition of ranks and notice that
X_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

is a vector of distinct real values, we can show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-I\left(X_{1}(n)<X_{1}(i)\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

If
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, then according to the definition of
W^{\prime}_{1}
, we have
X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
.
Since
n\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
, we have

X_{1}(i)>X_{1}(n)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right)-1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

(164)

However, if
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
, then according to the definition of
W^{\prime}_{2}
, we have
X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
.
Since
n\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
, we have

X_{1}(i)<X_{1}(n)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(u_{1}),X_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(165)

Thus, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:4}}R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)+1

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:5}}R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)+1

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:6}}R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right).

Similarly, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:4}}R\left(X_{2}(i),(X_{2}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)+1

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:5}}R\left(X_{1}(i),(X_{1}(l))_{l\in[n-1]}\right)+1

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.20:lemma:0:eqn:6}}R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+1.

Hence, we have shown that if
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
,
we have
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)
,
for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
, and
R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+1
, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Lemma C.16
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and for
2\leq m<n
assume
W=\{n-m+1,\ldots,n\}
,
and
W^{\prime}=\{n-m+1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Suppose
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
are imputations
of
X
and
Y
for indices
W^{\prime}
, and
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(\{n\},n)

are imputations of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
for the index
n
. Then
X_{2},Y_{2}

are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
such that

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

Proof.

First, we show that
X_{2},Y_{2}

are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
.
Since
X_{1},Y_{1}
are imputations
of
X
and
Y
for indices
W^{\prime}
, then according to
the definitions of imputations, we have

X_{1}(i)=X(i)

and

Y_{1}(i)=Y(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
.

Since
W^{\prime}\subset W
, we have
[n]\setminus W\subset[n]\setminus W^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\text{ and }Y_{1}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus W.

Since
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
for the index
n
,
then we have

X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)

and

Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{n\}
.
Since
\{n\}\subset W
, then we have
[n]\setminus W\subset[n]\setminus\{n\}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ and }Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus W

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X(i),\text{ and }Y_{2}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus W.

This proves that
X_{2},Y_{2}

are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
.

Now, we show that
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
. To start, since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
,
then we have
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
.
Similarly, since
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(\{n\},n)
,
we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(\{n\},[n],n)
.
Then, according to Lemma
C.4
,
we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
.
Hence, we have

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n),\mathcal{V}^{n})
.
Then, in order to show that
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
,
it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=n+1,\text{ for any }i\in W.

Since
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(\{n\},n)
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(n),Y_{2}\right)=n+1.

(166)

Then, we only need to show that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=n+1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

(167)

Below, we prove (
167
) is true.

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(\{n\},[n],n)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(n)>\max_{l\in[n-1]}X_{2}(l),~\text{or}~X_{2}(n)<\min_{l\in[n-1]}X_{2}(l).

Hence, we have either

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n,\text{ or }R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1.

Combining with (
166
), we
have either

\displaystyle\text{ case }(1):~R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n,\text{ and }R\left(Y_{2}(n),Y_{2}\right)=1,

or

\displaystyle\text{ case }(2):~R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1,\text{ and }R\left(Y_{2}(n),Y_{2}\right)=n,

is true.

Suppose case
(1)
is true. In other words,

~R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
,
and
R\left(Y_{2}(n),Y_{2}\right)=1
.

Since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l),~\text{or}~X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

Let us define

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{1}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\},

and

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{2}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\}.

Then, we have
W^{\prime}_{1}\cup W^{\prime}_{2}=W^{\prime}
.
Denote
n^{\prime}_{1}=|W^{\prime}_{1}|
and
n^{\prime}_{2}=|W^{\prime}_{2}|
.

Next, since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)

such that
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
. Then according to Lemma
C.15
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)-1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1},

(168)

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(169)

For any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
,
since
X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
,
then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{n,\ldots,n-n^{\prime}_{1}+1\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

Further, since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1

for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}_{1}\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}Y_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

(170)

Similarly, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
,
since
X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
,
then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}_{2}\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

Further, since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1

for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{n,\ldots,n-n^{\prime}_{2}+1\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}Y_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(171)

Combining (
170
) and (
171
),
we have
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
. Notice that
Y_{2}
is an imputation of
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)

for the index
n
such that
R\left(Y_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
. Then, according to Lemma
C.15
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)+1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1},

(172)

and

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(173)

Combining (
168
), (
169
),
(
172
) and (
172
), for any

i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right).

Since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then for any

i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1.

Hence, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1.

This proves (
167
) when the case
(1)
is true.

When the case
(2)
is true, (
167
) can be proved similarly.
Suppose the case
(2)
is true. In other words, suppose

~R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
,
and
R\left(Y_{2}(n),Y_{2}\right)=n
.

Since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l),~\text{or}~X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}.

Let us define

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{1}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\},

and

\displaystyle W^{\prime}_{2}=\left\{i\in W^{\prime}:X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)\right\}.

Then, we have
W^{\prime}_{1}\cup W^{\prime}_{2}=W^{\prime}
.
Denote
n^{\prime}_{1}=|W^{\prime}_{1}|
and
n^{\prime}_{2}=|W^{\prime}_{2}|
.

Next, since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)

such that
R\left(X_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=1
. Then according to Lemma
C.15
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1},

(174)

and

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(175)

For any
i\in W^{\prime}_{1}
,
since
X_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
,
then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{n,\ldots,n-n^{\prime}_{1}+1\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

Further, since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1

for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}_{1}\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}Y_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1}.

(176)

Similarly, for any
i\in W^{\prime}_{2}
,
since
X_{1}(i)<\min_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}X_{1}(l)
,
then we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}_{2}\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

Further, since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then
R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1

for any
i\in W^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)\in\{n,\ldots,n-n^{\prime}_{2}+1\},\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>\max_{l\in[n]\setminus W^{\prime}}Y_{1}(l),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(177)

Combining (
176
) and (
177
),
we have
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)
. Notice that
Y_{2}
is an imputation of
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W^{\prime},[n],n)

such that
R\left(Y_{2}(n),X_{2}\right)=n
. Then, according to Lemma
C.15
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right),\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{1},

(178)

and

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)=R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)-1,\text{ for any }i\in W^{\prime}_{2}.

(179)

Combining (
174
), (
175
),
(
178
) and (
179
), for any

i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(i),Y_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right).

Since
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)
, then for any

i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(i),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1.

Hence, for any
i\in W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(i),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(i),Y_{1}\right)=n+1.

This proves (
167
) when the case
(2)
is true.
Hence, we complete our proof.

∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem 2.20:

Theorem C.17
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
.
Then there exist imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Furthermore, consider any other imputations
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
W
.
Then
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Proof.

To prove Theorem
C.17
, we first prove the following
statement

\mathbb{S}

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and
W\subset[n]
.
Then there exist imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Then we will prove Theorem
C.17
using statement
\mathbb{S}
.

First, we prove the statement
\mathbb{S}
is true.
Suppose
W=\emptyset
. Then according to the definition of imputations,
we have
X_{*}=X
and
Y_{*}=Y
. Hence, we have

D\left(X,Y\right)=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. This proves the statement
\mathbb{S}

when
W=\emptyset
.

Suppose
W=[n]
. Then, let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

be a vector such that
X_{*}(1)>\ldots>X_{*}(n)
.
In other words,
X_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=n+1-i,\text{ for any }i\in[n].

Let
Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}

be a vector such that
Y_{*}(1)<\ldots<Y_{*}(n)
.
In other words,
Y_{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
is a vector such that

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=i,\text{ for any }i\in[n].

Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)+R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)=n+1,\text{ for any }i\in[n].

Thus,
X_{*},Y_{*}
are imputations of
X,Y
for
W=[n]
,
and
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

Next, let us assume (after relabeling)
Y_{1}<\ldots<Y_{n}
.
Then according to the definition of Spearman’s footrule, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X(i),X\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X(i),X\right)-i|.

Since
Y_{*}(1)<\ldots<Y_{*}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}(i),Y_{*}\right)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)-i|.

Notice that
(R\left(X(i),X\right))_{i\in[n]}

and
(R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right))_{i\in[n]}
are
both permutations of
[n]
. Then

(R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right))_{i\in[n]}
is a permutation
of
(R\left(X(i),X\right))_{i\in[n]}
.
Since
X_{*}(1)>\ldots>X_{*}(n)
, then we have

R\left(X_{*}(1),X_{*}\right)>\ldots>R\left(X_{*}(n),X_{*}\right)
.
Hence, according to Lemma
C.6
, we have
