\displaystyle D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)\geq D\left(X,Y\right).

This proves the statement
\mathbb{S}
is true when
W=[n]
.

In the following, we prove the statement
\mathbb{S}

is true when
W\neq\emptyset

and
W\neq[n]
.

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P{(k)}
be the statement
\mathbb{S}

when
|W|=k
.
We prove
P{(k)}
is true for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

by induction on
k
.

Base Case:
We prove
P{(1)}
is true.
When
|W|=1
, let us denote
W=u
.
Suppose
X_{1},Y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and

X_{2},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are imputations
of
X
and
Y
for the index
u
such that

\displaystyle R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{1}(u),Y_{1}\right)=n,

\displaystyle R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n,~\text{and}~R\left(Y_{2}(u),Y_{2}\right)=1.

Then, according to Proposition
C.13
, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
.

Since
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)=1
, we have
X_{1}(u)<X_{1}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
.
Notice that
W=\{u\}
. Then, we have
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
.
Since
R\left(X_{1}(u),X_{1}\right)+R\left(Y_{1}(u),Y_{1}\right)=n+1
,
we have
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

Similarly, since
R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)=n
, we have
X_{2}(u)>X_{2}(i)

for any
i\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
.
Notice that
W=\{u\}
. Then, we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
.
Since
R\left(X_{2}(u),X_{2}\right)+R\left(Y_{2}(u),Y_{2}\right)=n+1
,
we have
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

Notice that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq\max\{D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right),D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\}
,

(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
and
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.
Hence, we have shown
P{(1)}
is true.

Induction Step:
We show the implication
P{(1)},P{(k)}\Rightarrow P{(k+1)}

for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
.

Denote
m=k+1
.
Without loss of generality, let us assume
(after relabeling)
W=\{n-m+1,\cdots,n\}
.
Define
W^{\prime}=\{n-m+1,\cdots,n-1\}
.

Notice that
|W^{\prime}|=|W|-1=k
.
Then, since
P{(k)}
is true, there exist
imputations
(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W^{\prime},n)

of
X
and
Y
for indices
W^{\prime}

such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.

Next, since
P{(1)}
is true, then there exist imputations
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(\{n\},n)
of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
for the index
n

such that
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.

Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.
According to Lemma
C.16
,

X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W

such that

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.
Hence, we have shown
P{(k+1)}
is true.
This completes our proof for the statement
\mathbb{S}
.

Next, we prove Theorem
C.17
is true using the statement
\mathbb{S}
.
let us denote

\displaystyle S=\{D\left(V_{1},V_{2}\right):(V_{1},V_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)\text{ are imputations of }X,Y\text{ for indices }W\}.

Then, the carnality of
S
is finite and there exist
imputations
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X,Y
for indices
W
such that
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)=\max S
.

We now show that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
According to statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputations

(X_{1},Y_{1})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X,Y
for indices
W

such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.
According to the definition of
S
, we have

D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\in S
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq\max S=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
According to the statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputation

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
for indices
W

such that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.
Since
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
,
then
X_{2}
,
Y_{2}
are imputations of
X,Y
for indices
W
.
Then, according to the definition of
S
, we have
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\in S
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq\max S=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus, we have
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
This completes our proof.
∎

C.8
Proof of Theorem 2.21

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem 2.21.

Theorem C.18
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U
,
V
,
W\subset[n]
be pairwise disjoint subsets.
Then, there exist
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of imputations
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
. Furthermore,
consider any imputation
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, we have

D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Proof.

To prove Theorem
C.18
, we first prove the following
statement

\mathbb{S}

: Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and let
U
,
V
,
W\subset[n]
be pairwise disjoint subsets.
Then, there exist
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of imputations
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Then we will prove Theorem
C.18
using the statement
\mathbb{S}
.

According to Theorem
C.12
, there exist imputations

\displaystyle(X_{1},Y_{1})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.

Further, according to Theorem
C.17
, there exist imputations
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
for indices
W
such that
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)
.

Then, in order to prove our result, it is sufficient to show that
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W

and
V\cup W
, respectively, and are such that

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

First, we show that
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W

and
V\cup W
, respectively. Since
X_{1},Y_{1}
are imputations
of
X,Y
for indices
U
and
V
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus U,\text{ and }Y_{1}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus V

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(U\cup W),\text{ and }Y_{1}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(V\cup W).

Further, since
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
for indices
W
,
we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus W,\text{ and }Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus W

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(U\cup W),\text{ and }Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(V\cup W).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(U\cup W),\text{ and }Y_{2}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }[n]\setminus(V\cup W).

In other words,
X_{2},Y_{2}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W

and
V\cup W
, respectively.

Next, we show
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.
Since
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
, we only need to show that

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))
.

We now show
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
Since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, then according
to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }X_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Since
X_{2}
is an imputation of
X_{1}
for
W
, we have
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus W
.
Since
[n]\setminus(U\cup W)\subset[n]\setminus W
,
we have
\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{1}(j)=\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{2}(j)

and

\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{1}(j)=\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{2}(j)
.
Meanwhile, since
U\in[n]\setminus W
, we have

X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
for any
i\in U
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{2}(j),\text{ or }X_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W)}X_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Thus, we have shown
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n]\setminus W,n)
.

Next, since
X_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, then according
to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(i)>X_{1}(j),\text{ if }Y(i)<Y(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in U.

Since
X_{2}(i)=X_{1}(i)
for any
i\in U
,
then we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(j),\text{ if }Y(i)<Y(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in U.

Hence, we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n)
.

Similarly, we can show that

Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
Since
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
, then according
to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus V}Y_{1}(j),\text{ or }Y_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus V}Y_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}X_{1}(j),\text{ or }Y_{1}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{1}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V.

Since
Y_{2}
is an imputation of
Y_{1}
for
W
, we have
Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus W
.
Since
[n]\setminus(V\cup W)\subset[n]\setminus W
,
we have
\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{1}(j)=\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{2}(j)

and

\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{1}(j)=\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{2}(j)
.
Meanwhile, since
V\in[n]\setminus W
, we have

Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i)
for any
i\in V
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{2}(j),\text{ or }Y_{2}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W)}Y_{2}(j),\text{ for any }i\in V.

Thus, we have shown
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.

Next, since
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
, then according
to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{1}(i)>Y_{1}(j),\text{ if }X(i)<X(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in V.

Since
Y_{2}(i)=Y_{1}(i)
for any
i\in V
,
then we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)>Y_{2}(j),\text{ if }X(i)<X(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in V.

Hence, we have
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
This completes our proof for statement
\mathbb{S}
.

Next, we prove Theorem
C.18
is true using statement
\mathbb{S}
.
Let us denote

\displaystyle S=\{D\left(Z_{1},Z_{2}\right):(Z_{1},Z_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

\displaystyle\text{ are imputations of }X,Y\text{ for indices }U\cup W\text{ and }V\cup W,\text{respectively}\}.

The carnality of
S
is finite and there exist
imputations

\displaystyle(X_{*},Y_{*})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively, such
that
\max S=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

We now show that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
According to the statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputations

\displaystyle(X_{1},Y_{1})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

of
X,Y
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively
such that
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)
.
According to the definition of
S
, we have

D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\in S
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{1},Y_{1}\right)\leq\max S=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus, we have
D\left(X,Y\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.

Next, we show that
D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
According to statement
\mathbb{S}
, there exist imputations

\displaystyle(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X^{\prime};V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
for indices
U\cup W
and
V\cup W
, respectively
such that

\displaystyle D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right).

(180)

Below, we show that
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\in S
. In other words,
we show that
X_{2}
,
Y_{2}
are
imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
,
and
V\cup W
, respectively such that

(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
.

Since
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
,
and
V\cup W
, respectively, then
X_{2}
,
Y_{2}
are
also imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
U\cup W
,
and
V\cup W
, respectively.

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
then according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(j),\text{ if }Y^{\prime}(i)<Y^{\prime}(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in U.

Since
Y^{\prime}
is an imputation of
Y
for indices
V\cup W
, we have

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(V\cup W).

Then, since
U\subset[n]\setminus(V\cup W)
, we have

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}(i)=Y(i),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(i)>X_{2}(j),\text{ if }Y(i)<Y(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in U.

Since
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
then according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y^{\prime};U,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
we have
X_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
Hence, we have

X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n)
.

Similarly, we can show
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
Since
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X^{\prime};V,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
then according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(X^{\prime};V,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)>Y_{2}(j),\text{ if }X^{\prime}(i)<X^{\prime}(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in V.

Since
X^{\prime}
is an imputation of
X
for indices
U\cup W
, we have

\displaystyle X^{\prime}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup W).

Then, since
V\subset\subset[n]\setminus(U\cup W)
, we have

\displaystyle X^{\prime}(i)=X(i),\text{ for any }i\in V.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}(i)>Y_{2}(j),\text{ if }X(i)<X(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in V.

Since
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X^{\prime};V,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
then according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(X^{\prime};V,[n]\setminus W,n)
,
we have
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.
Hence, we have

Y_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n)
.

Next, since
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
,
we have
(X_{2},Y_{2})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n]\setminus W,n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n]\setminus W,n))\cap\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

Then, according to the definition of
S
, we have
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\in S
.
Hence, we have
D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq\max S=D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle D\left(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\right)\leq^{\eqref{supp:theorem:2.21:eqn:1}}D\left(X_{2},Y_{2}\right)\leq D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right).

This completes our proof.

∎

Appendix D
Proof of bounds of
p
-values

Definition D.1
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
F_{n}
is the cumulative distribution function of a normal distribution with mean, variance equal to
n^{2}/3
and
2n^{3}/45
, respectively. Then when
n

is sufficiently large, the
p
-value
of Spearman’s footrule is defined as

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))=2{\min}\{F_{n}(D(X,Y)),1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\}.

(181)

Proposition D.2
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
are partially observed. Assume
n
is sufficiently large. Let
D_{\min}
and
D_{\max}
be the minimum and maximum possible values of Spearman’s footrule between
X
and
Y
. Denote
p_{1}=p(D_{\min})
and
p_{2}=p(D_{\max})
, where
p(\cdot)
is defined in (
181
). Define
p_{\min}=\min\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
, and

\displaystyle p_{\max}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\max\{p_{1},p_{2}\},&\text{ if }(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then, the
p
-value of
D\left(X,Y\right)
is bounded such that
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]
.

Proof.

According to the definition
p(\cdot)
of
p
-values in (
181
),
we have

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))=2\min\{F_{n}(D(X,Y)),1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\},

\displaystyle p_{1}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\min}),1-F_{n}(D_{\min})\},

and

\displaystyle p_{2}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\max}),1-F_{n}(D_{\max})\},

separately.

In the following, we prove
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]

when
F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq 1/2
and
F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2
separately.

Suppose
F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq 1/2
.
We first show that
p_{\min}\leq p(D(X,Y)).

Since
F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq 1/2
, then we have
1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\geq 1/2
. Hence,

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))=2\min\{F_{n}(D(X,Y)),1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\}=2F_{n}(D(X,Y)).

(182)

Then, since
D_{\min}\leq D(X,Y)
, we have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq 1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 1-F_{n}(D_{\min})\geq 1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{1}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\min}),1-F_{n}(D_{\min})\}=2F_{n}(D_{\min}).

Since
F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq F_{n}(D(X,Y))
, we then have

\displaystyle p_{1}=2F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq 2F_{n}(D(X,Y))=p(D(X,Y))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{\min}=\min\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\leq p(D(X,Y)).

Now, we show
p(D(X,Y))\leq p_{\max}.

If
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)<0
, then we have
p_{\max}=1
. According
to the definition of
p
-values in (
181
), we have

p(D(X,Y))\leq 1\leq p_{\max}=1
.

However, if
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0
, then
since
F_{n}
is the cumulative distribution function of a normal
distribution with mean equal to
n^{2}/3
, we have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq 1/2\Rightarrow D_{\min}\leq n^{2}/3

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)\leq 0.

Since
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0
, then we have
D_{\max}-n^{2}/3\leq 0
.
Hence,

\displaystyle D_{\min}\leq D(X,Y)\leq D_{\max}\leq n^{2}/3

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq F_{n}(D_{\max})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow^{\eqref{supp:proposition:3.1:eqn:0}}

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})\leq p(D(X,Y))/2\leq F_{n}(D_{\max}).

(183)

Notice that since
D_{\max}-n^{2}/3\leq 0
, we also have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\max})\leq 1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 1-F_{n}(D_{\max})\geq 1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{2}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\max}),1-F_{n}(D_{\max})\}=2F_{n}(D_{\max}).

Combining this result with (
183
), we have

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))\leq p_{2}\leq\max\{p_{1},p_{2}\}=p_{\max}.

Hence, we have shown
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]

when
F_{n}(D(X,Y))\leq 1/2
.

Similarly, we can show
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]

when
F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2
.
Suppose
F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2
.
We first show that
p_{\min}\leq p(D(X,Y)).

Since
F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2
, then we have
1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))<1/2
. Hence,

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))=2\min\{F_{n}(D(X,Y)),1-F_{n}(D(X,Y))\}=2-2F_{n}(D(X,Y)).

(184)

Then, since
D_{\max}\geq D(X,Y)
, we have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\max})\geq F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 1-F_{n}(D_{\max})<1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{2}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\max}),1-F_{n}(D_{\max})\}=2-2F_{n}(D_{\max}).

Since
F_{n}(D_{\max})\geq F_{n}(D(X,Y))
, then we have

\displaystyle p_{2}=2-2F_{n}(D_{\max})\leq 2-2F_{n}(D(X,Y))=p(D(X,Y))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{\min}=\min\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\leq p(D(X,Y)).

Now, we show
p(D(X,Y))\leq p_{\max}.

If
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)<0
, then we have
p_{\max}=1
. According
to the definition of
p
-values in (
181
), we have

p(D(X,Y))\leq 1\leq p_{\max}=1
.

However, if
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0
, then
since
F_{n}
is the cumulative distribution function of a normal
distribution with mean equal to
n^{2}/3
, we have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})>1/2\Rightarrow D_{\min}>n^{2}/3

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)>0.

Since
(D_{\min}-n^{2}/3)(D_{\max}-n^{2}/3)\geq 0
, then we have
D_{\max}-n^{2}/3\geq 0
.
Hence,

\displaystyle D_{\max}\geq D(X,Y)\geq D_{\min}>n^{2}/3

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\max})\geq F_{n}(D(X,Y))\geq F_{n}(D_{\min})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow^{\eqref{supp:proposition:3.1:eqn:0}}

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\max})\geq 1-p(D(X,Y))/2\geq F_{n}(D_{\min})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))\leq 2-2F_{n}(D_{\min}).

(185)

Notice that since
D_{\min}-n^{2}/3>0
, we also have

\displaystyle F_{n}(D_{\min})>1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 1-F_{n}(D_{\min})<1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{1}=2\min\{F_{n}(D_{\min}),1-F_{n}(D_{\min})\}=2-2F_{n}(D_{\min}).

Combining this result with (
185
), we have

\displaystyle p(D(X,Y))\leq p_{1}\leq\max\{p_{1},p_{2}\}=p_{\max}.

Hence, we have shown
p(D(X,Y))\in[p_{\min},p_{\max}]

when
F_{n}(D(X,Y))>1/2
.
This completes our proof.
∎

Appendix E
Efficient algorithms for calculating exact upper bounds

This section gives efficient algorithms for calculating exact upper bounds
of Spearman’s footrule under Missing Case I, Missing Case II, Missing
Case III and General Missing Case.

E.1
Missing Case I

This subsection provides an efficient algorithm for calculating exact upper bounds
of Spearman’s footrule under Missing Case I. To start, we prove the
following lemma:

Lemma E.1
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1}\in[n]
,
let
U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{m_{1}}\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Suppose

Y(u_{1})<\ldots<Y(u_{m_{1}})
, and assume

X\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

is a vector such that
\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)<\min(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
, where
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
.
Then, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n-i+1,&\text{ if }r=0,\\
I\left(u_{i}\leq u_{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(u_{i}>u_{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1),&\text{ if }m_{1}>r>0,\\
m_{1}-i+1,&\text{ if }r=m_{1}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

To start, since
X\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
, then
according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
we have

\displaystyle X(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j),\text{ or }X(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

(186)

Since
Y(u_{1})<\ldots<Y(u_{m_{1}})
, then according to
the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
we also have

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{1}),X\right)>\ldots>R\left(X(u_{m_{1}}),X\right).

(187)

Below, we prove our result when
r=0
,
m_{1}>r>0
and
r=m_{1}
,
separately.

Suppose
r=0
, then according to (
186
),
we have

\displaystyle X(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j),\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)\in\{n,\ldots,n-m_{1}+1\},\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow^{\eqref{supp:proposition:alg:1:lemma:0:eqn:2}}

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=n-i+1~\text{for any}~i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}.

This proves our result when
r=0
.

Similarly, suppose
m_{1}>r>0
, then according to (
186
),
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j)\right)=r,\text{ and }\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j)\right)=m_{1}-r

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)\in\{1,\ldots,r\}\cup\{n,\ldots,n-m_{1}+r+1\},\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow^{\eqref{supp:proposition:alg:1:lemma:0:eqn:2}}

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=n-i+1~\text{for any }~i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}-r\},

\displaystyle\text{ and }R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=m_{1}-i+1~\text{for any }~i\in\{m_{1}-r+1,\ldots,m_{1}\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=I\left(u_{i}\leq u_{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(u_{i}>u_{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1).

This proves our result when
m_{1}>r>0
.

Finally, suppose
r=m_{1}
, then according to (
186
),
we have

\displaystyle X(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X(j),\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)\in\{1,\ldots,r\},\text{ for any }i\in U

\displaystyle\Rightarrow^{\eqref{supp:proposition:alg:1:lemma:0:eqn:2}}

\displaystyle R\left(X(u_{i}),X\right)=m_{1}-i+1~\text{for any}~i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}.

This proves our result when
r=m_{1}
and completes our proof. ∎

Then, we prove the following lemma:

Lemma E.2
.

Suppose
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, and
U\subset[n]

is a subset of indices. Let
X_{*}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
be an imputation
of
X
for indices
U
such that
\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}(i)<\min(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r,\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

Proof.

According to the definition of
\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
,
we have

\displaystyle X_{*}(i)>\max_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{*}(j),\text{ or }X_{*}(i)<\min_{j\in[n]\setminus U}X_{*}(j),\text{ for any }i\in U.

Then, since
\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}(i)<\min(X_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
, we have

r
components of
X_{*}
with indices
U
smaller than
any components of
X_{*}
with indices
[n]\setminus U
,
and other components of
X_{*}
with indices
U
larger than
any components of
X_{*}
with indices
[n]\setminus U
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in U}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)=r,\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

(188)

Next, according to the definition of rank, for any

i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in U}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)+\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=^{\eqref{supp:proposition:alg:1:lemma:2:eqn:1}}r+\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(X_{*}(j)\leq X_{*}(i)\right).

Since
X_{*}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
, we have

X_{*}(i)=X(i)
for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(X(j)\leq X(i)\right)=\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(X(j)\leq X(i)\right),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

Then, for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}(i),X_{*}\right)

\displaystyle=r+\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(X(j)\leq X(i)\right)

\displaystyle=r+R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

This completes our proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove the first main results for Missing Case I:

Proposition E.3
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1}\in[n]
,
let
U=\{1,\ldots,{m_{1}}\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Suppose

Y({1})<\ldots<Y({m_{1}})
, and
let
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

be an imputation of
X
for indices in
U
such that

\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
.
For any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, denote
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
,
and for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, denote
q^{(r)}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
. Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)=\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|.

Proof.

To start, according to the definition of Spearman’s footrule,
we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}({i}),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

In order to prove our result, it is then sufficient to show the following two equations hold:

\displaystyle(1):

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}({i}),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|,

\displaystyle\text{and }(2):

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|.

Below, we show equations (1) and (2) are true separately.

First, we show that equation (1) is true.

Since
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
and
Y({1})<\ldots<Y({m_{1}})
, then
according to Lemma
E.1
,
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, and

r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}({i}),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n-i+1,&\text{ if }r=0,\\
q^{(r)}_{i},&\text{ if }m_{1}>r>0,\\
m_{1}-i+1,&\text{ if }r=m_{1},\end{array}\right.

where
q^{(r)}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
.
Notice that when
r=0
and
r=m_{1}
,

R\left(X({i}),X\right)=q^{(r)}_{i}

is still true for any

i=1,\ldots,m_{1}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({i}),X\right)=q^{(r)}_{i},\text{ for any }i\in U,r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}.

Thus, for any
i\in U,r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}({i}),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|=\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|.

This proves equation (1).

Next, we show that equation (2) is true.

Since
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
then according to the definition of
\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
we also have
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.
Notice that
X_{*}^{(r)}
is an imputation of
X
for
U
, and
\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
. Then, according to Lemma
E.2
, for any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)=r+R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right),\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

(189)

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|.

This proves equation (2), and completes our proof.
∎

Before showing the second main result for Missing Case I,
we prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma E.4
.

Suppose
X=(X(u_{1}),\ldots,X(u_{n}))
is a vector of integers,
where
U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\}
is a set of indices such that
u_{1}<\ldots<u_{n}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)+1|-\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)|=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-n.

Proof.

To start, for any
i\in U
, if
X(i)\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|X(i)+1|-|X(i)|

\displaystyle=1.

If, however
X(i)<0
, then since

X
is a vector of integers, we
have
X(i)+1\leq 0
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle|X(i)+1|-|X(i)|

\displaystyle=-1.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)+1|-\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}\left(|X(i)+1|-|X(i)|\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)<0\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-\left(n-\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-n.

This completes our proof. ∎

Lemma E.5
.

Suppose
U=\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, and

n\in\mathbb{N}
is a number such that

n>m_{1}
.
For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
,
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, define

\displaystyle q^{(r)}_{{i}}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1).

Suppose
Y({1}),\ldots,Y({{m_{1}}})
are real numbers.
Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|+\left|r+1-Y({m_{1}-r})\right|-\left|n-m_{1}+r+1-Y({m_{1}-r})\right|.

Proof.

We show our result for
r=0
,
m_{1}-1>r>0
,
and
r=m_{1}-1
separately.

When
r=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}|n-i+1-Y({i})|,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}-1}|n-i+1-Y({i})|+|1-Y({i})|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|\\
&=|1-Y({m_{1}})|-|n-m_{1}+1-Y({m_{1}})|\\
&=\left|r+1-Y({m_{1}-r})\right|-\left|n-m_{1}+r+1-Y({m_{1}-r})\right|.\end{split}

This completes our proof when
r=0
.

Similarly, when
m_{1}-1>r>0
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}-r}|n-i+1-{Y({i})}|+\sum_{i=m_{1}-r+1}^{m_{1}}|m_{1}-i+1-{Y({i})}|,

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}-r-1}|n-i+1-{Y({i})}|+\sum_{i=m_{1}-r}^{m_{1}}|m_{1}-i+1-{Y({i})}|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|\\
&=|m_{1}-(m_{1}-r)+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}|-|n-(m_{1}-r)+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}|\\
&=\left|r+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}\right|-\left|n-m_{1}+r+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}\right|.\end{split}

This completes our proof when
m_{1}-1>r>0
.

Similarly, when
r=m_{1}-1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=|n-1+1-{Y({1})}|+\sum_{i=2}^{m_{1}}|m_{1}-i+1-{Y({i})}|,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}|m_{1}-i+1-{Y({i})}|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|+\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-Y(i)\right|\\
&=|m_{1}-1+1-{Y({1})}|-|n-1+1-{Y({1})}|\\
&=\left|r+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}\right|-\left|n-m_{1}+r+1-{Y({m_{1}-r})}\right|.\end{split}

This completes our proof when
r=m_{1}-1
.
Hence, we have shown our result.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove the second main result for Missing Case I.

Proposition E.6
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1}\in[n]
,
let
U=\{{1},\ldots,{m_{1}}\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Suppose

Y({1})<\ldots<Y({m_{1}})
, and
let
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

be an imputation of
X
for indices in
U
such that

\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r
.
For any
i\in[n]\setminus U
, denote
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
,
and for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, denote
s_{r}=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(d_{i}\leq r\right)
. Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y)

\displaystyle=D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)+2s_{r}-n+m_{1}+C^{(r)},

where
C^{(r)}=|r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|-|n-m_{1}+r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|
.

Proof.

To start, according to Proposition
E.3
,
we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)=\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|,

and

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y)=\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r+1-d_{i}\right|,

where
q^{(r)}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
for
any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
.
Then, in order to prove our result, it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r+1-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|=2s_{r}-n+m_{1},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=|r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|-|n-m_{1}+r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|.

According to Lemma
E.4
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r+1-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}\left|r-d_{i}\right|=2\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(r-d_{i}\geq 0\right)-(n-m_{1})=2s_{r}-n+m_{1}.

Next, notice that
q^{(r)}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
.
Then according to Lemma
E.5
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r+1)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|q^{(r)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=|r+1-Y(m_{1}-r)|-|n-m_{1}+r+1-Y(m_{1}-r)|

\displaystyle=C^{(r)}.

This completes our proof.
∎

Algorithm 4

An efficient algorithm for computing cumulative counts.

1:

A sequence of integers
Z=(Z(1),\ldots,Z(n))
, two integer number
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
.

2:

s_{r}=\sum_{i\in[n]}I\left(Z(i)\leq r\right)
for any
r\in\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{2}\}
.

3:

Initialize
s_{m_{1}}=0
. If
m_{2}>m_{1}
, initialize
t_{i}=0
for any
i\in\{m_{1}+1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
.

4:

for

i=1,\ldots,n

do

5:

if

Z(i)\leq m_{1}

then

6:

s_{0}=s_{0}+1
.

7:

end

if

8:

if

m_{1}<Z(i)\leq m_{2}

then

9:

t_{Z(i)}=t_{Z(i)}+1
.

10:

end

if

11:

end

for

12:

If
m_{2}>m_{1}
, compute
s_{r+1}=s_{r}+t_{r+1}
for any
r\in\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{2}-1\}
.

13:

Return
s_{r}
for any
r\in\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{2}\}
.

Remark 5
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
4
is analyzed as follows.
Each step between line
5
and line
10

takes constant time
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and since the loop runs
n
times,
the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(n)
.
The computation complexity for line
12

is
\mathcal{O}(m_{2}-m_{1})
.
Therefore, the overall computational complexity for Algorithm
4
is
\mathcal{O}(n+m_{2}-m_{1})
.

Algorithm 5

An efficient algorithm for computing exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule under Missing Case I.

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where either
X
or
Y
is fully observed,
while the other is partially observed.

2:

Maximum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

If
X
is fully observed while
Y
is partially observed, then change the label of
X
and
Y
.

4:

Denote
m_{1}
as the number of missing components in
X
.
If
m_{1}=n
, return
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|i-(n-i+1)|
.

5:

Rank all observed components in
X
and
Y
.

6:

Relabel
X
and
Y
such that
(X(1),\ldots,X(m_{1}))
are unobserved and
Y(1)<\ldots<Y(m_{1})
.

7:

Let
U=\{1,2,\dots,m_{1}\}.
For
i\in[n]\setminus U
, let
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),Y\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

8:

Run Algorithm
4
computing
s_{r}=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}I\left(d_{i}\leq r\right)
for any
r=\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
.

9:

For any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, compute

\displaystyle C^{(r)}=|r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|-|n-m_{1}+r+1-R\left(Y(m_{1}-r),Y\right)|.

10:

Initialize
D_{0}
=
\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|(n-i+1)-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]}\right)\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus U}|d_{i}|
.

11:

For
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, compute
D_{r+1}=D_{r}+2s_{r}-n+m_{1}+C^{(r)}
.

12:

Return
\max\{D_{0},\ldots,D_{m_{1}}\}
.

Remark 6
.

A few comments of Algorithm
5
are made below:

In Algorithm
5
, the initialization
D_{0}
computed in
line
10
equals to
D(X_{*}^{(0)},Y)
defined in
Proposition
E.3
.
Spearman’s footrule
D_{r+1}
and
D_{r}

equal to
D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y)

and
D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y)
, respectively,
and are updated according to Proposition
E.6
.

By computing
\{D_{0},\ldots,D_{m_{1}}\}
, Algorithm
5

finds all possible Spearman’s footrule
values
D\left(X_{*},Y\right)
, where
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)

is imputation of
X
for indices
U
. See Remark 3 in the main
paper for explanation.
Hence, according to Theorem
C.10
,
the algorithm guarantees to find the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule
between
X
and
Y
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
5
is analyzed as follows. Ranking and relabeling
all observed components in
X
and
Y
in line
5
requires
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps.
Using these rankings, in line
7
computing each
d_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and so overall
line
7
is
\mathcal{O}(n-m_{1})
.
According to Remark
5
, the computational
complexity of running Algorithm
4
in line
8

is
\mathcal{O}(n+m_{1})=\mathcal{O}(n)
. In line
9
, each iteration of the for loop is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and since the loop runs
m_{1}
times, the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
. Line
10
and
11

takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
steps, respectively.
Therefore, the overall computational complexity for Algorithm
5
is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
.

E.2
Missing Case II

This subsection provides an efficient algorithm for computing
exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule under missing case II,
where
X
and
Y
might be
partially observed, but for any pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
where
i\in[n]
, at least one value is observed.
First, we show the following result:

Proposition E.7
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1},m_{2}\in[n]
such
that
m_{1}+m_{2}<n
,
let
V=\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
and
U=\{m_{2}+1,\ldots,m_{2}+m_{1}\}
be subsets of indices. Suppose

X(1)<\ldots<X(m_{2})
, and

Y(m_{2}+1)<\ldots<Y(m_{2}+m_{1})
.
Let
(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

be imputations of
X
,
Y
for indices
U
and
V
,
respectively, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r_{1},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}I\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i)<\min(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=r_{2}.

For any
i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)
, denote

d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
.Then, for any
r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, and
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|.\end{split}

where
q^{(r_{2})}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{2}-i+1)
,
and
p^{(r_{1})}_{i}=I\left(i\leq m_{1}-r_{1}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(i>m_{1}-r_{1}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
.

Proof.

To start, according to the definition of Spearman’s footrule,
we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|.

Hence, in order to prove our results, it is then sufficient to show
the following three equations all hold:

\displaystyle(1):

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|,

\displaystyle(2):

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|,

\displaystyle\text{and }(3):

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|.

Below, we show equations (1), (2) and (3) are true seperately.

We first show equation (1) is true.
Since
X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
then according to the definition of

\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
we have
X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\in\mathcal{Z}(U,[n],n)
.
Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r_{1},

and
X_{*}^{(r_{1})}
is an imputation
of
X
for
U
, then according to Lemma
E.2
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1},\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus U.

(190)

Next, since
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
,
and
X(1)<\ldots<X(m_{2})
,
then according to Lemma
E.1
,
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n-i+1,&\text{ if }r_{2}=0,\\
q^{(r_{2})}_{i},&\text{ if }m_{2}>r_{2}>0,\\
m_{2}-i+1,&\text{ if }r=m_{2},\end{array}\right.

where
q^{(r_{2})}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{2}-i+1)
.
Notice that when
r_{2}=0
and
r_{2}=m_{2}
,

R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)=q^{(r_{2})}_{i}
is still true
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)=q^{(r_{2})}_{i},\text{ for any }i\in V,r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}.

(191)

Then, combining (
190
), and (
191
),
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|,

which proves equation (1).

Similarly, we can show equation (2) is true.
Since
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
,
then according to the definition of

\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
,
we have
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n],n)
.
Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}I\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i)<\min(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=r_{2},

and
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}
is an imputation
of
Y
for
V
, then according to Lemma
E.2
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)+r_{2},\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

(192)

Next, since
X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,
and
Y(m_{2}+1)<\ldots<Y(m_{2}+m_{1})
,
then according to Lemma
E.1
,
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(m_{2}+i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n-i+1,&\text{ if }r_{1}=0,\\
p^{(r_{1})}_{i},&\text{ if }m_{1}>r_{1}>0,\\
m_{1}-i+1,&\text{ if }r=m_{1},\end{array}\right.

where
p^{(r_{1})}_{i}=I\left(m_{2}+{i}\leq m_{2}+{m_{1}-r_{1}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(m_{2}+{i}>m_{2}+{m_{1}-r_{1}}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)=I\left({i}\leq{m_{1}-r_{1}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{1}-r_{1}}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
.
Notice that when
r_{1}=0
and
r_{1}=m_{1}
,

R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(m_{2}+i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)=p^{(r_{1})}_{i}
is still true
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(m_{2}+i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)=p^{(r_{1})}_{i},\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\},r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}.

In other words, for any
i\in\{m_{2}+1,\ldots,m_{2}+m_{1}\},r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)=p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}.

(193)

Then, combining (
192
), and (
193
),
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|,

which proves equation (2).

We now show equation (3) is true.
Since
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
,
then according to the definition of

\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
,
we have
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\in\mathcal{Z}(V,[n],n)
.
Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}I\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i)<\min(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=r_{2},

and
Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}
is an imputation
of
Y
for
V
, then according to Lemma
E.2
,
we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)+r_{2},\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus V.

(194)

Then, combining (
190
) and (
194
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|R\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i),X_{*}^{(r_{1})}\right)-R\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i),Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|.

This proves equation (3), and completes our proof.
∎

Next, we show the following result.

Proposition E.8
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
m_{1},m_{2}\in[n]
such
that
m_{1}+m_{2}<n
,
let
V=\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
and
U=\{m_{2}+1,\ldots,m_{2}+m_{1}\}
be subsets of indices. Suppose

X(1)<\ldots<X(m_{2})
, and

Y(m_{2}+1)<\ldots<Y(m_{2}+m_{1})
.
Let
(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})\in(\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n),\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n))

be imputations of
X
,
Y
for indices
U
and
V
,
respectively, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r_{1})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)=r_{1},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}I\left(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(i)<\min(Y_{*}^{(r_{2})}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)=r_{2}.

For any
r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and

r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, define

\displaystyle S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}=\sum_{i\in V}I\left(R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\geq 0\right),

and

\displaystyle R^{(r_{1},r_{2})}=\sum_{i\in U}I\left(p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}-1\geq 0\right),

where
q^{(r_{2})}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{2}-i+1)

and

p^{(r_{1})}_{i}=I\left(i\leq m_{1}-r_{1}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(i>m_{1}-r_{1}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
.
For any
i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)
, denote

d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)
, and for any
i\in\{-m_{2},\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
, denote
s_{i}=\sum_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(d_{l}\leq i\right)
. Denote
n^{\prime}=n-m_{1}-m_{2}
.
Then, for any
r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
,
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r_{1}+1)},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})=D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})+2s_{r_{1}-r_{2}}+n^{\prime}+2S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{2}+C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})},

(195)

and for any
r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}
,
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2}+1)})&=D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})\\
&-2s_{r_{1}-r_{2}-1}+n^{\prime}-2R^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{1}+C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})},\end{split}

(196)

where

\displaystyle C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}

\displaystyle=|r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|

\displaystyle-|n-m_{1}+r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|,

\displaystyle C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}

\displaystyle=|r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}|

\displaystyle-\left|n-m_{2}+r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}\right|.

Proof.

We first show (
195
) is true.
According to Proposition
E.7
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|,\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1}+1)},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}+1-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}+1-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|.\end{split}

In order to prove (
195
),
it is then sufficient to show the following three equations all hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}+1-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&-\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|=2S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{2},\end{split}

(197)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|\\
&-\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|=C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})},\end{split}

(198)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}+1-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|=2s_{r_{1}-r_{2}}+n^{\prime}.\end{split}

(199)

First, we show (
197
) is true.
According to Lemma
E.4
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}+1-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in V}I\left(R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\geq 0\right)+m_{2}

\displaystyle=2S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{2}.

This proves (
197
).

Next, we show (
198
) is true.
Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=m_{2}+1}^{m_{2}+m_{1}}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=m_{2}+1}^{m_{2}+m_{1}}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i-m_{2}}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i}-R\left(Y(m_{2}+i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(m_{2}+i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|.

Then, according to Lemma
E.5
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1}+1)}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|-\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=|r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|

\displaystyle-|n-m_{1}+r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|

\displaystyle=C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}.

This proves (
198
).

Next, we show (
199
) is true.
According to Lemma
E.4
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}+1-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\geq 0\right)+n-m_{1}-m_{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(d_{i}\leq r_{1}-r_{2}\right)+n^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2t_{r_{1}-r_{2}}+n^{\prime}.

This proves (
199
)
and completes our proof for (
195
).

Similarly, we can prove (
196
) is true.
According to Proposition
E.7
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|,\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2}+1)})&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2}+1)}_{i}\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}-1\right|\\
&+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-1-d_{i}\right|.\end{split}

Then, in order to prove (
196
),
it is sufficient to show the following three equations all hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2}+1)}_{i}\right|\\
&-\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|=C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})},\end{split}

(200)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}-1\right|\\
&-\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|=m_{1}-2R^{(r_{1},r_{2})},\end{split}

(201)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}-1\right|=n^{\prime}-2t_{r_{1}-r_{2}-1}.\end{split}

(202)

First, we show (
200
) is true.
Notice that

\displaystyle q^{(r_{2})}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{1}-i+1),

for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
and
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
.
Then, denote
u_{i}=i
for any
i=0,\ldots,m_{2}
. We have

\displaystyle q^{(r_{1})}_{u_{i}}=I\left(u_{i}\leq u_{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(u_{i}>u_{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{2}-i+1).

Then, according to Lemma
E.5
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2}+1)}_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-q^{(r_{2})}_{i}\right|\\
&=\sum_{i\in V}\left|q^{(r_{2}+1)}_{i}-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}\right|-\sum_{i\in V}\left|q^{(r_{2})}_{i}-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}\right|\\
&=|r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}|\\
&-\left|n-m_{2}+r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}\right|\\
&=C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}.\end{split}

This proves (
200
).

Next, we show (
201
) is true.
According to Lemma
E.4
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}-1\right|

\displaystyle-\sum_{i\in U}\left|p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|

\displaystyle=m_{1}-2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(p^{(r_{1})}_{i}-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}-1\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle=m_{1}-2R^{(r_{1},r_{2})}.

This proves (
201
).

Next, we show (
202
) is true.
According to Lemma
E.4
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|-\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}-1\right|

\displaystyle=n-m_{1}-m_{2}-2\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}-1\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle=n^{\prime}-2\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(d_{i}\leq r_{1}-r_{2}-1\right)

\displaystyle=n^{\prime}-2t_{r_{1}-r_{2}-1}.

This proves (
202
)
and completes our proof for (
196
).
Hence, we finish our proof.

∎

Algorithm 6

An efficient algorithm for computing exact upper bounds of Spearman’s Footrule under Missing Case II

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where
X
and
Y
might be
partially observed, but for any pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
where
i\in[n]
, at least one value is observed.

2:

Maximum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

Denote the number of unobserved components in
X
,
Y
as
m_{1}

and
m_{2}
, respectively. If at least
m_{1}=0
or
m_{2}=0
is true, run Algorithm
5
. If
m_{1}+m_{2}=n
, then return
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|i-(n-i+1)|
.

4:

Rank all observed in
X
and
Y
.
Relabel
X
and
Y
such that
(Y(1),\ldots,Y(m_{2}))
, and
(X(m_{2}+1),\ldots,X(m_{2}+m_{1}))
are unobserved, and
X(1)<\ldots<X(m_{2})
,
Y(m_{1})<\ldots<Y(m_{2}+m_{1})
.

5:

Let
V=\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, and
U=\{m_{2}+1,\ldots,m_{2}+m_{1}\}
.

6:

For
i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)
, let
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

7:

For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
and
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, let
q^{(r_{2})}_{i}=I\left({i}\leq{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left({i}>{m_{2}-r_{2}}\right)(m_{2}-i+1)
.

8:

For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
, let
p^{(0)}_{i}=I\left(i\leq m_{1}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(i>m_{1}\right)(m_{1}-i+1)
.

9:

Run Algorithm
4
for computing
s_{i}=\sum_{l\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}I\left(d_{l}\leq i\right)
for any
i\in\{-m_{2},\ldots,m_{1}-1\}
.

10:

Run Algorithm
4
for computing

\displaystyle R^{(0,r_{2})}=\sum_{i\in U}I\left(-p^{(0)}_{i-m_{2}}+R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)+1\leq-r_{2}\right),\text{ for any }r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}.

11:

For any given
r_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{2}\}
, run Algorithm
4
for computing

\displaystyle S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}=\sum_{i\in V}I\left(q^{(r_{2})}_{i}-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)\leq r_{1}\right),\text{ for any }r_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,m_{1}\}.

12:

Initialize

\displaystyle D_{0,0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\left|R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)+r_{1}-(n-i+1)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in U}\left|(n-i+m_{2}+1)-R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus(U\cup V)}\left|r_{1}-r_{2}-d_{i}\right|.

13:

for

r_{2}=0,\ldots,m_{2}

do

14:

for

r_{1}=0,\ldots,m_{1}-1

do

15:

Compute

\displaystyle C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}

\displaystyle=|r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|

\displaystyle-|n-m_{1}+r_{1}+1-R\left(Y(m_{2}+m_{1}-r_{1}),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-r_{2}|.

16:

Compute
D_{r_{1}+1,r_{2}}=D_{r_{1},r_{2}}+2s_{r_{1}-r_{2}}+n^{\prime}+2S^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{2}+C_{1}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}
.

17:

end

for

18:

if

r_{2}<m_{2}

then

19:

Let
r_{1}=0
and compute

\displaystyle C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}

\displaystyle=|r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}|

\displaystyle-\left|n-m_{2}+r_{2}+1-R\left(X(m_{2}-r_{2}),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right)-r_{1}\right|.

20:

Compute
D_{r_{1},r_{2}+1}=D_{r_{1},r_{2}}-2s_{r_{1}-r_{2}-1}+n^{\prime}-2R^{(r_{1},r_{2})}+m_{1}+C_{2}^{(r_{1},r_{2})}
.

21:

end

if

22:

end

for

23:

Return
\max\{D_{0,0},\ldots,D_{m_{1},m_{2}}\}
.

Remark 7
.

A few comments of Algorithm
6
are made below:

In Algorithm
6
, the initialization
D_{0,0}
computed in
line
12
equals to
D(X_{*}^{(0)},Y_{*}^{(0)})
defined in
Proposition
E.7
.
Spearman’s footrule

D_{r_{1}+1,r_{2}}
,
D_{r_{1},r_{2}+1}
,
and
D_{r_{1},r_{2}}
equal to

D(X_{*}^{(r_{1}+1)},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})
,

D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2}+1)})
,
and
D(X_{*}^{(r_{1})},Y_{*}^{(r_{2})})
,
respectively, and are updated according to
Proposition
E.8
.

By computing
\{D_{0,0},\ldots,D_{m_{1},m_{2}}\}
, Algorithm
6

finds all possible Spearman’s footrule
values
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
, where
X_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(Y;U,[n],n)
,

Y_{*}\in\mathcal{X}(X;V,[n],n)
are
imputations of
X,Y
for indices
U
, and
V
, respectively.
See discussions after Theorem 2.17 for explanations.
Hence, according to Theorem
C.12
,
the algorithm guarantees to find the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule
between
X
and
Y
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
6
is analyzed as follows.
Ranking and relabeling all observed components in
X
and
Y
in line
4

requires
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps.
Using these rankings, in line
6
computing each
d_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
and so overall line
6
is
\mathcal{O}(n-m_{1}-m_{2})
.
Line
7
and line
8
takes
\mathcal{O}(m_{2}^{2})

and
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
steps, respectively.

According to Remark
5
, the computational
complexity of running Algorithm
4
in
line
9
and line
10
is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1}+m_{2})
,
and
\mathcal{O}(m_{2})
, respectively. In line
11
, each iteration of running Algorithm
4

within the for loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
. Since the loop runs
m_{2}
times,
the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1}m_{2})
.

Line
12
requires
\mathcal{O}(n)
steps using the ranks of observed components.
Line
15
, line
16
,
line
19
, and line
20

all take
\mathcal{O}(1)
steps, and since the loop in
14

runs
m_{1}
times,
the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1})
.
Further, since the loop in
13

runs
m_{2}+1
times,
the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{1}m_{2})
. Therefore, the overall computational complexity for Algorithm
6
is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n+m_{1}m_{2}+m_{2}^{2})
.

E.3
Missing Case III

This section provides an efficient algorithm for computing
exact upper bounds of Spearman’s footrule under
missing case III, where
X
and
Y
might be
partially observed, and for any pair
(X(i),Y(i))
,
where
i\in[n]
, the two value
are either both observed, or both missing.

To start, we prove the following lemma:

Lemma E.9
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
0<m_{3}<n
,
let
W=\{n-m_{3}+1,\ldots,n\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. If
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

is such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}I\left(X^{(r)}(i)<\min(X^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=r,

then we have
Y\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
,

\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}-r
,
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(X({i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|&=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i\leq m_{3}-r\right)|n+1-2i|\right)\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i>m_{3}-r\right)|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|\right).\end{split}

(203)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume (after relabeling)

Y(n-m_{3}+1)<\ldots<Y(n)
.

Since
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
, then
we have
X\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
,
and

\displaystyle R\left(X(i),X\right)+R\left(Y(i),Y\right)=n+1,\text{ for any }i\in W.

(204)

Then, for any
i,j\in W
, we have

\displaystyle\text{ if }R\left(X(i),X\right)>R\left(X(j),X\right),\text{ then }R\left(Y(i),Y\right)<R\left(Y(j),Y\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle X(i)>X(j)\Rightarrow Y(i)>Y(j),\text{ for any }i,j\in W.

Hence, we have shown that
X\in\mathcal{X}(Y;W,[n],n)
.
Then, according to Lemma
E.1
,
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}({n-m_{3}+i}),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n-i+1,&\text{ if }r=0,\\
q^{(r)},&\text{ if }m_{3}>r>0,\\
m_{3}-i+1,&\text{ if }r=m_{3},\end{array}\right.

where
q^{(r)}=I\left({n-m_{3}+i}\leq{n-r}\right)(n-i+1)+I\left(n-m_{3}+i>n-r\right)(m_{3}-i+1)
.

In the following, we prove that the following three statements:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}):Y\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n),

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ii}):\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}-r,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{iii}):\text{ equation }\eqref{supp:proposition:alg:3:lemma:1:eqn:0.0}\text{ holds,}

are true when
r=0
,
m_{3}>r>0
and
r=m_{3}
, separately.

Suppose
r=0
. Then according to (
E.3
),
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)=n-i+1,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}.

According to (
204
), we further have

\displaystyle R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=i,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}.

Since the
m_{3}
components of
W
takes the ranks from
1
to
m_{3}
,
then we have

\displaystyle Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},\text{ for any }i\in W.

Hence, we have
Y\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)

and
\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}=m_{3}-r
.
Further, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=n-i+1-i=n+1-2i,

which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(X({i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}|n+1-2i|.

This proves our result when
r=0
.

Suppose
m_{3}>r>0
. Then according to (
E.3
),
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)=n-i+1,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}-r\},

and

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)=m_{3}-i+1,\text{ for any }i\in\{m_{3}-r+1,\ldots,m_{3}\}.

Then according to (
204
), we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=i,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}-r\},

and

\displaystyle R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=n-m_{3}+i,\text{ for any }i\in\{m_{3}-r+1,\ldots,m_{3}\},

Since the
m_{3}
components of
W
takes either the ranks from
1
to
m_{3}-r
,
or the ranks from
n-r+1
to
n
,
then we have

\displaystyle Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},\text{ or }Y(i)>\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},\text{ for any }i\in W.

Hence, we have
Y\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)

and
\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y(i)<\min(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}-r
.
Further, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}-r\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=n+1-2i,

and for any
i\in\{m_{3}-r+1,\ldots,m_{3}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=2m_{3}-2i+1-n.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(X({i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}-r}|n+1-2i|+\sum_{i=m_{3}-r+1}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|.

This proves our result when
m_{3}>r>0
.

Suppose
r=m_{3}
. Then according to (
E.3
),
we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)=m_{3}-i+1,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}.

Then according to (
204
), we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=n-m_{3}+i,\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}.

Since the
m_{3}
components of
W
takes the ranks from
n-m_{3}+1
to
n
,
then we have

\displaystyle Y(i)>\max(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W},\text{ for any }i\in W.

Hence, we have
Y\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)

and
\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y(i)<\min(Y_{*}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}-r
.
Further, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m_{3}\}
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X({n-m_{3}+i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({n-m_{3}+i}),Y\right)=2m_{3}-2i+1-n,

which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\left(R\left(X({i}),X\right)-R\left(Y({i}),Y\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|.

This proves our result when
r=m_{3}
, and completes our proof.
∎

Proposition E.10
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
0<m_{3}<n
,
let
W=\{n-m_{3}+1,\ldots,n\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Let
(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

be imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices in
W
such that

\sum_{i\in W}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=r
. For any
i\in[n]\setminus W
, denote
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus W}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus W}\right)
.
Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{3}\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|+g^{(r)},

where
g^{(r)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i\leq m_{3}-r\right)|n+1-2i|\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i>m_{3}-r\right)|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|\right)
.

Proof.

To start, according to the definition of Spearman’s footrule,
we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|.

Then, in order to prove our results, it is sufficient
to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|=g^{(r)},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|.

Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|=g^{(r)}

is true according to Lemma
E.9
.
Hence, we only need to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|.

Since
(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)
, then
we have
X_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=r,

then according to Lemma
E.2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)=R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)+r,\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus W.

Next, according to Lemma
E.9
, we have
Y_{*}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{Z}(W,[n],n)
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in W}I\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(Y_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=m_{3}-r.

Then, according to Lemma
E.2
, we have

\displaystyle R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)+m_{3}-r,\text{ for any }i\in[n]\setminus W.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\left|R\left(Y_{*}^{(r)}(i),Y_{*}^{(r)}\right)-R\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i),X_{*}^{(r)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}\left|R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)+m_{3}-2r\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|.

This completes our proof.
∎

Lemma E.11
.

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n>2
, and
m_{3}\in[n]
. For any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{3}\}
,
define

\displaystyle g^{(r)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i\leq m_{3}-r\right)|n+1-2i|\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left(I\left(i>m_{3}-r\right)|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|\right).

Then for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{3}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}=|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|.

Proof.

We prove our results when
r=0
,
m_{3}>r>0
,
and
r=m_{3}
, separately.

First, suppose
r=0
. Then we have

\displaystyle g^{(r)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}|n+1-2i|

and

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}-1}|n+1-2i|+|1-n|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}=|1-n|-|n+1-2m_{3}|.

This proves our results when
r=0
.

Next, suppose
m_{3}-1>r>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle g^{(r)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}-r}|n+1-2i|+\sum_{i=m_{3}-r+1}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|,

and

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}-r-1}|n+1-2i|+\sum_{i=m_{3}-r}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}

\displaystyle=|2m_{3}-2(m_{3}-r)+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|

\displaystyle=|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|.

This proves our results when
m_{3}-1>r>0
.

Finally, suppose
r=m_{3}-1
. Then we have

\displaystyle g^{(r)}=|n-1|+\sum_{i=2}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|,

and

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}|2m_{3}-2i+1-n|.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}=|2m_{3}-1-n|-|n-1|.

This proves our results when
r=m_{3}-1
, and completes our proof.
∎

Lemma E.12
.

Suppose
X=(X(u_{1}),\ldots,X(u_{n}))
is a vector of integers,
where
U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\}
is a set of indices such that
u_{1}<\ldots<u_{n}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)+2|-\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)|=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)+2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)+1\geq 0\right)-2n.

Proof.

To start,
for any
i\in U
, we have

\displaystyle|X(i)+2|-|X(i)|

\displaystyle=2,\text{ if }X(i)\geq 0,

\displaystyle|X(i)+2|-|X(i)|

\displaystyle=0,\text{ if }X(i)=-1.

If, however
X(i)<-1
, then since

X
is a vector of integers, we
have
X(i)+2\leq 0
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle|X(i)+2|-|X(i)|

\displaystyle=-2,\text{ if }X(i)<-1.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)+2|-\sum_{i\in U}|X(i)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}\left(|X(i)+2|-|X(i)-Y(i)|\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)<-1\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)-2\left(n-\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)\geq 0\right)+2\sum_{i\in U}I\left(X(i)+1\geq 0\right)-2n.

This completes our proof.
∎

Proposition E.13
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
and for
0<m_{3}<n
,
let
W=\{n-m_{3}+1,\ldots,n\}\subset[n]

be a subset of indices. Let
(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

be imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices in
W
such that

\sum_{i\in W}I\left(X_{*}^{(r)}(i)<\min(X_{*}^{(r)}(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus W}\right)=r
. For any
i\in[n]\setminus W
, let
d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus W}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(j))_{j\in[n]\setminus W}\right)
.
For any
i\in\{-m_{3},\ldots,m_{3}-1\}
, let
s_{i}=\sum_{l\in[n]\setminus W}I\left(d_{l}\leq i\right)
.
Then, for any
r\in\{0,\ldots,m_{3}-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y_{*}^{(r+1)})

\displaystyle=D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})+2(s_{2r+1-m_{3}}+s_{2r-m_{3}}-n+m_{3})

\displaystyle+|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|.

Proof.

To start,
according to Proposition
E.10
, we have

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|+g^{(r)},

\displaystyle\text{and }D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y_{*}^{(r+1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|+g^{(r+1)}.

Hence,

\displaystyle D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y_{*}^{(r+1)})-D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|+g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}.

Therefore, in order to prove our result,
it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}=|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|,

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|=2(s_{2r-m_{3}}+s_{2r-m_{3}+1}-n-m_{3}).

Notice that according to Lemma
E.11
,

\displaystyle g^{(r+1)}-g^{(r)}=|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|

is true.
Hence we only need to show

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|=2(s_{2r+1-m_{3}}+s_{2r-m_{3}}-n+m_{3})

is true.

According to Lemma
E.12
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|

\displaystyle=2(n-m_{3})-2\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2\geq 0\right)-2\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2+1\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle=2(n-m_{3})-2\left(n-m_{3}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2<0\right)\right)

\displaystyle-2\left(n-m_{3}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-1<0\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}<2r+2-m_{3}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}I\left(d_{i}<2r+1-m_{3}\right)-n+m_{3}\right).

Since
r,m_{3}
are integers and for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-m_{3}\}
,
d_{i}
is also an integer, then we have

\displaystyle I\left(d_{i}<2r+2-m_{3}\right)=I\left(d_{i}\leq 2r+1-m_{3}\right)

and

\displaystyle I\left(d_{i}<2r+1-m_{3}\right)=I\left(d_{i}\leq 2r-m_{3}\right).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r-2|-\sum_{i=1}^{n-m_{3}}|d_{i}+m_{3}-2r|=2(s_{2r+1-m_{3}}+s_{2r-m_{3}}-n+m_{3}).

This completes our proof.
∎

Algorithm 7

An efficient algorithm for computing exact upper bounds of Spearman’s Footrule under Missing Case III

1:

X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{n}
, where
X
and
Y
might be
partially observed, and for any pair
(X_{i},Y_{i})
,
where
i\in[n]
, the two values
are either both observed, or both missing.

2:

Maximum possible Spearman’s footrule distance between
X
and
Y
.

3:

Denote
m_{3}
as the number of components such that
both values
(X(i),Y(i))

are missing. If
m_{3}=n
, then return
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|i-(n-i+1)|
.

4:

Rank all observed data in
X
and
Y
. Relabel
X
and
Y
such that

(X(n-m_{3}+1),Y(n-m_{3}+1)),\ldots,(X(n),Y(n))
are missing.

5:

Let
W=\{n-m_{3}+1,\ldots,n\}
, and for any
i\in[n]\setminus W
, let

\displaystyle d_{i}=R\left(Y(i),(Y(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus V}\right)-R\left(X(i),(X(l))_{l\in[n]\setminus U}\right).

6:

Run Algorithm
4
for computing
s_{i}=\sum_{l\in[n]\setminus W}I\left(d_{l}\leq i\right)
for any
i\in\{-m_{3},\ldots,m_{3}-1\}
.

7:

Initialize
D_{0}
=
\sum_{i=1}^{m_{3}}\left|n+1-2i\right|+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus W}|d_{i}|
.

8:

for

r=0,\ldots,m_{3}-1

do

9:

Compute

\displaystyle D_{r+1}

\displaystyle=D_{r}+2(s_{2r+1-m_{3}}+s_{2r-m_{3}}-n+m_{3})

\displaystyle+|2r+1-n|-|n+1-2(m_{3}-r)|.

10:

end

for

11:

Return
\max\{D_{0},\ldots,D_{m_{3}}\}
.

Remark 8
.

A few comments of Algorithm
7
are made below:

In Algorithm
7
, the initialization
D_{0}
computed in
line
7
equals to
D(X_{*}^{(0)},Y_{*}^{(0)})
defined in
Proposition
E.10
.
Spearman’s footrule

D_{r+1}
, and

D_{r}
equal to

D(X_{*}^{(r+1)},Y_{*}^{(r)})
,

D(X_{*}^{(r)},Y_{*}^{(r)})
,
respectively, and are updated according to
Proposition
E.13
.

By computing
\{D_{0},\ldots,D_{m_{3}}\}
, Algorithm
7

finds all possible Spearman’s footrule
values
D\left(X_{*},Y_{*}\right)
, where
(X_{*},Y_{*})\in\mathcal{Y}(W,n)

are imputations of
X
and
Y
for indices
W
.
See discussions following Theorem 2.20 in the main paper
for explanations.
Hence, according to Theorem
C.17
,
the algorithm guarantees to find the maximum possible Spearman’s footrule
between
X
and
Y
.

The computational complexity of Algorithm
7
is analyzed as follows. Ranking and relabeling all observed components in
X
and
Y
in line
4

requires
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
steps. Using these rankings, in line
5
computing each
d_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
and so overall line
5
is
\mathcal{O}(n-m_{3})
.
According to Remark
5
, the computational
complexity of running Algorithm
4
in
line
6
is
\mathcal{O}(n+m_{3})
.
Line
7
requires
\mathcal{O}(n)
steps.
In line
9
, each iteration of the for loop is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and since the loop runs
m_{3}
times,
the computational complexity for the loop is
\mathcal{O}(m_{3})
.
Therefore, the overall computational complexity for Algorithm
7

is
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
.

E.4
General Missing Case

This subsection provides an efficient algorithm for computing
